## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/DiskSpaceGuard.cs

### DiskSpaceGuard (line 4)

Pre-flight free-space check for the render pipelines.

WHY: an export writes the encode to `%TMP%\...\core.mp4` and then COPIES it to the
output folder, so at peak the machine needs roughly 2x the output size (plus headroom for
the muxer and the thumbnail). Without this check a full drive surfaces to the user as a raw
"FFmpeg exited with code 1" after a ten-minute encode. Checking up front costs nothing and
turns it into an actionable message before any work starts.

### DiskSpaceGuard.SafetyMarginBytes (line 15)

Extra breathing room on top of the raw estimate (muxer, thumbnail, fragmentation).

### DiskSpaceGuard.Check (line 24)

Verifies both the staging drive and the destination drive can hold the job.
When temp and output live on the SAME volume the requirement is summed, because both
copies coexist on that one volume at the moment of the final `File.Copy`.

**tempDirectory:** Where the encode is staged.
**outputDirectory:** Where the finished file lands.
**estimatedOutputBytes:** Best estimate of the finished file size.

### DiskSpaceGuard.EstimateOutputBytes (line 111)

Rough finished-size estimate used by the pre-flight check.
Uses the explicit size target when the user locked one, otherwise derives it from the
chosen video bitrate; falls back to a deliberately generous 15 Mbit/s guess so the guard
still catches an almost-full drive when quality is CRF-driven.

### DiskSpaceGuard.TwoPassMasterKbps (line 126)

T01 — bitrate the near-lossless two-pass master is budgeted at, in kbit/s.

The master is CRF-driven (see `ProcessWorker.Stage1CodecArgs`), so its real size is not
knowable in advance. 32 Mbit/s is a deliberately GENEROUS flat estimate for 1080p60 /
1080x1920 CRF 14 content — over-estimating here only means we occasionally decline the fast
path on a nearly-full drive, which is the safe direction to be wrong in.

### DiskSpaceGuard.EstimateTwoPassMasterBytes (line 136)

T01 — size the temporary two-pass master is expected to occupy.

### DiskSpaceGuard.HasRoomFor (line 145)

T01 — NON-FATAL probe: can the staging volume additionally hold `extraBytes`?

Distinct from `Check` ON PURPOSE. Check ABORTS the export with a message; this
one only answers a yes/no question used to decide whether the fast (scratch-master) two-pass
route is affordable. A "no" must never fail the export — the caller falls back to the slower
route that runs the filter graph twice and needs no extra disk. Any probe error returns true
so a machine we cannot measure is never downgraded.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/GracefulProcessTerminator.cs

### GracefulProcessTerminator (line 10)

Cooperative, strictly bounded shutdown ladder for subordinate native processes
(FFmpeg, FFprobe, wevtutil, ...).

PROBLEM this solves: calling `Process.Kill(entireProcessTree: true)` the instant
cancellation or a timeout fires terminates the child mid-write, which can leave orphaned
grandchild processes, corrupted half-written output files, and unreleased file locks on disk.

ESCALATION LADDER (every step is time-bounded, so a stuck child can never hang a caller):
  1. Already exited → return immediately.
  2. CLEAN STOP — when the child's stdin is redirected, write FFmpeg's interactive quit
     command ('q') and flush. An interactive encoder then finalizes its output (moov
     trailer, indexes) and exits on its own.
  3. Await a voluntary exit for `CooperativeGraceMs` (default 1500 ms).
  4. ESCALATE — `Process.Kill(entireProcessTree: true)`, then await exit
     confirmation for `HardKillConfirmMs` (default 2000 ms).

The Windows Job Object assigned by `ChildProcessTracker` is untouched and
remains the ULTIMATE safeguard against orphaned processes: if even the hard kill fails,
the job object tears the whole tree down when this application exits.

### GracefulProcessTerminator.CooperativeGraceMs (line 33)

Cooperative grace period: how long the child gets to react to the quit command.

### GracefulProcessTerminator.HardKillConfirmMs (line 36)

Fallback limit: how long to await exit confirmation after a hard kill.

### GracefulProcessTerminator.TerminateAsync (line 39)

Async variant of the escalation ladder. Never throws — every failure is swallowed and
logged, because shutdown paths must not surface new exceptions at the caller.

**process:** The subordinate process to stop. May be null (no-op).
**logTag:** Tag used for CoreLogger lines.
**attemptQuitCommand:**
Write FFmpeg's 'q' quit command when (and only when) the child's stdin is redirected.
Pass false for non-interactive tools that would only choke on stray stdin.

**cooperativeGraceMs:** Grace period before escalating (default 1500 ms).
**hardKillConfirmMs:** Exit-confirmation budget after the kill (default 2000 ms).

### GracefulProcessTerminator.Terminate (line 95)

Synchronous, equally bounded variant of the ladder for teardown paths that cannot
await (e.g. `IDisposable.Dispose`). Worst case it blocks for
`cooperativeGraceMs` + `hardKillConfirmMs`.

### GracefulProcessTerminator.TryWriteQuitCommand (line 144)

Clean stop signal: writes FFmpeg's interactive quit command ('q') to the child's
redirected stdin and flushes. No-op (returns false) when stdin is not redirected or
the process is already gone; never throws.

FASTINSTALL_01 — a second name for an existing file on the same NTFS volume. Instant, and it uses

and its own staging area under Program Files): a link shares the bytes, so it must never point at
a file a normal user can still write. Anything that fails (other volume, FAT, non-Windows) returns

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/LegacyResidueSweep.cs

### LegacyResidueSweep — REBRAND_02, LEGACYKEEP_01, REBRAND_01, UPGRADE_04, RESCUE_01 (line 10)

REBRAND_02 / LEGACYKEEP_01 — rescues previous-brand renders while preserving legacy user data.

REBRAND_01 (`AppDataPaths`) and UPGRADE_04 (`UserDataUpgrade`) migrate
the data; neither deletes the copy-only sources. This sweep is the last step and runs on every
normal UI launch (never inside a deployment helper, never with the development override):

  - **Legacy temp roots** (`%TEMP%\<legacy temp names>`) may contain referenced user media.
  Rescued renders (`*-RECOVERED-*.mp4`, RESCUE_01) are user work and are MOVED into the
  current temp root first, renamed to `OutputFileNaming.MainRecoveredPrefix`
  (Merger rescues keep `OutputFileNaming.MergerRecoveredPrefix`). All remaining files are retained (LEGACYKEEP_01).
  - **Legacy per-user roots** under %LOCALAPPDATA% and %APPDATA% are retained automatically, including conflicting versions and paths referenced by old projects.
  Only an explicit uninstall or manual cleanup may remove them (LEGACYKEEP_01).

⚠️ SAFETY GATES — the sweep does nothing at all while any of these hold:
a legacy installation is still present in Program Files (a rolled-back update brings the old
app back, and it needs its own folders); a legacy process is running; or a user-data upgrade
transaction (`FreeVideoStudioMigration` journal) is not yet committed — that transaction
owns the legacy roots and moves them itself. The shared machine root under %ProgramData% is
NEVER touched here (other Windows accounts may not have migrated yet); the elevated uninstaller
removes it (DeploymentFootprint.GetDirectoryPurgeTargets).

TOTAL BY CONTRACT: never throws; every failure is logged and retried on the next launch.

### LegacyResidueSweep.SweepResult (line 41)

Result of one sweep, for the log and for tests.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/MergerAutosaveStore.cs

### MergerAutosaveStore — MERGESESSION_01, WRITEORDER_01 (line 9)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
MERGESESSION_01 — THE VIDEO MERGER'S WRITE-BEHIND AUTOSAVE (Video-Merger-Migration.md P3.2, D6).

Every edit calls `Schedule` (cheap, UI thread). The newest edit list is written
`DefaultDebounce` after the LAST edit, on the thread pool, atomically
(`AtomicJsonFile.WriteText`). A burst of edits costs one write.

ORDER (WRITEORDER_01 style): every Schedule/Clear takes a number from one process-wide counter
at call time; a write or delete is applied only if its number is newer than the last one applied
to the file. A slow write queued before a Clear can therefore never resurrect the file.

`Load` never throws: a missing, locked or corrupt file is simply "nothing to restore".
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### MergerAutosaveStore.Schedule (line 48)

Queues `edl` for writing. An empty edit list means "nothing to restore" and clears the file.

### MergerAutosaveStore.FlushAsync (line 60)

Writes anything pending now (still off the caller's thread). The task completes when the write is done.

### MergerAutosaveStore.Clear (line 67)

Deletes the autosave (ordered after every earlier Schedule).

### MergerAutosaveStore.Load (line 87)

The saved edit list, or null. Never throws. Call off the UI thread.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/NamedSystemMutex.cs

### NamedSystemMutex.UserScopedName — USERSCOPE_01 (line 28)

USERSCOPE_01 — a machine-wide ("Global\") name that is private to the current Windows user.
It stays Global so the same user's processes in different sessions (console + RDP) still
exclude each other over the per-user data root, while other accounts can never collide
with it or be denied by its DACL.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/ProcessArgs.cs

### ProcessArgs — ISSUE_07 (line 7)

ISSUE_07 — shared helper for the ONE remaining legitimate use of a joined command string:
writing a copy-pasteable line into the DEBUG log.

BACKGROUND. Every FFmpeg/FFprobe invocation in this codebase must be launched through
`ProcessStartInfo.ArgumentList`, never through a hand-assembled `Arguments` string.
The old hand-built strings only wrapped an argument in quotes when it contained a SPACE, so:
  * a path containing a double-quote broke the command line outright, and
  * a path ending in a backslash escaped its own closing quote and swallowed the next argument.
Either way the user got an inscrutable FFmpeg error with no clue that their FOLDER NAME was
the cause. `ArgumentList` applies the real Windows CreateProcess escaping rules per
argument and removes the whole class of bug.

The formatted string produced here is for HUMAN EYES ONLY. It must never be fed to
`ProcessStartInfo.Arguments`.

### ProcessArgs.FormatForLog (line 25)

Renders an argument list as a readable, copy-pasteable command line for the debug log.
NEVER use the result to launch a process.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/ProcessImage.cs

### ProcessImage — NOMEMREAD_01 (line 7)

NOMEMREAD_01 — which program file another process runs, asked the way Task Manager asks.

WHAT WAS WRONG: `Process.MainModule` opens the target with
`PROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_VM_READ` and walks its loaded-module list, i.e. it READS
THE OTHER PROCESS'S MEMORY. The elevated uninstaller did that for EVERY process on the machine —
antivirus services and `lsass.exe` included. An elevated, unknown program opening lsass with
memory-read rights is the textbook signature of credential-dumping tools (MITRE T1003.001), and
endpoint protection products alert on it and may quarantine the program that did it.

THE FIX: `OpenProcess(PROCESS_QUERY_LIMITED_INFORMATION)` + `QueryFullProcessImageNameW`.
No memory is read; protected processes even allow this right. Callers only ever ask about
processes whose NAME is one of ours (05 SYS-AVCLEAN).

### ProcessImage.GetPath (line 41)

The full path of the program `processId` runs.
Throws `Win32Exception` when Windows refuses (another account, access denied) and
`InvalidOperationException` when the process no longer exists — the same two
exceptions `Process.MainModule` threw, so callers keep their handling.

### ProcessImage.TryGetPath (line 72)

`GetPath`, or null when it cannot be read for any reason.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/PublisherRoot.cs

### PublisherRoot — UPDATEPIN_01, SIGNLOCAL_01, UPDATETRUST_02 (line 6)

UPDATEPIN_01 — THE PUBLISHER ROOT, BUILT INTO THE APP.

Releases are signed with the repository's own certificate (ssl-certificate\, SIGNLOCAL_01),
which chains to a PRIVATE root. No other PC trusts that root, so the old rule — "the running
build must chain to a root Windows trusts" — refused every in-app update on every user's PC
(UPDATETRUST_02) and sent them to download 296 MB by hand.

The fix needs no paid certificate: the root's PUBLIC certificate is compiled into the app.
An update is trusted when its Authenticode signature is intact AND its signer chains to THIS root
(custom trust, code-signing EKU). That is a stronger statement than "some public CA vouches for
someone": only the holder of this root's key can produce a file that passes. The same anchor
verifies the signed update manifest (`SmartUpdateManifest`).

⚠️ NEVER REGENERATE THE ROOT casually. A new root means every installed copy refuses every
future update until the user reinstalls by hand. ssl-certificate\create-dev-codesign-cert.ps1
makes a new root; FvsBuild refuses to sign with a certificate that does not chain to the root
below. A new LEAF from the same root (the leaf expires 2029-09-24) is fine.

Windows' own "unknown publisher" warning on the very FIRST download is not affected: that
needs a publicly trusted certificate, which only money buys.

### PublisherRoot.Sha256Thumbprint (line 30)

SHA-256 fingerprint of the root below (CN=FVS Local Development Root CA, valid to 2036-09-22).

### PublisherRoot.RevokedSignerThumbprints — REVOKE_01 (line 35)

REVOKE_01 — publisher LEAF certificates (SHA-256 thumbprints) withdrawn by the publisher, compiled
in. A leaked leaf key is switched off by (1) issuing a new leaf from the same root, (2) adding the
old thumbprint to `build/revoked-signers.txt` AND here, (3) releasing. Every copy that installs
that release refuses the old leaf for good, and every copy that only DOWNLOADS its signed list
learns it too (`SmartUpdateManifest.RevokedSigners` → `AddRevoked`).
⚠️ Must equal `build/revoked-signers.txt` (PublisherRootTests.CompiledRevocationListMatchesTheBuildList).

### PublisherRoot.AddRevoked — REVOKE_01 (line 48)

REVOKE_01 — adds thumbprints learned at run time (a signed list, or the per-user store).

### PublisherRoot.IsRevoked — REVOKE_01 (line 62)

REVOKE_01 — true when the publisher withdrew this signer (compiled list or learned at run time).

### PublisherRoot.RuntimeRevokedSnapshot — REVOKE_01 (line 70)

The run-time revocations, for persisting them (REVOKE_01).

### PublisherRoot.IsThumbprint (line 76)

A SHA-256 thumbprint: 64 hexadecimal characters.

### PublisherRoot.Load (line 114)

The pinned root certificate (public part only).

### PublisherRoot.Vouches — SIGNMANDATE_01 (line 117)

True when `signer` chains to the pinned root and may sign code. Time
validity is not required: Authenticode signatures are RFC-3161 timestamped, so a file signed
while the leaf was valid stays valid after it expires (SIGNMANDATE_01).

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/RecoveryManager.cs

### RecoveryManager.SchemaVersion — ISSUE_06 (line 9)

Shape version of `recovery_v2.json`.

ISSUE_06 — this did not exist, and THAT was the bug. `LoadState` threw
"Missing schema_version in recovery state" whenever the key was absent, but NOTHING ever
wrote the key: MainWindow.SaveRecoveryState builds the payload by hand and stamps 59 keys,
none of them `schema_version`. So the throw fired on every single restore, was
swallowed by the catch below, and LoadState returned null every time — meaning the
"restore your previous work?" prompt could never restore anything. The stamp is now applied
centrally in `SaveState` so every writer gets it automatically and no caller
has to remember.

Bump ONLY when the payload shape changes incompatibly.

### RecoveryManager.TryClaimVersionLocked (line 34)

Caller holds _saveLock. True when `version` is newer than anything applied to the file.

### RecoveryManager.CrashedRestoreAttempts — RESTORECRASH_01 (line 48)

RESTORECRASH_01 — consecutive restore attempts that died with their owner (0 = none).

### RecoveryManager.RestoreCrashDetected — RESTORECRASH_01 (line 51)

RESTORECRASH_01 — true when the previous launch died while restoring the snapshot.

### RecoveryManager.CanRetryRestore — RESTORECRASH_01 (line 54)

RESTORECRASH_01 — false once the retry budget is spent or this process already tried.

### RecoveryManager.QuarantinedSnapshotFile — RESTORECRASH_01 (line 57)

RESTORECRASH_01 — where the last snapshot moved aside by `QuarantineSnapshot` is kept,
byte for byte. Each quarantine gets a unique name so an earlier kept copy is never overwritten.

### RecoveryManager.MarkCleanShutdownIntent — RECOVERY_02 (line 68)

RECOVERY_02 — records that the user is closing the app ON PURPOSE.

MUST be called synchronously at the very top of the close handler, BEFORE any await. The
close path is asynchronous (save window bounds, stop mpv, dispose the host) and during a
Windows shutdown / restart / sign-out the OS can terminate the process partway through it.
The session lock then survives and the next launch reports a crash that never happened.
This marker is the evidence that the exit was intentional, written while we still certainly
have a thread to write it with.

Non-throwing by contract: it runs inside a close handler, and a failure here must never
prevent the app from closing.

### RecoveryManager.IsSafeModeActive — RECOVERY_05 (line 185)

RECOVERY_05 — is a restore currently in flight (or did one die mid-flight)?

OWNERSHIP FIRST, AGE ONLY AS A BACKSTOP. This used to expire the sentinel purely on
wall-clock age (120 seconds). A restore of a large project on a slow disk can legitimately
take longer than that, and when it did the sentinel was deleted MID-RESTORE — so a crash
during that restore was no longer recognised as a crash loop, which is precisely the
scenario safe mode exists to break, arriving exactly when the session is heaviest.

The sentinel now records the owning process the same way the session lock does
(PID + process start ticks, which together defeat PID reuse). It is honoured for as long as
that process is genuinely alive, however long the restore takes. It is considered stale —
and cleaned up — only when the owner is gone. The age check survives purely as a backstop
for a sentinel written by an older build that carries no owner stamp.

### RecoveryManager.ParseRestoreAttempt — RESTORECRASH_01 (line 233)

RESTORECRASH_01 — the attempt number in "pid:startTicks:attempt"; older stamps count as attempt 1.

### RecoveryManager.TryParseOwner (line 240)

Parses a "pid:startTicks" owner stamp. Shared by the lock and the safe-mode sentinel.

### RecoveryManager.IsProcessStillRunning (line 251)

True when the stamped process is still alive AND is genuinely the same process — the start
ticks are what stop a recycled PID from impersonating the previous owner.

### RecoveryManager.ActivateSafeMode — RESTORECRASH_01 (line 280)

Marks this process as the restore owner. RESTORECRASH_01: returns false (and restores
nothing) once the retry budget is spent or this process already attempted a restore, so a
crashing snapshot can never be retried automatically or endlessly.

### RecoveryManager.DeactivateSafeMode (line 309)

Clears the safe-mode sentinel. Called after a recovery restore completes
successfully so that genuine later crashes in this session remain recoverable.

### RecoveryManager.ReleaseLockOnly (line 374)

Deletes only the session lock file without clearing recovery state.
Use during intentional process handoffs (Main → Merger/CropTool) so
CheckFault() returns false on restart, but recovery state is preserved
for genuine crash detection.

### RecoveryManager.UpdateGranularSession — GRANULARPERF_01 (line 399)

GRANULARPERF_01 — atomic editor-node update under the same gate as app-level saves.

### RecoveryManager.ReserveVersionForTests — WRITEORDER_01 (line 414)

WRITEORDER_01 test seam: reserve a version now, apply the save later (what SaveStateAsync does).

### RecoveryManager.WriteLocked — RECOVERY_03 (line 432)

Caller holds _saveLock. The RECOVERY_03-preserving atomic write.

### RecoveryManager.QuarantineSnapshot — RESTORECRASH_01, RESTOREFAIL_01, WRITEORDER_01 (line 522)

RESTORECRASH_01 — the retry budget is spent, or a restore failed without crashing
(RESTOREFAIL_01). Moves the snapshot aside INTACT (one atomic
rename, sequenced like every other whole-file write by WRITEORDER_01) so the crash loop
stops without destroying the user's work. Returns false when there was nothing to move.

### RecoveryManager.DiscardRecoveryState — RESTORECRASH_01 (line 548)

RESTORECRASH_01 — explicit user Start Fresh: the ONLY path that discards a crashed-restore snapshot.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/ReleaseChangelog.cs

### ReleaseChangelog — RELNOTES_01 (line 7)

RELNOTES_01 — the ONE parser for both release logs (09 DIST-RELEASENOTES):

- `docs/CHANGELOG.md` — what USERS read. Agents add plain-English bullets under
  `## Upcoming`; the official release turns that heading into `## v<version> — <date>`,
  publishes its bullets as the GitHub release notes (which the in-app update card shows) and keeps
  the latest `PublicReleasesKept` releases.
- `docs/changes.md` — what ENGINEERS read. Agents log every technical change under
  `## Unreleased`; the release promotes it the same way and keeps `TechnicalReleasesKept`.

⚠️ WHY A FIXED HEADING. The staging heading used to be a GUESSED version
(`## v2026.10.16.1230 (Upcoming)`) while the build invents its version from the clock at
build time. The publisher searched for the real version, never found it, and every release went
out with a one-line placeholder instead of its notes. A heading that never contains a version
cannot disagree with the version.

### ReleaseChangelog.TechnicalReleasesKept (line 35)

Released sections kept in `docs/changes.md`.

A release a person can read in one glance. More bullets fail the release lint.

Longest single user-facing bullet, in characters.

One `## ` section: its heading line and every line under it.

True for `## v<version>…` headings.

### ReleaseChangelog.Section.Text (line 67)

Everything above the first `## ` heading (title and introduction).

### ReleaseChangelog.Sections (line 83)

Released sections (`## v…`) in file order, newest first by convention.

RELNOTES_01 — turns the staging section `pendingTitle` into the release

The whole document, with the line endings it was read with.

RELNOTES_02 — the release lint for USER-FACING notes. Every problem is one plain sentence; an
empty list means the bullets are fit to publish. Catches what slipped into the old changelog:
engineering tags (`NODROPPER_01`), file names, code (`Class.Method`, backticks),
over-long bullets, the same headline twice and a release nobody can read in one sitting.

RELNOTES_03 — up to `max` bullets of release-notes markdown as plain text, for

is reduced to that headline, so four highlights fit a small card.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/RuntimePayloadManifest.cs

### RuntimePayloadManifest — UPDATETRUST_02 (line 11)

SYS-PAYLOADSPLIT — WHAT THE INSTALLED RUNTIME ACTUALLY IS, SO A PATCH DOES NOT HAVE TO RESHIP IT.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
**THE PROBLEM THIS EXISTS TO MEASURE.** The product ships as one 322 MB executable: a
NativeAOT installer with `payload.zip` embedded as a managed resource, which it extracts on
first run. The payload is FFmpeg and libmpv — `avcodec-62.dll` alone is 97 MB — plus the
starter media. The application's own code is a small fraction of that.

⚠️ **AND THE AUTO-UPDATER DOWNLOADS ALL OF IT, EVERY TIME.** `UpdateService` fetches the
single `FreeVideoStudio.exe` release asset and reinstalls. So a one-line typo fix
costs every user 322 MB over a 30-minute timeout — for a set of codec DLLs that have not changed
since June and will not change in this release either. On a metered connection that is a reason
to stop updating, which turns every shipped fix into a fix most users never receive.

**WHAT THIS CLASS DOES.** It records a fingerprint of the runtime payload — the binaries, not
the app — next to the installed files. The build writes one into the payload; the updater
compares the installed fingerprint against the one the release advertises. Equal means the
runtime is already correct on this machine and only the application needs to come down.

⚠️ **NAMES AND SIZES, NOT CONTENT HASHES.** Hashing 368 MB of DLLs on every startup to
decide whether to check for an update would be a worse bug than the one being fixed. Name plus
exact byte length over the whole set is enough to notice a version change — FFmpeg builds do not
coincidentally keep every file at exactly the same length — and it costs a directory listing.
The downloaded package is still verified by SHA-256 before it is trusted (UPDATETRUST_02); this
fingerprint decides WHAT to download, never whether to trust it.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
SYS-PAYLOADSPLIT — content fingerprint of files a compact installer reuses.
Computed at packaging time; update checks read the stored fingerprint. Before selecting
a compact package, the updater separately verifies installed bytes against their manifest.
Application executables and manifests are excluded; runtime binaries and starter assets
are included. The fingerprint selects a download and never replaces signature verification.

### RuntimePayloadManifest.FileName (line 54)

The file the build writes into the payload and the installer lays down beside the app.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/SmartUpdateManifest.cs

### SmartUpdateManifest — BINDELTA_01 (line 10)

DIST-SMART — the SIGNED list of every file in a release, so an update downloads only what changed.

Published as `FileName` beside a detached signature `SignatureFileName`.
It carries the release's own install manifest (path, length, SHA-256 of every installed file),
the compressed, content-addressed asset that holds each file, and binary patches for the main
program from recent versions (BINDELTA_01).

⚠️ FILES ARE MATCHED BY CONTENT, NEVER BY NAME. A file is reused only when an installed file
has the same SHA-256; the same name with different bytes is downloaded. Everything downloaded is
re-hashed against this manifest, and the manifest itself is only believed when its signature
chains to the pinned publisher root (`PublisherRoot`). The elevated installer verifies
the signature again itself before it copies a single byte.

### SmartUpdateManifest.InstallManifest — UPGRADE_06 (line 33)

The exact text of the release's install.manifest.json (UPGRADE_06).

### SmartUpdateManifest.FullInstaller (line 39)

The full installer, for any machine the smart path cannot serve.

### SmartUpdateManifest.RevokedSigners — REVOKE_01 (line 42)

REVOKE_01 — publisher leaf thumbprints (SHA-256) the publisher has withdrawn, copied from
`build/revoked-signers.txt` into every signed list. A client that verifies this list
remembers them (`PublisherRoot.AddRevoked`) and refuses those leaves from then on.
Older clients ignore the field (unknown JSON members are skipped).

### SmartUpdateManifest.Parse (line 57)

Parses and checks internal consistency (every installed file has exactly one asset).

### SmartUpdateManifest.Plan — BINDELTA_01 (line 86)

DIST-SMART — WHAT TO DOWNLOAD, decided by CONTENT ONLY. A file is skipped only when some
installed file already has exactly its SHA-256; a file with the same name but different bytes
is downloaded. The main program is rebuilt from a patch when one was made from exactly the
installed program's SHA-256 (BINDELTA_01); otherwise it is downloaded whole.

### SmartUpdateManifest.Sign (line 112)

Signs `json` with the publisher certificate (build side).

### SmartUpdateManifest.VerifySignature (line 124)

True when `json` was signed by the publisher: the signer chains to the pinned
root, or (for a publicly trusted certificate) is exactly `alsoTrustThumbprint`
— the certificate that signed the running program — and chains to a root Windows trusts.

### SmartUpdateManifest.VerifySignature — SIGNEXPIRY_01 (line 132)

SIGNEXPIRY_01 — as above; with `requireCurrentSigner` the signing leaf must also be
inside its validity period at `now`. The list signature is a plain RSA signature
with NO timestamp, so ignoring expiry would let a leaf key sign lists forever. The in-app updater
(a list fetched from GitHub NOW) requires it; the elevated worker and an installer's packed list,
which may legitimately be run years after it was signed, do not.

### SmartPlan (line 202)

The result of `SmartUpdateManifest.Plan`.

### SmartPlan.DownloadBytes (line 205)

Bytes on the wire: the compressed files plus the patch.

### SmartPlan.ChangedFiles (line 208)

How many installed files change.

### SmartFile.AssetSha256 — ASSETHASH_01 (line 220)

ASSETHASH_01 — SHA-256 of the compressed asset itself (the bytes on the wire). The downloaded
asset is checked against it BEFORE it is decompressed, so a tampered asset is rejected before
it can expand on disk. Empty in lists written before this field existed; the client then
relies on the size-capped decompression and the hash of the result.

### SmartPatch.AssetSha256 — ASSETHASH_01 (line 237)

ASSETHASH_01 — SHA-256 of the patch file; checked before the patch is applied.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Infrastructure/UiStateStore.cs

### UiStateStore — ISSUE_09, STORAGEPATHS_01 (line 4)

ISSUE_09 — the one accessor for small per-user UI state files (onboarding counters,
dismissed hints, etc.).

Everything lives under `ApplicationPaths.UiStateDirectory`
alongside settings.json and session_state.json. STORAGEPATHS_01 puts development
preferences in the original OS TEMP folder; production preferences stay in LocalAppData.

Files previously written to `%APPDATA%\FreeVideoStudio\Settings` are migrated
across automatically the first time this class is touched.

Every method is total — a locked or unreadable file returns the caller's default rather
than throwing. UI state is never important enough to fail an operation over.

### UiStateStore.MigrateLegacyFilesOnce (line 25)

One-time move of the legacy %APPDATA% files into the consolidated root. Idempotent and
safe to call from any thread; runs at most once per process.

### UiStateStore.ReadText (line 84)

Reads a small text value. Returns `fallback` on any problem.

### UiStateStore.WriteText — ATOMICSTATE_01 (line 99)

Writes a small text value. Silently no-ops on failure.

ATOMICSTATE_01 — this used `File.WriteAllText`, which opens the target with
truncation: from the moment it opens until the last byte lands, the file on disk is
SHORTER than the document it is replacing, and it returns when the bytes reach the OS
cache rather than the platter. A crash, a forced kill or a power loss inside that window
leaves a correctly named but truncated or zero-length file — the exact failure
`MemeDimensionCache` documents. Routing through
`AtomicJsonFile.WriteText` gives this the same three-step guarantee
`docs/05_SYSTEM_LIFECYCLE_STORAGE.md#SYS-RECOVERY` mandates everywhere else:
unique GUID temp file in the TARGET directory opened WriteThrough, flush-to-disk, then an
atomic same-volume rename. A reader therefore only ever sees the whole old document or
the whole new one.

The no-op-on-failure contract is unchanged: `AtomicJsonFile.WriteText` throws
on a failed write and the catch below still swallows it into a log line.

### UiStateStore.ReadInt (line 129)

Reads an integer counter, returning `fallback` when absent or malformed.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Ipc/CropConfigDefaults.cs

### CropConfigDefaults.SchemaVersion (line 9)

v4 (IDEA_1) added the optional "crops_source" section — see `SourceCropsSection`.
It is purely additive: a v3 file loads unchanged and the source rects are derived once.

### CropConfigDefaults.MinimumUsableSchemaVersion (line 15)

The OLDEST schema this build can still read and upgrade in place.

CRITICAL — `CropConfigStore.IsUsableConfig` used to compare against
`SchemaVersion` itself, meaning any config written by an older build was
declared unusable and REPLACED with factory defaults. Bumping the version to 4 for IDEA_1
would therefore have destroyed every user's saved masks on first launch, and every future
bump would do the same.

3 is the first version that stored crops in content space, so anything from 3 upward can be
read as-is and upgraded by HudConfig.Sanitize. Only raise this if a change genuinely makes
older data unreadable — and if you do, migrate rather than discard.

### CropConfigDefaults.NoMaskProfileName — NOMASK_01 (line 32)

NOMASK_01 — the RESERVED, HUD-FREE profile name.

A profile by this name means "portrait conversion only": the Portrait Canvas Trick, the
150 / 1620 / 150 layout and the optional canvas-text strip, with ZERO HUD layers composited.
It is produced by `CreateNoMask` and is read-only — MaskOverlayManager refuses
to overwrite it and the Crop Tools app refuses to edit it — because the instant a layer is
added to it, it stops being what its name promises.

⚠️ IT MUST STORE EXPLICIT ZERO RECTS, NOT AN EMPTY "crops_1080p".
HudConfig.Sanitize builds its key set from HudKeys UNION the keys present in the file, and
for any key whose rect is MISSING it falls back to CropConfigDefaults.Create()'s rect. An
empty crops section would therefore be silently repopulated with the full Fortnite HUD on
the next ApplyProfile. A present-but-zero rect is read as-is and is the documented way to
express "this layer is switched off" (MobileFilterBuilder.RegisterLayer requires w >= 1 and
h >= 1). DO NOT "simplify" CreateNoMask to an empty object.

### CropConfigDefaults.SourceCropsSection (line 51)

IDEA_1 — the AUTHORITATIVE crop rectangles, in SOURCE-video pixels, `[width, height, x, y]`
to match "crops_1080p".

WHY THIS EXISTS. "crops_1080p" is content-space, and converting content -> source and back
again is LOSSY: CoordinateMath.TransformToContentAreaInt and
InverseTransformFromContentAreaInt both round strictly OUTWARD on purpose (the export crop
must COVER the selection or HUD pixels get clipped). Composing them therefore grows the rect
by up to 2px per axis. That was harmless while saved layers could not be reopened for
editing — nothing iterated the conversion. The moment the Crop Tool rehydrates a saved layer
into an editable box, every open/save cycle would compound that growth.

Storing the source rect removes the round trip entirely: editing reads these numbers
directly and never converts backwards. "crops_1080p" stays the single input the exporter
reads, so nothing downstream changes.

MISSING IS LEGAL. A v3 file has no such section; the Crop Tool derives it once via the
inverse transform (a single outward snap, exactly what happens today) and writes it back.

### CropConfigDefaults.Create — FORTNITEDEFAULT_02 (line 80)

Creates the default crop configuration.

The crop rectangles (crops_1080p) use [width, height, x, y] format in content-space
(1080x1620) coordinates. A layer is rendered only when width and height are both >= 1:
MobileFilterBuilder's RegisterLayer skips anything smaller, and the Crop Tools ghost
renderer skips w <= 1 || h <= 1. A zero-size rect is therefore the canonical way to
express "this layer is switched off" — see the deletion handling in
CropToolWindow.SaveConfig.

Negative X is normal and load-bearing, not a bug. Content X = 0 corresponds to source
X ~= 599.6 for a 1920x1080 input, because the 1280-wide internal window is a centre crop of
the 3414-wide scaled frame (see CoordinateMath.ScalePlan). HUD elements near the left edge
of the source therefore land at negative content X, which is why
CoordinateMath.ClampContentCrop deliberately permits a wide negative range.

ISSUE_6: this comment previously listed "minimap" among the overlays these defaults render.
It does not — the five keys below are the complete shipped set, and they match
HudConfig.HudKeys exactly. HudConfig.CropDriftType still recognises "map"/"minimap" because
the Crop Tool lets the user name a custom element freely and HudConfig.Sanitize admits
arbitrary keys, so a user-created minimap layer does receive the +1px LEFT bias. No default
minimap rectangle is shipped, and none has been invented here.

FORTNITEDEFAULT_02 — copied exactly from the dev sandbox's Apex Legends profile
on 2026-09-13, excluding the retired boss_hp layer. Shared by new profiles and recovery.

### CropConfigDefaults.CreateNoMask — NOMASK_01 (line 155)

NOMASK_01 — the reserved "No Mask Profile" document: the exact same schema, scales,
overlay positions and z-orders as `Create`, with EVERY crop rectangle zeroed.

Derived from Create() on purpose so the five HudKeys are guaranteed present (see the
remarks on `NoMaskProfileName` for why a missing key is not the same as a zero
key). The scales/overlays/z_orders values are left intact and are simply never read: with
no active layer, MobileFilterBuilder takes its zero-layer branch and composites nothing.

"crops_source" is dropped — it only exists so the Crop Tool can rehydrate a saved layer for
editing, and there is nothing here to rehydrate.

### CropConfigDefaults.IsHudFree — NOMASK_01 (line 179)

NOMASK_01 — true when `config` is a well-formed HUD-free document: every
key Create() ships is PRESENT (so Sanitize cannot fall back to a default rect) and no rect
anywhere in the section has both width and height >= 1 (so no layer can be composited).

Used to self-heal the reserved profile in MaskOverlayManager.EnsureDefaults — a file that
has drifted is rewritten rather than trusted.

### CropConfigDefaults.Rect (line 212)

Creates a rect array in [width, height, x, y] format matching the Python config.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Ipc/IpcProtocol.cs

### IpcProtocol.ServerMutexName — IPCLEASE_01 (line 118)

IPCLEASE_01 — THE SINGLE-SERVER LEASE. IT IS THE OPEN HANDLE, NOT AN ACQUIRED LOCK.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
WHAT WAS WRONG: the server created this mutex with `initiallyOwned: true` — that is,
it OWNED it — and released it in Dispose. A Win32 mutex is THREAD-AFFINE: only the thread
that acquired it may release it. Ownership was taken on whichever thread called TryStart
(startup) and released on whichever thread ran Dispose (shutdown / app teardown), which is
almost never the same one. So `ReleaseMutex` threw
  "Object synchronization method was called from an unsynchronized block of code"
on EVERY CLEAN SHUTDOWN, was swallowed by a bare catch, and the handle was then closed while
still owned — which marks the mutex ABANDONED. The next launch duly hit the
AbandonedMutexException branch and logged "Prior server process exited abruptly": a
permanently false crash signal on a completely normal exit.

THE FIX IS TO STOP OWNING IT. A named kernel object lives exactly as long as one handle to
it remains open, so "I hold a handle" is already a perfect lease:
  • `new Mutex(initiallyOwned: false, name, out createdNew)` — createdNew is true only
    for the process that created it, which is the one that becomes the server.
  • Nothing is ever acquired, so there is nothing to release, no thread affinity, and no
    abandoned state that can exist at all.
  • A crashed server closes its handle with the process; the name frees itself and the next
    launch simply creates it again.

⚠️ The NAME is deliberately unchanged. A build from before this fix, still running, OWNS this
mutex — and because `initiallyOwned` is ignored when the object already exists, old and
new builds still see each other's lease correctly and exactly one of them serves. Renaming it
(or switching to a Semaphore, which cannot share a name with a Mutex) would let two servers
bind the same pipe and silently diverge the session state. Keep the name.

⚠️ Mutex is also the only named primitive .NET implements on every platform. Named Semaphore
and EventWaitHandle are Windows-only and throw PlatformNotSupportedException elsewhere, which
takes the IPC test suite with them. Do not substitute one.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### IpcProtocol.ExchangeAsync — IPCDEADLINE_01 (line 327)

IPCDEADLINE_01 — one request/response exchange under ONE end-to-end deadline.

Connect, write AND read share a single linked token that fires after `deadline`;
the registration additionally disposes the stream so a peer that accepts the connection and then
never answers cannot hold the caller past the deadline even if an I/O primitive ignored the
token. The previous synchronous client bounded only `Connect()`; its `ReadFrame` had no
deadline at all and froze the UI thread forever against a hung peer.
Returns null on timeout, cancellation, a closed pipe or any I/O failure — never throws.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Ipc/NamedPipeStateClient.cs

### NamedPipeStateClient — IPCDEADLINE_01, UIIPC_01 (line 10)

Named-pipe client for the session-state server.

IPCDEADLINE_01 — every call is asynchronous and bounded end to end: connect, write and read run
under ONE deadline (`IpcProtocol.ExchangeAsync`). There is deliberately NO synchronous
variant: the former `GetStateSync`/`UpdatePropertiesSync`/`SaveStateSync`/
`ClearStateSync`/`SendHandoffSync` bounded only `Connect()` and then blocked in
`ReadFrame` with no deadline, and they were reachable from the UI thread (UIIPC_01).
UI code reads through `SessionStateWriter.ReadSnapshot` and writes through
`SessionStateWriter.Enqueue`.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Ipc/NamedPipeStateServer.cs

### NamedPipeStateServer._serverLease — IPCLEASE_01 (line 26)

IPCLEASE_01 — see `IpcProtocol.ServerMutexName`. This handle IS the lease; it is
never acquired and never released, so it has no thread affinity and can never be abandoned.

### NamedPipeStateServer.FlushDebounceMs — FLUSHCEILING_01 (line 40)

FLUSHCEILING_01 — the debounce interval. Coalescing is mandatory here: without it a timeline
drag would issue hundreds of AtomicJsonFile.WriteObject calls per second, each taking a
Global\ mutex and a WriteThrough flush, and the disk pressure would visibly stutter preview
playback.

### NamedPipeStateServer.FlushMaxWaitMs — FLUSHCEILING_01 (line 48)

FLUSHCEILING_01 — THE MAXIMUM a change may sit in memory before it is forced to disk.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
WHAT WAS WRONG: ScheduleDiskFlush did Stop() then Start() on a 500ms one-shot timer, and it
is called from EVERY mutating opcode. A CONTINUOUS stream of updates — which is precisely
what dragging a timeline knob or a volume slider produces — restarted the 500ms clock before
it could ever elapse. The flush was therefore postponed INDEFINITELY: the app believed it was
"continuously serialising the session" (docs/05 §SYS-RECOVERY) while in fact nothing reached
the disk for as long as the user kept working. A crash during that drag — the moment a crash
is MOST likely, because it is when the app is busiest — lost everything since the last pause.

A debounce with no maximum-wait ceiling is not a debounce. It is a promise that the work
happens only when the user stops.

THE FIX: the trailing 500ms edge still coalesces bursts, but once a change has been waiting
this long the flush is forced regardless of how much more traffic is arriving. Worst case is
bounded at one write per 3 seconds during sustained editing.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

FLUSHCEILING_01 — TickCount64 when the current unflushed change first appeared, or 0
when there is nothing pending. Guarded by `_stateLock`.

### NamedPipeStateServer.SaveState — IPCOWNER_01 (line 300)

### NamedPipeStateServer.ClearState — IPCOWNER_01 (line 303)

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Ipc/PipeAccess.cs

### PipeAccess — PIPEAUTH_01 (line 11)

PIPEAUTH_01 — both ends of every local named pipe prove who the other end is.

WHAT WAS WRONG: the servers (`NamedPipeStateServer`, `SingleInstanceGuard`) checked each
CLIENT's identity, but they were created with the default DACL and without
`PipeOptions.FirstPipeInstance`, and no client ever checked the SERVER. The pipe names
carry the user's SID, which is not a secret, so another account on the same machine could create
the pipe first and receive the paths and session state that this user's processes sent to it.

THE FIX, three layers:
  1. `CreateServer` — an explicit, protected DACL granting ONLY the current user and
     LocalSystem; no other account can connect to, or add an instance to, our pipe.
  2. `PipeOptions.FirstPipeInstance` wherever the caller knows no instance of its own
     is live, so a server never silently joins a pipe somebody else already created.
  3. `IsServerTrusted` — after connecting and BEFORE writing a byte, the client asks
     Windows which process owns the server end and refuses unless it runs as the same user.
     Any failure to establish that fails CLOSED.

### PipeAccess.CreateOwnerOnlySecurity — PIPEAUTH_01 (line 53)

PIPEAUTH_01 — the DACL every server pipe is created with: current user + LocalSystem, nothing inherited.

### PipeAccess.CreateServer — PIPEAUTH_01 (line 68)

PIPEAUTH_01 — creates a server pipe that only the current user (and LocalSystem) can reach.

### PipeAccess.CreateUpgradeServer — NOLOCALPORT_01, UPGRADE_07 (line 78)

NOLOCALPORT_01 — the installer's channel to its elevated worker and to the first launch of the
new version. It used to be a TCP port on 127.0.0.1, and third-party firewalls (ESET, Norton,
Comodo, ZoneAlarm…) ask about or silently block an unknown program that listens on a port —
the update then failed. A named pipe is not network traffic. The worker may run under ANOTHER
account (a standard user typing an administrator's password, UPGRADE_07), so besides the
current user and LocalSystem the DACL admits elevated administrators — who could reach any
local channel anyway. The 64-hex-digit token in the first message still decides who is the
worker. One instance, and it must be the first: nobody can pre-create the name.

### PipeAccess.NewUpgradePipeName — NOLOCALPORT_01 (line 100)

NOLOCALPORT_01 — a fresh, unguessable name for `CreateUpgradeServer`.

### PipeAccess.IsUpgradePipeName — NOLOCALPORT_01 (line 103)

NOLOCALPORT_01 — true only for names `NewUpgradePipeName` makes (checked before connecting).

### PipeAccess.IsServerTrusted — PIPEAUTH_01 (line 112)

PIPEAUTH_01 — true only when the process serving `client` runs as the same
Windows user as this process. Call after `Connect` and before the first write.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Ipc/SessionStateWriter.cs

### SessionStateWriter — UIIPC_01, IPCOWNER_01 (line 8)

UIIPC_01 — THE ONLY WAY UI CODE PERSISTS OR READS SESSION STATE.

UI threads used to call `StateTransferStore.UpdatePropertiesSync`, which dispatched the commit
to the thread pool and then BLOCKED the dispatcher on it with `GetAwaiter().GetResult()` for up
to the whole delivery budget, and `NamedPipeStateClient.GetStateSync`, whose synchronous
`WriteFrame`/`ReadFrame` had no deadline at all: a peer that accepted the connection and
never answered froze the window forever.

The contract here is:

- **Immutable snapshot in, nothing blocks.** `Enqueue` deep-clones the payload,
merges it into one pending batch (latest value per key wins — identical to applying the updates in
order, because an UpdateProperties replaces whole top-level keys) and returns.
- **Coalesced, ordered commits.** One background drain per writer; a batch is handed to
`StateTransferStore.UpdatePropertiesAsync` (IPCOWNER_01: in-process apply → pipe ACK →
lease-guarded disk) only after the previous one settled, so writes are never reordered.
- **Failure is reported, and the data is kept.** An unacknowledged batch is logged with
`CoreLogger.Fail`, raised through `CommitFailed`, and retained underneath any
newer values so the next commit or `FlushAsync` retries the latest state.
- **Memory-only reads.** `ReadSnapshot` never touches a pipe, a mutex or the
disk: the live in-process server's state, or the snapshot primed asynchronously at bootstrap,
overlaid with everything enqueued but not yet acknowledged.

### SessionStateWriter.For (line 39)

The process-wide writer for one session-state file.

### SessionStateWriter.FlushAllAsync (line 46)

Bounded final flush of every writer in this process (process exit path).

### SessionStateWriter.SessionStateWriter (line 78)

Composition/test seam: an explicit commit (and optional load) function.
The in-process server is not consulted; reads come from the primed snapshot only.

### SessionStateWriter.CommitFailed (line 89)

Raised on the thread pool when a batch was NOT acknowledged by the state owner.

### SessionStateWriter.HasUnacknowledgedChanges (line 92)

True while anything enqueued has not yet been acknowledged.

### SessionStateWriter.IsPrimed (line 98)

True once a snapshot was primed (bootstrap) or the live in-process server is available.

### SessionStateWriter.Prime (line 108)

Seeds the memory snapshot with state already loaded asynchronously (bootstrap).

### SessionStateWriter.PrimeAsync (line 116)

Loads cross-process state asynchronously, ONCE, before UI construction needs it.

### SessionStateWriter.ReadSnapshot — UIIPC_01 (line 138)

UIIPC_01 — memory only. Live in-process server state, else the primed snapshot, overlaid with
unacknowledged → in-flight → pending updates (newest last). Never blocks on IPC or disk.

### SessionStateWriter.Enqueue — UIIPC_01 (line 161)

UIIPC_01 — nonblocking from any thread. The payload is cloned immediately (callers may reuse it).
Returns the sequence number this update was assigned.

### SessionStateWriter.FlushAsync (line 185)

Waits (asynchronously, bounded) until everything enqueued before this call was acknowledged.
Returns false — and logs Fail — when it was not acknowledged within `timeout`.

### SessionStateWriter.MergeUnderPendingLocked (line 305)

Older values go UNDER the pending batch: a pending key always wins.

The commit always runs on the thread pool, so the named mutex is never taken on the UI
thread and no caller's SynchronizationContext is captured. Nothing may block on the result
(UIIPC_01): callers await it, or enqueue through `SessionStateWriter`.

### StateTransferStore.TryCommitAsPersistenceOwner — IPCOWNER_01, IPCLEASE_01 (line 235)

IPCOWNER_01 — the ONLY direct writer of SessionStateFile outside the server. Creating the lease
NEW proves that no server (current or pre-fix build, which OWNS the same name) is alive, and
holding the handle across the write keeps one from starting and loading the file until the
atomic replace has finished. Nothing is ever acquired on the lease (IPCLEASE_01).
Lock order: lease handle → `MutexName` → AtomicJsonFile. Synchronous on purpose:
the named mutex is thread-affine, so no await may sit between acquire and release.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/LocalAi/LocalSubjectTracker.cs

### LocalTrackingRequest (line 10)

What one local tracking run needs. Times are seconds; `SourceStartSec` is in the SOURCE file.

### LocalTrackingResult (line 22)

The dense result and the waypoints made from it.

### LocalSubjectTracker — LOCALTRACK_01 (line 34)

LOCALTRACK_01 (docs/01 TL-LOCALTRACK) — follows one box through a block on the user's own computer. The target is
tracked FORWARD from the moment it was picked to the end of the block and BACKWARD from that moment to the start
(in 2 s chunks read newest-first, so memory stays at one chunk). Each direction stops at a hard cut
(`SceneCutDetector`): the frames after it are "not visible" and the camera eases wide there.

### LocalSubjectTracker.Trace (line 46)

Diagnostics only (tests): one line per frame with the prediction and every candidate.

### LocalSubjectTracker.DirectionStart — LOCALTRACK_02 (line 118)

LOCALTRACK_02 — what the forward pass learned on the picked frame: the target's exact box, its look and its first motion per frame.

### LocalSubjectTracker.DirectionState (line 121)

One direction's memory: the last good box, its motion per frame, how long it has been lost, the target's usual
size (reference area: the first accepted box, then 90 % old + 10 % new) and the cut. While lost the motion is
kept, so the prompt coasts with the target instead of waiting where it was last seen.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/LocalAi/LocalTrackingMath.cs

### PixelBox (line 7)

A box in SOURCE pixels.

### MaskCandidate (line 21)

One mask the decoder proposed: its box in SOURCE pixels and the decoder's predicted IoU.

### TrackedFrame (line 24)

One tracked frame: time in seconds from the start of the block, the box, and whether the target was seen.

### LocalTrackingMath — LOCALTRACK_01 (line 27)

LOCALTRACK_01 (docs/01 TL-LOCALTRACK) — the pure maths around the local tracker: the model's input tensor, mask to
box, the next frame's box prompt, the "is this still the same target" check and the reduction of dense per-frame
boxes into the 0.5 s 0-1000 waypoints the existing `AiTrajectorySmoother` consumes.

### LocalTrackingMath.FillEncoderInput (line 43)

RGB24 (size × size) → normalised float CHW, the SAM 2 encoder input.

### LocalTrackingMath.BoxPrompt (line 58)

Prompt in model space: SAM's box corners (labels 2 = top-left, 3 = bottom-right) plus one positive point
(label 1) at `centreX`, `centreY`, where the target is expected to be.

### LocalTrackingMath.Iou (line 70)

Intersection over union of two boxes (0 when either is empty).

### LocalTrackingMath.SameSize — LOCALTRACK_02 (line 82)

LOCALTRACK_02 — how alike two boxes are in SIZE only (1 = same width and height), wherever they sit.

### LocalTrackingMath.MinAppearance (line 89)

Motion-aware choice among the decoder's masks (the SAMURAI idea): a candidate must pass `IsConsistent`
against the last good box and overlap the PREDICTED box (IoU at least `MinPredictedIou`); of those the
highest 0.4 × decoder score + 0.6 × IoU with the prediction wins. On the first frame (`last` empty)
the prediction is the starting box itself. Null = nothing believable.

### LocalTrackingMath.Choose — LOCALMEMORY_01, OWNBODY_01 (line 99)

LOCALMEMORY_01 — picks the candidate that is the same target. `appearance` (colour similarity with the
target's remembered look, 0-1) refuses a candidate below `MinAppearance` and adds to the score, so the
tracker does not swap onto someone else when two bodies cross. `excluded` (OWNBODY_01) refuses
the player's own avatar or hands.

### LocalTrackingMath.Appearance — LOCALMEMORY_01 (line 135)

The bounding box of the positive logits of one low-resolution mask, in SOURCE pixels. Null when empty.
LOCALMEMORY_01 — normalised 4×4×4 RGB histogram of the inner 80 % of `box` on the model-size frame.

### LocalTrackingMath.Similarity (line 163)

Bhattacharyya coefficient of two normalised histograms: 1 = same colours, 0 = nothing in common.

### LocalTrackingMath.Blend (line 172)

Moves the remembered look `rate` of the way towards `sample` (slow, so a brief swap cannot rewrite it).

### LocalTrackingMath.LooksLikeOwnBody — OWNBODY_01 (line 179)

OWNBODY_01 — true when `b` sits where the player's own body is for this footage: the third-person
avatar (large, lower centre — the same rule as the online path's SanitizeWaypointsAgainstLocalPlayer) or the
first-person hands and weapon (tall, touching the bottom, centre-right). Generic footage has none.

### LocalTrackingMath.ShiftMask — LOCALMEMORY_01 (line 193)

LOCALMEMORY_01 — the remembered mask (low-resolution logits) moved by (`dx`, `dy`)
mask pixels into `dst`; uncovered pixels get `fill` (background).

### LocalTrackingMath.NextPrompt (line 230)

The prompt for the next frame: the last box moved by its motion over `framesAhead` frames
(1 normally; more while the target is lost, so the prompt coasts with it) and widened by
`PromptMargin` of its size plus the per-frame speed on each side, ×(1 + 0.2 per lost frame, max 2.5).

### LocalTrackingMath.IsConsistent (line 247)

True when the new box is believable as the SAME target: the model is confident, the size changed by at most
`MaxStepAreaRatio`× since the last good box and stays within `MaxReferenceAreaRatio`× of
the target's usual size (`referenceArea`, 0 = unknown) — so a mask cannot creep step by step
over the background — and the centre did not leap further than 1.5 box diagonals.

### LocalTrackingMath.ToWaypoints (line 269)

Dense frames → one waypoint every `WaypointIntervalSec` (0-1000 grid): a window is visible when at
least half its frames saw the target, and its box is the MEDIAN of those frames (one wrong frame cannot move
the camera). Windows without frames are left out; the first and last windows are anchored at 0 and the end.

### LocalTrackingMath.FromGrid (line 321)

0-1000 box (the AI discovery grid) → source pixels.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/LocalAi/Sam2BoxTracker.cs

### Sam2BoxTracker — LOCALTRACK_01 (line 6)

LOCALTRACK_01 (docs/01 TL-LOCALTRACK) — SAM 2.1 used as a frame-to-frame box tracker: every frame is encoded once,
then the mask decoder is prompted with the previous box (moved by its motion, widened by a margin) and the best
mask becomes the new box. The model is class-agnostic, so it follows whatever the first box held — a player, an
animal, a vehicle — and never "prefers" the large foreground character.

### Sam2BoxTracker.Remember — LOCALMEMORY_01 (line 52)

LOCALMEMORY_01 — keeps mask `index` of the last `Step` as the next frame's mask prompt.

### Sam2BoxTracker.Forget (line 66)

Drops the remembered mask (a new direction, or the target has been lost for a while).

### Sam2BoxTracker.Step (line 69)

Runs one frame (RGB24, model size square) with a box prompt and an expected centre in SOURCE pixels. Returns every
non-empty mask the decoder proposed and the decoder's "object present" logit (above 0 = in the picture; +infinity
when the model has no such output).

### Sam2BoxTracker.Candidates (line 116)

Every non-empty mask of the decoder output as a box with its predicted IoU.
