## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AiHudDetection.cs

### HudCandidateSource (line 10)

Where a Magic Wand candidate came from.

### HudCandidateSource.Local (line 13)

The offline `HudAutoDetector` only.

### HudCandidateSource.Ai (line 16)

The AI provider only (validated, converted once to source pixels).

### HudCandidateSource.Fused (line 19)

A local box the AI agreed with — ONE result, never two.

### HudCandidate (line 23)

One Magic Wand suggestion in SOURCE-FRAME pixel space (the frozen frame's own pixels — never
Canvas or display pixels). This is a suggestion only: nothing in this type can commit a crop.

### AiHudCandidate (line 37)

One validated AI answer. Coordinates are NORMALIZED 0..1 SOURCE-FRAME coordinates, top-left
origin; `AiHudResponseParser` is the only producer and guarantees every field is
finite, inside the frame, and neither tiny nor near-whole-screen.

### AiHudFrame (line 50)

The ONE frozen frame an AI provider is allowed to see. Never a video, never a clip.

**JpegBytes:** The encoded still. Scaling it does not change normalized coordinates.
**SourceWidth:** Width of the source frame the answer will be mapped back onto.
**SourceHeight:** Height of the source frame the answer will be mapped back onto.
**SourceFingerprint:** Identity of the clip (no path text, no secrets).
**FrameKey:** Identity of THIS frame (timestamp and/or content hash).

### AiHudOutcome (line 65)

How an AI request ended. Every value except `Success` means "use local only".

### AiHudOutcome.AccountProblem — AIHUDERRORS_01 (line 75)

AIHUDERRORS_01 — key rejected, no permission, model gone or quota used up: only the user can fix it.

### AiHudDetectionResult (line 79)

The result of one AI request. `Detail` is technical text for the log; it is
guaranteed by every implementation to contain no credential.

### AiHudDetectionResult.UserMessage — AIHUDERRORS_01 (line 85)

AIHUDERRORS_01 — the provider's plain-English sentence for the user (no credential), or null.

### IAiHudDetectionService — AIHUD_01 (line 92)

AIHUD_01 — an optional cloud HUD detector. Implementations hold NO UI types and perform their
own validation through `AiHudResponseParser`.

**Contract.** `DetectAsync` never throws for provider trouble — a missing
key, a timeout, a 429, a network failure or a malformed answer all come back as an
`AiHudDetectionResult` with a non-success outcome. The ONLY exception it lets out is
`OperationCanceledException` for the CALLER'S token, because a cancel is the user
getting what they asked for.

### IAiHudDetectionService.ProviderName (line 104)

Human name of the provider, for the privacy notice ("Google Gemini").

### IAiHudDetectionService.IsConfigured (line 107)

True when a credential is present. Says nothing about whether it is valid.

### AiHudLabelMapper — AIHUD_01 (line 113)

AIHUD_01 — maps a free-text AI label to one of this suite's HUD role keys, or null when the
label names something real that simply has no built-in role (e.g. "ability cooldowns").
Deterministic, ordinal, culture-invariant.

### AiHudGeometry — AIHUD_01 (line 141)

AIHUD_01 — THE one conversion from normalized AI coordinates to source-frame pixels.

Done exactly once, here, against the SOURCE frame's own width and height — never the
Canvas, never the display, never the downscaled JPEG that was sent. The result feeds the
existing crop geometry pipeline unchanged.

Edges are rounded independently (left/top and right/bottom) and clamped to the frame, so
two adjacent boxes share an edge rather than overlapping or leaving a 1px gap, and a box that
touches the right edge maps to exactly `sourceWidth`.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AiHudResponseParser.cs

### AiHudParseResult — AIHUD_01 (line 10)

AIHUD_01 — the outcome of parsing one AI answer.

**Candidates:** Only the candidates that survived every check, best first.
**Rejected:** How many entries were present but failed validation.
**Error:** Non-null when the document as a whole could not be read (malformed JSON,
wrong shape). Candidates is then empty.

### AiHudResponseParser — AIHUD_01 (line 22)

AIHUD_01 — DO NOT TRUST MODEL GEOMETRY. The only door an AI answer comes in through.

**Schema** (normalized 0..1 SOURCE-FRAME coordinates, top-left origin):

```csharp

{ "candidates": [ { "label": "minimap", "normalizedX": 0.82, "normalizedY": 0.03,
                    "normalizedWidth": 0.15, "normalizedHeight": 0.26, "confidence": 0.9 } ] }

```

A bare top-level array of the same objects is also accepted, and a markdown code fence
around the JSON is stripped — models add one even when told not to. Anything else is
malformed.

**Rejected per entry:** missing/non-numeric fields; NaN or ±Infinity; negative or zero
width/height; X/Y outside [0,1]; a box whose right/bottom edge leaves the frame; confidence
outside [0,1]; an empty label; a box smaller than `MinSide` on either side or
`MinArea` in area (sensor noise, not HUD); a box larger than
`MaxSide` on either side or `MaxArea` in area (near-whole-screen junk —
no single HUD element covers a third of the screen).

Parsing uses `JsonDocument` only, so it is NativeAOT- and trim-safe with no
reflection and no source-generated context.

### AiHudResponseParser.EdgeEpsilon (line 46)

Tolerance for a right/bottom edge that lands a hair past 1.0 through float noise.

### AiHudResponseParser.MinSide (line 49)

1% of the frame on the short side: a 1080p health pip is ~11px; anything smaller is noise.

### AiHudResponseParser.MinArea (line 52)

0.05% of the frame's area (~1,000 px² at 1080p).

### AiHudResponseParser.MaxSide (line 55)

No HUD element spans 90% of either axis.

### AiHudResponseParser.MaxArea (line 58)

No HUD element covers more than a third of the frame.

### AiHudResponseParser.MaxLabelLength (line 61)

Longest label kept; longer text is truncated, not rejected.

### AiHudResponseParser.MaxCandidates (line 64)

Hard cap on AI candidates taken from one answer (highest confidence first).

### AiHudResponseParser.MaxEntriesInspected (line 67)

Hard cap on entries even looked at, so a runaway answer cannot cost unbounded work.

### AiHudResponseParser.TryReadCandidate (line 141)

True when one entry passes every geometric and semantic check.

### AiHudResponseParser.IsValid (line 161)

The geometry and confidence gate, public so a test (and any other producer) applies the
exact same rule.

### AiHudResponseParser.CleanLabel (line 201)

Single line, printable, trimmed, at most `MaxLabelLength` characters.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AiHudResultCache.cs

### AiHudCacheKey — AIHUD_04 (line 7)

AIHUD_04 — what an AI HUD answer is keyed by. All three parts are required, so an answer can
never be reused for another clip, another frame, or another model. Holds no credential and no
path text: `SourceFingerprint` and `FrameKey` are opaque hashes.

### AiHudResultCache — AIHUD_04 (line 20)

AIHUD_04 — a small, bounded, in-memory, per-process cache of SUCCESSFUL AI HUD answers.

Never persisted (nothing here reaches a project, recovery or settings file), never stores
a credential, and only ever stores `AiHudOutcome.Success` results — a timeout or a
rate limit is not an answer. Thread-safe. Oldest entry is evicted first.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AiTrajectorySmoother.cs

### RawSubjectWaypoint (line 11)

AI Tracking raw waypoint received from the Gemini vision protocol.
Coordinates are normalized to a 0-1000 scale relative to the frame.

### AiTrackingKeyframe (line 36)

A time-indexed keyframe along a smoothed AI camera zoom path.
Coordinates are in source video pixels.

### AiTrackingJsonContext (line 64)

JSON serializer context for AI tracking data structures to ensure Native AOT compliance.

### AiTrajectorySmoother (line 74)

Mathematical smoothing, velocity-adaptive breathing, and vanishing-target fallback
engine for universal AI subject tracking.

### AiTrajectorySmoother.SmoothedTrajectory.RawWaypoints — AIRESMOOTH_01 (line 94)

AIRESMOOTH_01 — the raw waypoints Gemini returned (after sanitising, times relative to the
zoom start). Kept so "EDIT AI ZOOM" can re-frame with new settings WITHOUT another paid AI
call. Null for trajectories written before this field existed (they can only be re-tracked).
Omitted from the JSON when null so older documents round-trip byte-identically.

### AiTrajectorySmoother.SmoothedTrajectory.IsScaleDynamic (line 167)

True when the keyframes change the zoom scale (the export then uses the scale+crop form).

### AiTrajectorySmoother.SmoothedTrajectory.EvaluateExportCrop — AIPARITY_01 (line 177)

AIPARITY_01 — the SOURCE-pixel region the EXPORT's crop expression (`ToFfmpegCropFilter`)
selects at `relSec` seconds after the zoom's own start, on an input frame of
`inW` x `inH` (the zoom's resolution after the pre-scale).

This is the expression evaluated in C#, term for term: the same 2-decimal rounding of
every keyframe value and time, the same LINEAR piecewise interpolation (the export never used
`EvaluateAt`'s smoothstep), the same even truncation and the same
`min(in-out, max(0, v))` clamp. The live preview (`ZoomPreviewSimulator`) uses
THIS, so preview and export frame the same region by construction.

### AiTrajectorySmoother.SmoothedTrajectory.Piecewise (line 228)

The value `BuildPiecewiseExpr`'s expression evaluates to at time `t`.

### AiTrajectorySmoother.SmoothedTrajectory.Round2 (line 244)

What a "0.00"-formatted number reads back as.

### AiTrajectorySmoother.SmoothedTrajectory.ToFfmpegCropFilter — AIPARITY_01 (line 248)

Formulates an FFmpeg piecewise-lerp crop filter expression across keyframes:
crop=w='W(t)':h='H(t)':x='X(t)':y='Y(t)':eval=frame

AIPARITY_01 — `timeOffsetSec` is how far into the zoom the CHUNK this filter
runs in starts. Every export chunk restarts its own clock at 0 (`setpts=PTS-STARTPTS`), so a
zoom split into several chunks (a cut or a freeze inside it) used to restart the trajectory from
its first keyframe at every split. 0 (the default) emits exactly the previous expression.

### AiTrajectorySmoother.AvoidHudZones — AIHUDZONE_01 (line 794)

AIHUDZONE_01 — moves one crop window out of every HUD zone it overlaps, by the SMALLEST single
horizontal or vertical shift that (a) clears the zone, (b) keeps the window inside the frame and
(c) keeps the whole target box inside the window. When no shift satisfies all three the window
is left where it is: the target always wins over the HUD. Two passes, so clearing one zone
cannot silently push the window into another without a second look.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AsyncProcessRunner.cs

### AsyncProcessRunner.WantsStdinQuitCommand (line 76)

FFmpeg-style interactive tools accept 'q' on stdin as a clean quit; anything else
(and any run without redirected stdin) goes straight to the hard-kill escalation.

### AsyncProcessRunner.DrainReadersAsync (line 85)

Waits for both pipe readers to finish, but never longer than
`GracefulProcessTerminator.HardKillConfirmMs` — a caller waiting on a
cancellation must stay strictly bounded.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AudioFilterChain.cs

### AudioFilterChain — VOPROT_01, DUCKMB_01, VOPRIO_01, LOUDSTD_REMOVED_01 (line 9)

Builds the FFmpeg audio filter chain for the rendering pipeline (Main App and Video Merger —
one implementation, so a mix sounds the same in both).

Pipeline:
1. Game audio: optional caller filters, the VIDEO fader.
2. For each music track: atrim, fade in/out, the MUSIC fader, delay to align; several tracks are summed.
3. VOPROT_01: across voice-over takes only, the music is ducked 85% and speech-carved (gated).
4. DUCKMB_01: multiband sidechain against the gameplay (+voice) — see `Build`.
5. Game and music are summed with weights '1 1', normalize=0.

PRIORITY (VOPRIO_01): voice-over first, gameplay second, music last. The voice is protected from
both other buses by the take pulse (step 3 here, the game side in ProcessWorker); the gameplay is
protected from the music by the sidechain (step 4), whose trigger is the game bus WITH the voice
mixed in, so a take also pushes the music down.

LEVEL CONTRACT (LOUDSTD_REMOVED_01): there is no loudness normalisation anywhere. Each bus
arrives at its recorded level and is scaled by its own fader only. Peak protection
(`PeakSafety`) is applied by the callers: the tamer on the game bus before it gets
here, the always-on safety limiter on the final mix after.

### AudioFilterChain.CrossfadeOutSec — XFADEPLAN_01 (line 33)

Song-to-song: the OUTGOING song starts fading out this long before its end.
XFADEPLAN_01 — the number lives in `MusicCrossfade` so the music plan and the
previews lay songs out exactly the way this chain plays them.

### AudioFilterChain.EdgeFadeSec (line 38)

Fade applied at the very start and the very end of the whole music bed.

### AudioFilterChain.Build — ISSUE_04, FREEZESFX_01, VOPROT_01 (line 41)

Builds the complete audio filter chain.
Returns (filterChains, finalLabel).
Exact port of build_audio_chain().

**musicLeadFadeIn:**
ISSUE_04 — false when the user dragged the music-start note marker to the RIGHT of
MARK START. The music then begins partway into the video, which is a deliberate entrance,
so it hits at full level instead of fading up. True (music starts with the video) gives
the normal `EdgeFadeSec` lead-in.

**musicTailFadeOut:**
ISSUE_04 — false when the user dragged the music-end note marker to the RIGHT of
MARK END. The music is asking to outlast the video, so there is nothing left to fade
over: it is cut dead at MARK END. True gives the normal `EdgeFadeSec` tail.

**freezeMusicDipPulse:**
FREEZESFX_01 — `FreezeSoundEffect.MusicDipPulse` on the music's clock: 1 while a freeze
plays. Non-null means the music bed dips under every freeze and comes back after it.

**voiceProtectMusicPulse:**
VOPROT_01 — "Protect VoiceOver Recording from Music". An ffmpeg expression that evaluates to
1.0 wherever a voice-over take is playing and 0 elsewhere, built by ProcessWorker from the
take times. Non-null means the MUSIC bed is ducked and carved across those windows.

This is NOT the wizard's own ducking. That one is triggered by the GAME and protects the
game from the music. This one is triggered by the VOICE and protects the voice from the
music, so the two are orthogonal and can be on or off in any combination.

It is applied to the music bed BEFORE the wizard's crossover/sidechain apparatus, so the
music that reaches the sidechain is already out of the voice's way and the two stages do
not fight over the same band.

### AudioFilterChain.CrossoverLowHz — DUCKMB_01 (line 372)

DUCKMB_01 — the music bed's band edges, Hz.

### AudioFilterChain.VoiceDuckDepth — VOPROT_01 (line 376)

VOPROT_01 — the voice protection's level dip (85%) and speech-band carve.

### AudioFilterChain.VoiceRampSec — VOPROT_01 (line 379)

VOPROT_01 — the pulse's ramp either side of every take, seconds (the export's `0.3`).

### AudioFilterChain.VoiceProtectionPulseAt — VOPREVIEW_02 (line 382)

VOPREVIEW_02 — the export's voice-protection pulse evaluated at output second `t`:
`clip(Σ clip((t-(s-0.3))/0.3,0,1)·clip(((e+0.3)-t)/0.3,0,1), 0, 1)` over the takes (ProcessWorker).
The SUM matters: two takes closer than 0.6 s overlap their ramps and the export adds them; the live
preview used to take the MAXIMUM and dipped less there. Every live preview uses this.

### AudioFilterChain.GatedVoiceProtection — VOGATE_01 (line 401)

VOGATE_01 — voice protection that exists ONLY across the takes.

The level dip was always time-varying (`volume=...:eval=frame`), but the speech carve was a
plain `equalizer`, which has no time-varying gain — so it scooped 2.5 kHz out of the whole
bus for the whole video, takes or no takes. Now the bus is split: a carved copy is faded in by
the take pulse and the dry copy faded out by its complement (the pulse already ramps over
0.3 s), then the two are summed. Outside the takes the output is the dry signal exactly.

### AudioFilterChain.ReadBool — AUDIOCHK_01 (line 421)

AUDIOCHK_01 — type-agnostic, non-throwing bool read. See the comment at the call site.

### MusicTrack (line 448)

Represents a single music track for the audio chain.
Path, offset (in seconds from file start), duration, timeline delay (in seconds from video start), and whether to fade out.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AudioGraphPruner.cs

### AudioGraphPruner — PREVIEWMIX_01 (line 10)

PREVIEWMIX_01 — cuts an export filter graph down to the chains that produce ONE audio label.

The live preview's rendered mix is the export's OWN audio graph, not a re-implementation of it:
ProcessWorker builds the full graph exactly as it would for the export, and this keeps only the
chains the final audio label depends on. The single coupling between picture and sound in that
graph — the meme splice `concat=n=N:v=1:a=1` — is rewritten to its audio half
(`v=0:a=1`, audio inputs and output only). Video inputs are then never decoded, so a whole
clip's final mix renders in seconds.

A kept chain whose output nothing kept consumes (e.g. one pad of a split whose other consumer
was video) is terminated with `anullsink`, because ffmpeg rejects unconnected pads.

### AudioGraphPruner.Prune (line 32)

Returns the pruned graph whose only exposed output is `finalAudioLabel`.

### AudioGraphPruner.IsStreamSpecifier (line 78)

"0:a", "12:v" — a reference to an input file's stream, not a graph label.

### AudioGraphPruner.SplitTopLevel (line 102)

Splits on ';' outside single quotes (expressions may contain anything).

### AudioPreviewMap — PREVIEWMIX_01 (line 122)

PREVIEWMIX_01 — where the live preview's clock lands in the rendered mix.

The preview clock is BODY output seconds from MARK START (OutputTimeline, memes excluded). The
rendered mix is the export's audio: thumbnail intro first, then the body starting at the
fade-in pad, with every meme spliced in at its render-clock cut.

**IntroSec:** Thumbnail-intro silence at the very start.
**PadStartSec:** Fade-in pad before MARK START (output seconds).
**Memes:** Each spliced meme: its cut in the render clock (intro included) and its length.

### AudioPreviewMap.MixSecFor (line 134)

Mix position for preview body time `bodySec` (memes at that instant not yet played).

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AudioLoudnessProbe.cs

### LoudnessReading — LOUDSTD_REMOVED_01 (line 9)

One loudness measurement of a source file, in the units FFmpeg's `loudnorm` analysis
reports them.

LOUDSTD_REMOVED_01 — there is NO loudness standard in this app any more. The old
"too quiet / too loud versus -14 LUFS" verdict, its prompt, its setting and the dead
normalisation pipeline behind it were deleted. A reading exists for exactly three consumers:
harsh-peak detection (`HasHarshPeaks`), the peak tamer's threshold
(`PeakSafety.TamerThresholdDb`), and matching a meme / a Merger clip to the
gameplay it sits in (`MemeLoudness`). None of them moves the gameplay level.

**IntegratedLufs:** Average perceived loudness over the measured window (LUFS).
**TruePeakDbtp:** Highest true peak (dBTP). Above 0 clips.
**LoudnessRangeLu:** Spread between the quiet and loud parts (LU).

### LoudnessReading.PeakAboveAverageLu (line 28)

Crest factor: how far the loudest instant sticks out above the average body of the
audio. A conversational clip sits near 10-14 LU; a gameplay capture with an explosion
or a scream spikes far higher, and that spike is what hurts a viewer wearing headphones.

### LoudnessReading.HasHarshPeaks (line 35)

True when the file contains sudden peaks far above its own average AND those peaks
actually reach the danger zone — the "quiet video, then an explosion takes your head off"
problem, which the peak tamer fixes.

### AudioLoudnessProbe (line 45)

Measures a media file's loudness and peaks (EBU R128, via FFmpeg's `loudnorm` in pure
analysis mode — its output is discarded into the null muxer, nothing is ever normalised).

### AudioLoudnessProbe.HarshPeakFloorDbtp (line 51)

A true peak above this counts towards `LoudnessReading.HasHarshPeaks`.

### AudioLoudnessProbe.MinSegmentSec — BEDSEG_01 (line 56)

BEDSEG_01 — the shortest window worth measuring on its own.

EBU R128 integrated loudness gates in 400 ms blocks and then applies a relative gate across
them; with only a second or two of material the relative gate has almost nothing to work on
and loudnorm reports a figure that swings wildly or comes back as -70 (silence). Anything
shorter than this falls back to measuring the whole file — a known-imperfect answer beats a
random one.

### AudioLoudnessProbe.CrestWarnLu (line 67)

Crest factor above which peaks count as "harsh". Speech and music normally land around
10-14 LU above their own average; beyond 15 LU there is a genuine bang in the file.

### AudioLoudnessProbe.MeasureAsync — BEDSEG_01 (line 73)

Measures `inputPath` end to end.

Integrated loudness is only meaningful over a WHOLE CONTIGUOUS STRETCH — a clip that is
silent for a minute and then deafening averages out to something neither half resembles —
so this decodes the entire window rather than sampling within it. By default that window is
the whole file; BEDSEG_01 added the option to narrow it to the part that will actually be
used (the exported range of a clip).
Video, subtitles and data streams are dropped
(`-vn -sn -dn`) so only the audio is touched, which keeps it fast enough to run in
the background while the user is already working.

Returns null when the file has no audio, when FFmpeg fails, or when cancelled. A null
return must never block the user — it simply means "we could not tell", and the caller
stays silent rather than guessing.

**segmentStartSec:**
BEDSEG_01 — optional: measure only from this point in the file. See `MinSegmentSec`.

**segmentDurationSec:**
BEDSEG_01 — optional: measure only this many seconds. Ignored together with
`segmentStartSec` when the window is shorter than `MinSegmentSec`.

### AudioLoudnessProbe.ParseJsonBlock (line 224)

Pulls the final JSON object out of loudnorm's stderr. The filter prints its report last,
so the LAST braces in the stream are the ones that matter.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/AudioTempoFilterBuilder.cs

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder — TEMPO_01, AVSYNC_01, CLIPFRAMES_01, SPLICE_01 (line 9)

TEMPO_01 — THE ONE AUDIO TEMPO POLICY (03 FFM-TEMPO). Every export route that changes the speed
of clip audio asks this class for its filters: the Main App base-speed path
(`ProcessWorker`), every granular chunk (`GranularSpeedBuilder.Build`),
Merger plain clips (`MergerWorker`) and Merger effects clips (via
`MergeClipGraph` → `GranularSpeedBuilder.Build`). No other file may spell
an `atempo=` or `rubberband=` filter.

**Policy.**

- **1.0x** — NO filter (AVSYNC_01: `atempo=1.0000` still runs WSOLA and was measured
moving a sharp attack 19.3 ms).
- **< 1.0x** — `rubberband=tempo={s}:transients=mixed` when the bundled FFmpeg
GENUINELY supports it (probed once, cached); otherwise the atempo chain.
- **> 1.0x** — the atempo chain, unchanged. No measurement justified a change there.

**Why `transients=mixed` (measured, reference FFmpeg build with the identical option
table to the bundled one).** The default (`crisp`) left a steady 440 Hz tone 0.3-0.6 % flat
(437.4 Hz at 0.5x) with the off-band floor at only -12 dB; `mixed` holds it at 440.00 Hz while
keeping crisp's attack (10-90 % rise 9-25 ms); `smooth` is cleanest on tones but smeared a
percussive hit to a 59 ms rise at 0.25x — gunshots and footsteps are what game audio is made of.

**Duration.** Neither filter's own output length is trusted: every caller keeps its
existing hard bound (`apad,atrim=duration=` per granular chunk, CLIPFRAMES_01's
`apad,atrim=end=` on Merger clips) and its SPLICE_01/03 fades, which sit AFTER the tempo
filter. Measured unbounded lengths: rubberband exact at every tested rate; atempo up to 1 % short.

**Capability.** Unknown (never probed — unit tests, gates) means ABSENT, so a graph built
before or without a probe is the old, always-valid atempo graph. The probe runs once per FFmpeg
binary on the export thread (`EnsureProbedAsync`) and asks two questions: does the
filter's option table name `tempo` and `transients`/`mixed` (the exact syntax this
class emits), and does a 0.25 s tone actually pass through that exact filter with exit code 0
(a listed filter whose library fails to load is not support). Any failure caches ABSENT.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.RubberbandOptions — AVSYNC_02 (line 46)

The Rubber Band options appended to every slowdown filter. Verified against the bundled option table.
AVSYNC_02 — `window=short`: the standard analysis window smears a burst's onset across
tens of milliseconds and a slow segment's first event lands late. Measured with the bundled
FFmpeg (1 kHz burst, 48 kHz): onset on a segment's first sample +8.1/+8.7/+8.7 ms → +4.2/+4.2/+4.2 ms,
mid-segment onset error -59…-40/-35…-29/-11…-10 ms → -27…-26/-16…-14/-5…-4 ms at 0.25/0.5/0.75x;
a steady 440 Hz tone stays 440.00 Hz at every rate and its off-band floor improves
(-37.3/-37.7/-41.7 dB → -40.1/-40.1/-44.1 dB).

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.MinTempo (line 57)

Rubber Band and atempo both accept 0.01..100; the chain is clamped to the same range as before.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.UnityEpsilon — AVSYNC_01 (line 61)

The AVSYNC_01 no-op band around 1.0x.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.RubberbandAvailable (line 71)

The capability the builders currently use (a test scope wins over the probe).

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.IsProbed (line 74)

True once a probe has finished in this process.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.OverrideCapability (line 77)

TESTS ONLY — forces the capability for the current async flow (other tests are unaffected).
Dispose to restore.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.EngineFor (line 93)

Which engine the policy picks for a rate (normalised exactly as `Build(double, bool)` does).

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.Build (line 102)

The tempo filters for a rate under the current capability.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.Build (line 105)

The tempo filters for a rate under an explicit capability. Empty at 1.0x.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.Segment — AVSYNC_01 (line 120)

The chain as a graph segment: "" at 1.0x (AVSYNC_01 — no dangling comma), otherwise the
filters joined by commas with ONE comma on the requested side.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.Segment (line 126)

As `Segment(double, bool)`, with the input length that bounds the compensated Rubber Band output.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.RubberbandStartPadSamples — AVSYNC_04 (line 143)

AVSYNC_04 — Rubber Band's documented start compensation, which FFmpeg's `rubberband` filter
does not perform (it runs the realtime stretcher and passes its output straight through).
The library asks for `getPreferredStartPad()` samples of silence before the audio and for
`getStartDelay()` samples to be dropped from the start of the output; with the short window
at 48 kHz both are 512 for every ratio (Rubber Band 3 API, queried at 0.1-0.99x). Without it,
a slow segment's first event lands +4.2 ms late and events inside it -27…-4 ms early.
Measured with the bundled FFmpeg (1 kHz burst, 48 kHz, 0.25/0.5/0.75x): segment start
+4.2 → +0.06 ms at every rate; inside the segment -27…-26 → +6…+14, -16…-14 → -4.1…-3.7,
-4.8…-4.2 → -2.3…-0.5 ms; 440 Hz stays 440.00 Hz.
The padding makes the stretcher's flushed tail longer by 512/tempo - 512 samples, so when the
caller knows its input length the output is cut to exactly input / tempo, as before.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.BuildAtempoChain — ISSUE_04 (line 175)

The atempo-only fallback chain (ISSUE_04 guard included). Kept public for the gates that
verify atempo's [0.5, 2.0] element bounds; export routes call `Build(double)`.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.Normalise — ISSUE_04 (line 181)

ISSUE_04 — zero, negative or non-finite rates spun the old normalisation loops forever
(0/0.5 is 0 forever) and froze the app on PROCESS. Fall back to 1.0x, loudly; then clamp.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.EnsureProbedAsync (line 212)

Probes `ffmpegPath` once (cached per path for the process lifetime) and
publishes the result to `RubberbandAvailable`. Never throws; failure = absent.
Call on the export thread BEFORE any graph is built.

### AudioTempoFilterBuilder.HelpListsRequiredOptions (line 239)

Pure parse of `ffmpeg -h filter=rubberband`: the filter must exist and its option table
must carry every name this class emits.

### AudioTempoEngine (line 320)

The engine `AudioTempoFilterBuilder` chose for a rate.

to syllable weighting.

output-time `MappedCaptionLine` items.

  - Cuts: words falling completely inside cuts are dropped; lines with zero surviving words are pruned.
  - Speed ramps: each word is stretched or compressed proportionally to the footage speed.
  - Freezes: pauses and extends the duration of the held word.
  - Durations: centisecond precision for ASS \k karaoke tags.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/CompositeTimeline.cs

### CompositeClip (line 6)

One clip's place in the `CompositeTimeline`.

### CompositeClip.OutputLengthSec (line 21)

Output seconds of this clip BEFORE the global base speed (speed/freeze/memes applied).

### CompositeTimeline — COMPOSITE_01, FRAMESNAP_01 (line 25)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
COMPOSITE_01 — THE VIDEO MERGER'S ONE TIME MAPPER (Video-Merger-Migration.md P2.3).

Three clocks, never mixed:
  1. SOURCE µs   — inside one clip's file (`EdlAnchor`). Real frame pts (FRAMESNAP_01).
  2. MERGED frame — kept content of all clips end to end at 1.0x, integer frames at
                    `MergeFps`. Integer, so 200 clips add up with zero drift. Each
                    clip is rounded on its own, which is exactly what the export's per-clip
                    fps=60 normalisation produces.
  3. OUTPUT sec  — the finished file: each clip's own speed/freeze/memes/cuts (the Main App's
                    `OutputTimeline`, so both apps share one set of time maths) with
                    the global base speed applied per clip to footage OUTSIDE speed segments (D19,
                    the Main App editor's rule), then the synthetic thumbnail intro in front.

Intro removal is DERIVED here, never stored per clip (user decision D13): clip 1 loses its intro
only when a custom thumbnail replaces it; clips 2..N lose theirs while the scraper is on.

Every consumer (preview, music, export, undo remap) reads this object, so they cannot disagree.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

this is the held/next source moment (OutputTimeline's documented lossy inverse). Inside the
synthetic intro it is the first frame.

### CompositeTimeline.Remap (line 273)

Moves a merged frame chosen on `from` onto this timeline through
(ClipId, source µs), so it survives intro toggles, a new thumbnail and reordering.
Null (stale) when that clip is no longer queued.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/CoordinateMath.cs

### Frac (line 8)

Exact rational arithmetic to match Python's Fraction semantics.
Used throughout coordinate transforms to guarantee identical rounding.

### Frac.FromString (line 59)

Parses "30", "30000/1001" or "29.97". Values arrive from ffprobe/ffmpeg output, so nothing
here may throw on malformed text — an unparseable or zero-denominator expression yields
`Zero` and lets the caller apply its own default.

### CoordinateMath.GetResolutionInts (line 210)

Parses "1920x1080" into numbers, falling back to 1920x1080 for anything unusable.

ISSUE_2 — two guards that were missing. This value is not always produced in-process: zoom
settings persist it as plain TEXT in the recovery JSON on disk (SpeedSegment.ZoomOrigRes),
and it comes back out through GranularSpeedBuilder at export time. A damaged or hand-edited
file could therefore feed this anything.

  * ZERO. The regex happily matches "0x0". A zero then reached ScalePlan's
    `new Frac(InternalW, inW)`, and the Frac constructor throws DivideByZeroException on a
    zero denominator. Both dimensions must be positive to be a usable resolution.
  * OVERFLOW. int.Parse throws OverflowException on a run of digits too large for an int.
    TryParse simply fails instead.

Both faults threw out of the export path and out of crash recovery — and until ISSUE_1 was
fixed, a throw during recovery deleted the user's saved session. Falling back is always
safer here than throwing: a wrong-but-sane resolution produces a slightly wrong crop, while
an exception loses the whole job.

### CoordinateMath.InverseTransformFromContentAreaInt (line 332)

Content-space rect -> SOURCE-video rect, as whole even pixels.

ISSUE_3 — ONE-WAY CONTRACT. This rounds strictly OUTWARD (floor the origin, ceil the far
edge, then EvenDown/EvenUp) and that is deliberate: the FFmpeg crop it feeds must fully
COVER the region the user selected, because a crop that lands one pixel short permanently
clips a column of HUD pixels out of the export. `TransformToContentAreaInt`
rounds outward for the same reason.

The consequence is that the two integer wrappers are NOT inverses of each other — composing
them grows the rect by up to 2px per axis. The exact-rational
`TransformToContentArea` / `InverseTransformFromContentArea` pair IS
symmetric; only the integer snapping is lossy.

This is safe today because nothing in the pipeline iterates the composition: the Crop Tools
editor never rehydrates saved config entries into editable items (existing layers are drawn
as read-only placeholders by LoadExistingPlaceholdersAsync), undo/redo stores the SourceRect
verbatim in ItemSnapshot, and the exporter transforms once per render.

DO NOT feed the output of one of these back into the other in a loop, and do not "fix" the
rounding to nearest — that would silently change the crop geometry of every existing saved
profile.

### CoordinateMath.TransformToContentAreaInt (line 376)

SOURCE-video rect -> content-space (1080x1620) rect, as whole pixels.
ISSUE_3: rounds outward via `OutwardRoundRect`. See the one-way contract note on
`InverseTransformFromContentAreaInt` — these two must not be composed in a loop.

### CoordinateMath.BackendSizeStep — RATIOLOCK_01 (line 423)

RATIOLOCK_01 — the smallest step a HUD layer's backend size may move in.

TWO, not thirty-two. See `QuantizeBackendSizeInternal` for why the 32 went and
what replaced the property it was protecting. Two rather than one because ffmpeg's scale
filter feeds a yuv420p chain, whose chroma planes are half-resolution: an odd layer size is
legal but makes the encoder round it anyway, in a place this code cannot see.

### CoordinateMath.QuantizeBackendSizeInternal — RATIOLOCK_01 (line 433)

ISSUE_4 — THE single quantizer for HUD layer size, in BACKEND (1280x1920 internal) pixels.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
RATIOLOCK_01 — WHAT CHANGED AND WHY THE OLD 32-PIXEL GRID HAD TO GO.

This method used to round WIDTH and HEIGHT INDEPENDENTLY to multiples of 32 backend pixels
(= `BackendScale.Num`, since 1280/1080 reduces to 32/27). Two faults came out of that,
and both were reported from the composer as feel problems rather than as maths problems:

  * THE RATIO WAS DESTROYED. Two axes rounded separately do not keep their proportion. A
    300x60 element became 297x54 — a 10% vertical squash — and a 100x40 element became
    81x27, a 33% squash. The user drew a box around a health bar on the frozen frame and
    the composer showed them a different shape. No amount of care with the mouse could fix
    it, because the distortion was applied AFTER the mouse.
  * THE RESIZE STAIRCASED. 32 backend pixels is 27 content pixels, so dragging a corner
    moved the edge in 27-pixel jumps. That is the "jumpy, stuttery, unstable" feel: the box
    does not follow the pointer, it lurches after it.

The 32 existed for one reason, stated in the old comment: a backend size that is a multiple
of 32 converts to `(rw / 32) * 27` content pixels EXACTLY, with no rounding. That is a
nicety, not a requirement — `QuantizeBackendSize` has always passed that
conversion through `ScaleRound`, and MobileFilterBuilder never converts a layer
size back to content space at all: it hands `rw` and `rh` straight to ffmpeg's
scale filter in backend pixels. Giving up exactness costs at most half a content pixel of
display rounding. Keeping it cost the user the shape of every element they cropped.

SO: the WIDTH is rounded to `BackendSizeStep`, and the HEIGHT is then derived
from the width by the EXACT source ratio (contentH : contentW) rather than being rounded on
its own. The ratio is therefore locked by construction, to within one backend pixel, at
every size — which is the property the composer, the preview and the export all needed and
none of them had.

Rounding stays exact rational half-up via `ScaleRound`, NOT
`Math.Round(double)`: 32/27 has no exact binary representation and banker's rounding
would put preview and export half a pixel apart at random sizes.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### CoordinateMath.QuantizeBackendSize (line 489)

Same quantization as `QuantizeBackendSizeInternal`, expressed in UI/content
(1080x1620) pixels. Used by the Crop Tools preview and by HudConfig. The division is exact
because the backend size is always a multiple of 32.

### CoordinateMath.SnapZoomWindow — DRIFT_01 (line 527)

DRIFT_01 — snaps zoom crop window onto the grid FFmpeg actually uses.
Emits a window that is whole and chroma-aligned, preserving the centre exactly.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/CornerMemeOverlayGraph.cs

### CornerMemeInput (line 9)

One corner-overlay meme as the graph needs it.
**InputIndex:** FFmpeg input index of the meme file (images: `-loop 1 -framerate F -t D`).
**StartSec:** Output second (of the stream being overlaid) at which the meme appears.
**EndSec:** Output second at which it disappears (already clipped to the stream).

### CornerMemeOverlayResult (line 27)

Filters plus the labels that carry the overlaid video and the mixed audio.

### CornerMemeOverlayGraph — MEMEMODE_01, MEMELEVEL_02, SPLICE_03 (line 30)

MEMEMODE_01 — FFM-MEMECORNER. Corner-overlay memes over a running stream, for BOTH the Main App
export (`ProcessWorker`) and the Merger's effects clips (`MergeClipGraph`).

**Zero added duration, by construction.** The overlaid stream is the MAIN input of every
`overlay` (`eof_action=pass`) and the FIRST input of the audio `amix`
(`duration=first`), so the output is exactly as long as the input — nothing after the meme
moves. The meme picture is shifted onto the stream's clock (`setpts=PTS+S/TB`) and gated
with `enable='between(t,S,E)'`.

**Geometry** is `MemeOverlayLayout`, the same numbers every preview draws with.
**Sound** (only when `CornerMemeInput.PlaySound` and the file has audio) is matched to
the gameplay (MEMELEVEL_02 gain), de-clicked (SPLICE_03), delayed to S and summed over the game
audio without normalisation.

### CornerMemeOverlayGraph.DuckGain — MEMEDUCK_01, MEMELEVEL_02 (line 47)

MEMEDUCK_01 — the game sound under a corner meme that plays its own sound, as a linear gain
(0.5 ≈ −6 dB). Without it the meme's voice was summed at the SAME loudness as the gameplay
(MEMELEVEL_02 matches them) and the two fought each other.

### CornerMemeOverlayGraph.InputArgs (line 124)

The meme inputs as FFmpeg arguments: images looped for their visible length, videos as-is.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/EncoderManager.cs

### EncoderManager.GetDecodeFlags (line 82)

Per-video-input decoder options, chosen together with the filter graph.
Software effects decode to RAM directly, avoiding GPU download followed by
encoder upload. Resident CUDA graphs share one device across all inputs.

### EncoderManager.DescribeDecoder (line 93)

G09: human-readable name of the chip doing the DECODING for a given encoder.

### EncoderManager.DescribeEncoder (line 100)

G09: human-readable name of the chip doing the ENCODING.

### EncoderManager.AppendHardwareBitrateFlags — DEDUPE_02 (line 112)

DEDUPE_02 — THE ONE COPY OF THE HARDWARE VBV / PEAK-BITRATE ARITHMETIC.

`h264_nvenc`, `h264_amf` and `h264_qsv` each carried a byte-identical three-line block:
clamp the requested rate into [300, MaxBitrateKbps], derive the peak (equal to the average
when the export is size-locked, otherwise 1.5x capped at the same ceiling), and size the VBV
buffer from THE PEAK — never from the average, or a size-locked CBR export overshoots its
target. Three copies meant three places to forget when the ceiling or the 1.5x headroom
changes; there is now one.

⚠️ SILICON-SPECIFIC FLAGS DO NOT BELONG HERE. `-rc`, `-cq`/`-qp_*`/`-global_quality`,
presets, AQ and the `rcLabel` string differ per vendor and stay at their call sites. This
helper emits ONLY `-b:v` / `-maxrate` / `-bufsize`, which is exactly the part that was
identical. Adding a vendor branch in here re-creates the duplication in a worse place.

`VbvBufKbps` was made static for this; it reads no instance state (its only capture is the
`MaxBitrateKbps` const), so the change is behaviour-neutral.

No-ops on a null rate so the guard can stay at the call site and read naturally.

### EncoderManager.GetCodecFlags (line 244)

Returns (codecArgs, rcLabel) — exact port of get_codec_flags().
codecArgs is a list of ffmpeg command-line arguments starting with "-c:v".

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/ExportColorPolicy.cs

### VideoColorInfo — COLOR_01 (line 11)

COLOR_01 — the colour description ffprobe reports for a video stream. Every field is the raw
ffprobe token (e.g. "smpte2084", "bt2020nc", "pc") or null when the file does not say.

### VideoColorInfo.IsHdr (line 19)

PQ (HDR10 / HDR10+ / Dolby Vision base layer) or HLG.

### VideoColorInfo.IsFullRange (line 22)

Full-range ("pc"/JPEG) luma. yuvj* formats are full range by definition.

### VideoColorInfo.IsBt601Matrix (line 27)

An SD-era matrix explicitly declared (would be decoded with the wrong matrix if passed as BT.709).

### ExportColorPolicy — COLOR_01 (line 50)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
COLOR_01 — ONE DELIVERY TARGET: SDR, BT.709, LIMITED (TV) RANGE, AND SAYING SO IN THE FILE.

Before this the export had no colour handling at all. Every route only forced
`-pix_fmt yuv420p`, so:
  • HDR captures (HEVC Main10, BT.2020 + PQ/HLG, which is what NVIDIA/AMD capture produce with
    Windows HDR on) were squeezed to 8-bit with NO tone mapping. The result was grey,
    washed-out footage or a file still flagged as PQ, while the mpv preview (which tone-maps
    automatically) looked right. Preview ≠ export.
  • full-range sources (a common OBS setting) kept full-range levels, which most social
    platforms decode as limited range, so shadows crush and highlights clip;
  • no output was tagged, so each player and platform guessed the matrix.

The policy:
  • ALWAYS tag the output bt709 / bt709 / bt709 / tv (`OutputTagArgs`).
  • SDR BT.709 limited range (the dominant case): no filter is added, so the GPU-resident route
    stays zero-copy.
  • Full range or a BT.601 matrix: one `colorspace` pass converts to BT.709 TV range.
  • HDR: zscale → linear light → BT.709 primaries → Hable tone map → BT.709 TV range, when the
    bundled FFmpeg has zscale. Without zscale no correct CPU tone map exists, so the export
    proceeds and says so (Degraded), rather than failing.
  • Any conversion filter is unknown to ExportVideoPipeline's CUDA substitution table, so an
    HDR or full-range source automatically takes the CPU filter route. Correct colour beats a
    fast wrong export.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### ExportColorPolicy.OutputTagArgs (line 79)

Encoder-side colour description for every delivered file.

### ExportColorPolicy.HdrToneMapChain (line 83)

HDR → SDR BT.709 via zscale (libzimg) + tonemap. Ends in 8-bit 4:2:0 TV range.

### ExportColorPolicy.BuildConversionChain (line 87)

The filter chain (no pad labels) that brings `info` to SDR BT.709 TV range,
or null when the source already is. `degradedReason` is set when a correct
conversion was needed but is not possible with this FFmpeg build.

### ExportColorPolicy.HasFilterAsync (line 131)

True when the FFmpeg at `ffmpegPath` was built with `filter`. Cached per binary.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/ExportEncoderStrategy.cs

### ExportEncoderStrategy (line 6)

THE ONE PLACE THAT DECIDES WHICH CHIP ENCODES (issue 2).

Every application in the suite that exports video routes through this class. Before it existed
the Main App and the Video Merger reached the same decision by DIFFERENT routes:
  * Main App  : Settings override -> boot hardware scan -> EncoderManager
  * Merger    : hardcoded the literal string "GPU", ignoring both the user's Settings choice
                and the boot scan entirely
so the two apps could legitimately pick different encoders on the same machine, and the
documented "encoder selection chain" was only ever true for one of them.

RULE FOR ALL AI AGENTS AND HUMANS: an app must NEVER build its own strategy string. If a new
export surface is added, it calls `Resolve`. If the precedence needs to change, it
changes HERE, once, for everybody.

### ExportEncoderStrategy.Resolve (line 23)

Resolves the strategy string handed to `EncoderManager`.

PRECEDENCE (highest first):
  1. An explicit Settings ▸ Performance choice ("NVIDIA" / "AMD" / "INTEL" / "CPU").
     The user's word beats any probe, always. If the chosen encoder genuinely is not in the
     bundled FFmpeg, EncoderManager surfaces a clear preflight error rather than quietly
     doing something else.
  2. The suite-wide boot scan result published by the Main App
     (`HardwareCapability`), so the Merger and any future export surface reach
     the SAME answer as the Main App without re-running a four-process ffmpeg probe.
  3. `HardwareScanner.ScanFailed` when nothing is known. This is NOT "CPU" —
     EncoderManager treats it as "unknown, re-probe at export time" and recovers to
     hardware if its own `ffmpeg -encoders` probe finds any. Returning "CPU" here is the
     exact bug that made a machine with a working RTX export everything on libx264.

**userOverride:** AppSettings.VideoEncoderOverride. "Auto"/empty means "no opinion".
**bootScanResult:**
The current process's own scan result, or null if it never ran one (the Merger and Crop
Tools never do). Null falls through to the shared cache.

**ffmpegPath:** Used to validate the shared cache against the actual binary.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/ExportTimingTag.cs

### ExportTiming — TIMINGTAG_02 (line 8)

TIMINGTAG_02 — the frame-exact timing an export carries (Video-Merger-Migration.md P1, D11/D12).
Frames are counted at the export's own constant frame rate (`FpsNum`/`FpsDen`).

Layout of an exported file, in frames: `[intro][fade-in ... body ... fade-out]`. The fade-in
starts on the first frame AFTER the intro, and the fade-out ends on the last frame.

A NULL fade means UNKNOWN (not written, e.g. a meme sits at that edge, or an older/merged file).
ZERO means "this file has no such fade". Consumers must treat unknown as "no fade information",
never as a fade.

### ExportTiming.SecToFrames (line 27)

Whole frames for `seconds` at `fps` (nearest).

### ExportTiming.TryParseFps (line 31)

Parses an FFmpeg frame-rate expression ("60", "30000/1001", "59.94").

### ExportTimingTag — TIMINGTAG_02 (line 58)

TIMINGTAG_02 — reads and writes the `fvs_timing` container tag:
`v=2;fps=60/1;intro=6;fadein=30;fadeout=45`. Unknown keys are ignored (forward compatible);
a missing `fadein`/`fadeout` key means unknown. Parsing is culture-invariant and never throws.

### ExportTimingTag.MaxIntroSec (line 67)

Largest intro accepted, in seconds (mirrors `IntroTag.MaxPlausibleIntroSec`).

### ExportTimingTag.MaxFadeSec (line 70)

Largest single fade accepted, in seconds. Anything longer is treated as corrupt.

### ExportTimingTag.TryParse (line 83)

Parses a tag value. False when absent, not v2, or implausible for a file of `fileDurationSec`.

### ExportTimingTag.Read (line 132)

Reads timing from ffprobe's `format.tags`: the v2 key when present and valid, else the
v1 `IntroTag.Key` (seconds; fps unknown → returned as frames at 1000/1 "millisecond
frames", fades unknown). Null when the file carries neither.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/ExportVideoPipeline.cs

### ExportVideoPipeline (line 7)

Plans video memory and filtering together. A hardware encoder alone does not
imply GPU filtering. Unknown effects retain the original software graph.
Audio filters always remain on the CPU and do not download video frames.

### ExportVideoPipeline.Create — CUDATAG_01 (line 42)

CUDATAG_01 — FFmpeg 7.1+ negotiates colour space on every filter link. The encoder-side
output tags (`ExportColorPolicy.OutputTagArgs`, passed here as
`codecFlags`) become a buffersink constraint, and untagged CUDA frames
cannot meet it: FFmpeg inserts a software auto_scale after the last scale_cuda and the
graph fails ("Impossible to convert between the formats supported by the filter
'Parsed_scale_cuda_N' and the filter 'auto_scale_0'"). The CPU route gets the same
relabel for free from swscale. The resident route therefore stamps the identical
metadata on each decoded input with `setparams` (a metadata-only filter that
accepts hardware frames), so every link agrees and frames never leave VRAM.

### ExportVideoPipeline.ColourTagFilter — CUDATAG_01 (line 90)

CUDATAG_01 — the setparams equivalent of the encoder's colour flags, or null when there are none.

### ExportVideoPipeline.TagVideoInputs — CUDATAG_01 (line 106)

CUDATAG_01 — every decoded video input pad (`[N:v]`, `[N:v:M]`) is routed through
one setparams chain before its first consumer. Labels are only rewritten outside quotes.

Conservatively identifies the root cause of an FFmpeg export failure.
