and returns an honest Unknown category when evidence is insufficient.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/FfmpegJobLifetime.cs

### FfmpegJobLifetime — PIPELIFE_01, PIPEDEDUP_01, FFMPEGSTOP_01, PIPELIFE_02, PROCGATE_01 (line 8)

PIPELIFE_01 — THE ONE COPY OF "AN FFMPEG JOB'S LIFETIME".

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
WHAT THIS ENDS. `ProcessWorker` (172KB) and `MergerWorker` (69KB) are two independent
FFmpeg pipelines. Their filtergraph construction is genuinely different and stays separate —
that is not duplication, it is two different jobs. What WAS duplicated is the part that has
nothing to do with filtergraphs: owning the child process reference, the cancel flag, the
single-flight finish notification, and the teardown ladder.

That shared part had already drifted once, and the repository carries the receipts:
`PIPEDEDUP_01` notes that the twin `ReadExitCodeSafely` pair "silently diverged
between these two files", and `FFMPEGSTOP_01` notes that `ProcessWorker` "was a bare
WaitForExit -> Kill(tree) -> WaitForExit while the sibling copy in MergerWorker had
already been hardened". Each time, one twin was fixed and the other was not.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

**⚠️ PIPELIFE_02 — AND IT HAD DRIFTED AGAIN, IN THE SAME DIRECTION.**
`PROCGATE_01` fixed a use-after-dispose race in `ProcessWorker`: the process field was
read twice — once to null-test, once to act on — so the export thread could run
`_currentProcess = null; proc.Dispose();` in between, and the teardown then called
`Kill()` on a disposed `Process`. The fix was a lock-guarded gate with an
atomic take.

`MergerWorker` never received it. At the time of writing its `_currentProcess` is a
plain unsynchronised field, its `Cancel()` passes that field straight to the shutdown
ladder, and its `Dispose()` does exactly the two-read null-test-then-use the fix was
written to eliminate. Routing both through this type closes it in the Merger and makes a third
divergence impossible, because there is no longer a second copy to diverge.

**THREADING.** Every member is safe from any thread. `Cancel` is called from the
UI thread, cancellation-token registrations run on whichever thread cancelled, and the pipeline
itself publishes from a worker — all three touch the process slot, which is the entire reason
it is gated rather than volatile. A volatile field would give a consistent READ but still allow
two callers to both believe they own the disposal.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.FfmpegJobLifetime (line 61)

**logTag:** The `CoreLogger` subsystem tag: "Process" or "Merger".
**processLabel:**
How the child is named in shutdown logs: "FFmpeg" or "FFmpeg MERGE". Preserved verbatim
from each pipeline, because the crash digest and the user's log are read by these strings.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.WasCanceled (line 72)

True when this job ended because the user stopped it, not because it failed.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.MarkCanceled (line 75)

Marks the job cancelled WITHOUT touching the process. For cancellation-token registrations,
which only need the flag — the ladder is started by `Cancel`.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.SetCurrentProcess — PROCGATE_01 (line 81)

PROCGATE_01 — publish the live child process. Pipeline thread only.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.TakeCurrentProcess — PROCGATE_01 (line 87)

PROCGATE_01 — claim the live child process AND clear the slot in one atomic step, so exactly
one caller can ever be responsible for disposing it. Every teardown site uses this instead
of the old `_currentProcess = null; proc.Dispose();` pair.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.PeekCurrentProcess — PROCGATE_01 (line 97)

PROCGATE_01 — one consistent read, for callers that only observe.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.BeginCooperativeShutdown — FFMPEGSTOP_01 (line 103)

FFMPEGSTOP_01 — starts the bounded cooperative shutdown ladder exactly once, off the calling
thread. Single-flight: `Cancel`, token registrations and `DisposeJob`
may all race, but only one ladder runs per process.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.AwaitActiveShutdownAsync (line 111)

Awaits the in-flight shutdown ladder, if any. Bounded by the ladder itself.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.Cancel (line 114)

The user pressed Cancel. Returns immediately — the ladder runs off-thread — so a cancel can
never hang the UI, but FFmpeg still gets the chance to finalize its container instead of
being shot mid-write.

**idleMessage:** Logged when no encode was actually running.
**stoppingMessage:** Logged when a child process is being stopped.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.DisposeJob — ISSUE_11 (line 139)

ISSUE_11 — disposing the worker also STOPS the encoder.

Callers are expected to call `Cancel` first, but nothing enforces it, so any
path that disposed a worker without cancelling left FFmpeg grinding at full CPU on a render
whose progress window had already gone — the machine stayed hot and loud for a file nobody
would ever receive. Killing the tree here makes teardown self-sufficient.

⚠️ SYNCHRONOUS on purpose, matching both pipelines' previous behaviour: this returns only
once the tree is dead or the bounded confirmation window has elapsed. Worst case it blocks
for `CooperativeGraceMs + HardKillConfirmMs`, the same ceiling every other teardown in
this solution accepts, and the ChildProcessTracker job object remains the final safeguard.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.EmitFinished — ISSUE_04, CANCELREG_01 (line 176)

ISSUE_04 — fires the caller's completion notification exactly once.

Single-flight matters more than it looks: `CANCELREG_01` records a hang in which
`EmitFinished` never fired at all, leaving the controller's TaskCompletionSource
uncompleted and the caller's await pending forever — overlay gone, PROCESS button dead, no
error on screen. The failure modes at both ends of this call are unrecoverable from the UI,
so it is owned in one place.

### FfmpegJobLifetime.FinishEmitted (line 194)

True once the completion notification has been delivered.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/FilterGraphBuilder.cs

### PadFilterNode.Width (line 23)

ABSOLUTE output canvas width in pixels (not a delta — see the class remarks).

### PadFilterNode.Height (line 25)

ABSOLUTE output canvas height in pixels (not a delta — see the class remarks).

### SidechainCompressNode — DUCKMB_01 (line 58)

DUCKMB_01 — one sidechain compressor stage of the music bed's multiband protection
(AudioFilterChain). Two tunings: `Duck` (the bed steps back while the gameplay is loud)
and `Carve` (an extra, gentler dip of the speech band only).
Measured with a near-full-scale trigger: the old 0.15 / 1.13 tuning reduced the music by 1.2 dB
(inaudible); threshold 0.1 / ratio 4 reduces it by about 8 dB.

### SidechainCompressNode.BypassThreshold (line 78)

Legacy only: how "ducking off" was encoded before the explicit ducking_enabled flag.

### SidechainCompressNode.DefaultStrength — DUCKSTRENGTH_01 (line 88)

DUCKSTRENGTH_01 — the user's strength handle (Settings › Sound & Music), 0-100, middle = the
tuned value above. Only the RATIO moves, and it moves smoothly (exponential in the handle, no
steps): ratio = 1 + (tuned - 1) · 2^((handle - 50) / 50 · `StrengthSpanOctaves`).
Every notch changes the effect by the same small proportion, so there is never a jump; the
threshold, attack and release stay tuned. Ducking: 2.1 (gentlest) · 4 (middle) · 9.5 (strongest).
Carving: 1.5 · 2.5 · 5.2.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/FramePtsProbe.cs

### FramePtsProbe — FRAMESNAP_01 (line 12)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
FRAMESNAP_01 — CUTS LAND ON REAL FRAME TIMESTAMPS (Video-Merger-Migration.md P2.2).

"Skip the first 6 frames" is only exact when the cut is the presentation time of frame #6 as
the file actually stores it. Seconds computed from a nominal rate drift on 59.94 fps and are
simply wrong on variable-frame-rate captures. So the analyzer reads the first frames' pts once,
in the background, and the cut is that pts.

CLOCK: pts are returned in µs relative to the container's `format.start_time`. That is the
clock ffmpeg's filters see by default (it offsets every input to start at 0), so a trim at this
value hits exactly that frame.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### FramePtsProbe.PacketMargin (line 28)

Extra packets read beyond the frames wanted: decoder delay (B-frames) can hold some back.

### FramePtsProbe.ProbeAsync (line 31)

Presentation timestamps (µs, sorted, relative to the container start) of at least the first
`frames` video frames. Empty on any failure. Never throws.

### FramePtsProbe.Parse (line 74)

Parses ffprobe's JSON (see `ProbeAsync`). Empty on garbage. Never throws.

### FramePtsProbe.IntroCutUs (line 97)

The cut that removes exactly `introFrames` frames: the pts of the first kept
frame (index `introFrames`). Null when there are not enough frames.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/FreezeSoundEffect.cs

### FreezeSoundEffect — FREEZESFX_01 (line 5)

FREEZESFX_01 (docs/02 AUD-FREEZESFX) — the movie-style "slow motion" sound of a freeze, and the
music dip that makes room for it.

**The sound.** A held frame used to be pure silence (`anullsrc`). It is now a deep,
falling bass hit — the "car-crash slow-motion" boom of film trailers — synthesised by FFmpeg itself
(`aevalsrc`), so there is no sound file to ship, license or lose: a sub-bass sine that glides
from `StartHz` down to `EndHz` over the whole hold, with a short noise
"impact" at the start, a slow decay that keeps a low rumble under the entire freeze, and a soft
fade at its end. A low-pass keeps it a felt boom rather than a beep. It replaces the silence in
the ONE graph compiler (`GranularSpeedBuilder`), so every path that renders a freeze —
export, the rendered preview mix, the voice-over picture — carries the same sound.

**The music dip.** While a freeze plays, the music glides down to
`1 - ``DipDepth` of its level (starting `DipLeadSec` before the hold,
over `DipInSec`), stays there for the hold, and fades back to normal over
`DipOutSec` after it, so the boom stands out and the music returns gently.

### FreezeSoundEffect.StartHz (line 25)

Starting pitch of the falling bass, in Hz.

### FreezeSoundEffect.EndHz (line 28)

Pitch the bass has fallen to at the end of the hold, in Hz.

### FreezeSoundEffect.BassLevel (line 31)

Peak level of the bass (linear, before the video volume fader and the safety limiter).

### FreezeSoundEffect.ImpactLevel (line 34)

Level of the short noise "impact" at the start of the hold.

### FreezeSoundEffect.DipDepth (line 37)

Fraction of the music level REMOVED while a freeze plays (0.65 = music at 35%).

### FreezeSoundEffect.DipLeadSec (line 40)

The music starts dipping this long before the hold begins.

### FreezeSoundEffect.DipInSec (line 43)

How long the music takes to glide down.

### FreezeSoundEffect.DipOutSec (line 46)

How long the music takes to come back after the hold.

### FreezeSoundEffect.Expression (line 51)

The FFmpeg expression of the sound, sample by sample, for a hold of `durationSec`
(t = seconds since the hold began). Public for tests.

### FreezeSoundEffect.Source (line 66)

One filtergraph chain producing the sound as a 48 kHz stereo stream of exactly
`durationSec` seconds, labelled `outLabel` (e.g. "[a_c3]").
Drop-in replacement for the `anullsrc` a freeze used to produce.

### FreezeSoundEffect.MusicDipPulse (line 79)

An FFmpeg expression in t (seconds on the MUSIC's clock) that is 1 while a freeze plays, ramps in
before it and out after it, and 0 elsewhere. Null when there is no freeze.

### FreezeSoundEffect.Render — FREEZESFX_02 (line 95)

FREEZESFX_02 — the SAME sound rendered in-process (mono float samples, -1..1) for previews that do
not go through FFmpeg (the Speed Editor plays a freeze by pausing its player). Same formula as
`Expression` and the same 2-pole Butterworth low-pass (RBJ biquad, Q = 0.7071, the
FFmpeg `lowpass` filter's default); only the noise impact's random sequence differs.

### FreezeSoundEffect.MusicDipFilter (line 131)

The volume filter that applies `pulse` to the music bed.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/GranularSpeedBuilder.cs

### CutRange (line 7)

A speed or freeze segment for the granular timeline.

CUT_01 — a stretch of footage the user deleted, in ABSOLUTE SOURCE MILLISECONDS.

⚠️ TWO FRAMES OF REFERENCE, ON PURPOSE. This mirrors `SpeedSegment`: the UI and the
saved session speak absolute source milliseconds, because that is what trim handles and speed
segments already use and what survives a trim change. The Core timeline model speaks
CLIP-RELATIVE seconds (`OutputTimeline.Cut`), because that is what
`OutputTimeline.Insertion` uses. `ToClipRelative` is the ONE conversion
between them — do not hand a CutRange straight to OutputTimeline, and do not "simplify" either
type into the other's units.

### CutRange.ToClipRelative (line 23)

Converts to the Core timeline's clip-relative seconds, given the export's trim-in point.
Clamping is left to `OutputTimeline.NormalizeCuts`, which owns every rule about
ordering, merging and minimum length.

### CutRange.ToClipRelative (line 31)

Convenience for the common "convert a whole list" call at an export boundary.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.CreateTimeMapper — TIME_01 (line 55)

TIME_01 - ABSOLUTE source seconds to FINISHED-VIDEO seconds.

The mathematics no longer lives here. It moved to `OutputTimeline`, which is the
only place in the application that knows how speed changes and freezes stretch time. This
method survives purely as an adapter: six callers depend on its exact signature and on being
handed a plain `Func{T, TResult}`.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.HighChunkCountWarnThreshold — ISSUE_08 (line 76)

ISSUE_08 — chunk count above which the graph's parallel branches are likely to buffer an
uncomfortable number of uncompressed frames in RAM (see `Build` remarks).
Exposed so the UI can warn BEFORE the render starts rather than after the machine swaps.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.SpliceFadeSec — SPLICE_01 (line 83)

SPLICE_01 — length of the de-click fade applied at each end of every concatenated audio
chunk, in seconds. 8 ms is under half a frame at 60fps: long enough to turn a step
discontinuity into a ramp the ear reads as continuous, short enough that no one perceives a
gap. Raising this into the tens of milliseconds starts to be audible as a stutter at every
speed change; lowering it below ~3 ms stops suppressing the click.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.BuildSpliceFade — SPLICE_01 (line 92)

SPLICE_01 — the fade pair for one audio chunk, or an empty string when the chunk is too
short to carry one. A chunk shorter than twice the fade would have its fade-in and fade-out
overlap, which attenuates the middle and audibly ducks very short chunks — so those are
left alone; they are far too brief for a click to register anyway.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.ZoomRampSeconds (line 114)

How long the glide lasts, in seconds, on a side that has room for it.

FIXED length, deliberately. It used to be "up to 1 second, or whatever is available if
that is less" — which meant the same Slow setting produced a 1.0s glide in open footage
and a 0.6s glide next to a neighbour, with no way for the user to know which they got.
A full second also simply read as too slow for gameplay clips. It is now always exactly
this value, or nothing at all.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.ZoomRampMinAvailableSeconds (line 125)

Minimum free footage a side must have before it gets a glide at all.

Equal to `ZoomRampSeconds` by construction: a side either has room for the
whole ramp or it gets none. There is no partial ramp any more.
A side with less than this snaps instantly — see the Slow-zoom notes in
docs/README.md (formerly project_structure.txt) Section 9.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.ZoomRampRequiredGapBetweenSlowZooms (line 135)

OPTION A — free footage required between TWO ADJACENT **SLOW** ZOOMS before either of them
is granted a ramp on the side that faces the other. Twice the ramp length, because BOTH
zooms want to borrow from that one gap: the earlier zoom needs 0.5s after it to glide out,
the later zoom needs 0.5s before it to glide in.

THE BUG THIS FIXES: the two borrow windows used to be allowed to overlap. The chunk builder
takes the FIRST matching zoom phase and stops looking, so the earlier zoom's glide-out won
the whole contested stretch and the later zoom's glide-in was partly or entirely discarded.
Measured results before the fix, with the zooms 0.75s apart: the picture glided out to
normal, then JUMPED straight to half zoom, then glided the rest of the way in — a visible
hop that looked like a rendering fault. At 0.5s apart the later zoom's glide vanished
completely and it hard-cut in, despite the user having chosen Slow.

The rule is now symmetric and all-or-nothing: if two Slow zooms are closer than this,
NEITHER gets a ramp on the facing side and both snap. Predictable beats clever here — a
clean cut reads as deliberate, a half-zoom hop reads as broken.

⚠️ ONLY APPLIES WHEN BOTH NEIGHBOURS ARE SLOW. An Instant zoom borrows nothing, so it never
contests the gap and the ordinary `ZoomRampMinAvailableSeconds` applies against
it. Same for the clip start and clip end. Do not widen this to all neighbours — that would
deny ramps that are perfectly safe.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.SlowZoomGlideAvailability — SLOWZOOMGLIDE_01 (line 160)

SLOWZOOMGLIDE_01 — Per-side glide availability evaluation for a slow zoom.

Evaluates whether the potential 0.5s incoming and outgoing ramps fit the source footage.
Uses exact renderer ordering and nearest valid neighbour on each side:
- Clip boundaries or Instant zoom neighbour: requires ZoomRampMinAvailableSeconds (0.5s)
- Adjacent Slow zoom neighbour: requires ZoomRampRequiredGapBetweenSlowZooms (1.0s)

### GranularSpeedBuilder.GetSlowZoomGlideAvailability — SLOWZOOMGLIDE_01 (line 228)

SLOWZOOMGLIDE_01 — Evaluates per-side glide availability for the zoom at `segIndex`.
Uses exact renderer ordering and nearest valid neighbour on each side.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.EvenDim — ISSUE_02 (line 321)

ISSUE_02 — filter dimensions must be positive even integers.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.ZoomPadMargin (line 329)

G05 — minimal safe black margin (per side) for a digital-zoom pan, in source pixels.

WHY A MARGIN EXISTS AT ALL: the zoom is `pad -> crop`. The crop window follows the zoom
box's centre, and that centre can sit anywhere from 0 to the frame edge — so the window
half-hangs off the frame at the extremes. Without a black border FFmpeg throws an
out-of-bounds `crop` error and the whole export dies (changelog item (10)).

WHY THIS SIZE: for a FIXED crop window of width `cropExtent`, the furthest it can ever
hang off either edge is exactly `cropExtent / 2`. Anything beyond that is black pixels
that are allocated, cleared and then never sampled. The old code hardcoded HALF THE FULL
FRAME regardless of zoom level, which — combined with the `pad=iw+` double-count bug in
PadFilterNode — produced a 7680x4320 working frame for a 2560x1440 source.

The +2px and the even-alignment are float-rounding insurance; `crop` is unforgiving and
two wasted pixels cost nothing.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.Build — ISSUE_08 (line 354)

**needHudBranch:**
ISSUE_08 — whether the caller will actually CONSUME the HUD output label.

The graph fans the decoded stream out with a `split` and gives every chunk its own
branch. Because `concat` reads branch 1 to completion before touching branch 2, frames
pushed down the not-yet-read branches sit in FFmpeg's link queues in RAM — so peak memory
scales with the NUMBER OF BRANCHES.

This flag halves that. The HUD copy only exists for the portrait/mobile cut-out overlay;
in landscape the caller used to feed it straight into `nullsink`, which meant FFmpeg
still allocated, filled and buffered an entire second set of branches purely to throw
them away. Pass false and no HUD split/trim/concat is emitted at all: `split=n` instead
of `split=2n`.

When false the returned `hudLabel` is an empty string — callers must check it.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.BuildConstantZoomFilter — ISSUE_02 (line 871)

ISSUE_02 — builds the zoom filter for a chunk whose ramp progress does NOT change across
it. That covers the entire held body of every slow zoom (emitted with ProgStart =
ProgEnd = 1.0) and every freeze frame.

It computes the visible source region directly and scales THAT to the output, instead of
magnifying the whole padded canvas and cropping a window out of the result. Both express
the identical region — this was confirmed by rendering the old and new expressions through
FFmpeg and comparing the framing, which matched exactly at p = 1.0 — but this form never
materialises the giant intermediate frame, and resamples the picture once rather than
twice, so it is marginally sharper as well.

The padding is kept at +-resW/-+resH (the value the audited instant-zoom path uses) so the
coordinate chain documented in docs/README.md (formerly project_structure.txt) is unchanged; with no per-frame scale
in this branch the padding costs nothing meaningful.

### GranularSpeedBuilder.BuildAtempoChain — ISSUE_04, AVSYNC_01, TEMPO_01 (line 929)

ISSUE_04 — the atempo-only chain, kept as a forwarder for the gates that check atempo's
[0.5, 2.0] element bounds. The guard against impossible rates and the AVSYNC_01 empty chain
at 1.0x live in `AudioTempoFilterBuilder`; export routes call
`AudioTempoFilterBuilder.Build(double)` (TEMPO_01), never this.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HardwareCapability.cs

### HardwareCapability — ISSUE_09 (line 9)

SUITE-WIDE HARDWARE CAPABILITY CACHE (issue 2).

── THE PROBLEM THIS SOLVES ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The suite is THREE separate processes (Main App, Video Merger, Crop Tools — Section 2). Each
one used to work out the machine's capabilities from scratch:
  * `VideoRenderMode.Initialize()` ran the D3D11/DXGI GPU probe in EVERY process, and
  * the Main App ran `HardwareScanner.ScanAsync` (which spawns ffmpeg.exe up to FOUR times:
    one `-hwaccels` listing plus one real trial encode per vendor) while the Merger ignored it
    entirely and hardcoded the strategy string "GPU".
So the same questions were answered repeatedly, slowly, and — worse — INCONSISTENTLY: the
Merger could pick a different encoder than the Main App on the very same machine, and neither
could see the other's result.

── THE CONTRACT ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The Main App answers both questions ONCE at boot and publishes the answers here. Every other
process in the suite READS them instead of re-probing. There is now exactly ONE code path for
"which chip previews video" and exactly ONE for "which chip encodes video", shared by all three
applications. Do not reintroduce a per-app probe or a per-app default.

── WHY IT IS SAFE TO CACHE, AND WHEN IT IS NOT ──────────────────────────────────────────────
The two halves are validated INDEPENDENTLY because they go stale for different reasons:

  ENCODER half (which ffmpeg encoder works) is invalidated by:
    * a different ffmpeg.exe — validated by a path + byte-length + last-write fingerprint, so
      an app update or a swapped binary re-probes automatically;
    * age (`MaxAgeDays`), which covers a GPU driver install;
    * a recorded `HardwareScanner.ScanFailed` — a failure is NEVER cached, because
      caching "we could not tell" would freeze a broken state across the whole suite.

  RENDER half (whether the zero-copy preview bridge works) is invalidated by:
    * a different Windows SESSION — this is the RDP case and it matters. Windows blocks GPU
      access inside a remote session, so a result recorded in a local session is actively wrong
      inside an RDP session (and vice-versa). Session id + the remote-session flag are both
      part of the key, so switching between them always re-probes.

── FAIL-SAFE ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Every read path returns null on ANY problem (missing file, malformed JSON, unreadable ffmpeg,
exception) and every write path is silently non-throwing. A cache that cannot be read simply
means the caller probes for itself — exactly the old behaviour. This cache can never make the
suite worse than not having it.

── STORAGE ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Goes through `UiStateStore` (ProgramData\uistate), per ISSUE_09: "NEW SMALL STATE
FILES BELONG THERE — do not create another root." Serialised with JsonObject rather than
reflection-based JsonSerializer because the app is NativeAOT.

### HardwareCapability.SchemaVersion (line 60)

Bump when a field changes meaning. A mismatch discards the record entirely.

### HardwareCapability.MaxAgeDays (line 63)

A recorded capability older than this is re-probed. Long enough that the suite is not
re-scanning constantly, short enough that a GPU driver install is picked up on its own.

### HardwareCapability.EncoderCapability (line 69)

The published answer to "which chip encodes video".
**EncoderMode:** "NVIDIA" | "AMD" | "INTEL" | "CPU". Never `HardwareScanner.ScanFailed`.

### HardwareCapability.RenderCapability (line 73)

The published answer to "which path previews video".

### HardwareCapability.TryLoadEncoder (line 78)

Returns the suite-wide encoder answer, or null when the caller must probe for itself.

⚠️ Whole body wrapped — same boot-path reasoning as `TryLoadRenderMode`.

### HardwareCapability.TryLoadRenderMode (line 118)

Returns the suite-wide preview-path answer, or null when the caller must probe for itself.

⚠️ WHOLE BODY WRAPPED. This runs on the BOOT PATH via `VideoRenderMode.Initialize()`,
before any window exists, so an exception here does not surface as a degraded feature — it
kills the app on launch. `JsonNode.GetValue<bool>()` THROWS when the stored value
is not actually a bool (a hand-edited or half-written file is enough), so the null-coalesce
on its own was not protection. Nothing in this class may throw at a caller.

### HardwareCapability.TryReadBool (line 160)

Reads a bool without trusting the stored type. `GetValue<bool>()` throws on a
type mismatch rather than returning null, so `?? false` does not save you.

### HardwareCapability.TryReadUtc (line 233)

Reads a round-trip ("O") timestamp.

⚠️ `DateTimeStyles.RoundtripKind` MUST BE USED ALONE. Combining it with
`AdjustToUniversal` (or AssumeLocal/AssumeUniversal) makes DateTime.TryParse throw
ArgumentException on EVERY call, whatever the input — it is an invalid-argument throw, not
a parse failure, so `TryParse` does not swallow it. That combination shipped here briefly
and killed the app at startup, because this runs on the boot path via
VideoRenderMode.Initialize(). RoundtripKind alone is correct anyway: values are written
with `DateTime.UtcNow.ToString("O")`, which ends in "Z", so the parsed value already comes
back with Kind=Utc and needs no adjustment.

Wrapped defensively as well — nothing on the boot path may throw out of this class.

### HardwareCapability.FingerprintFfmpeg (line 264)

Identifies the exact ffmpeg binary the recorded encoder answer was measured against.
Path + byte length + last-write time is enough to notice an app update or a swapped build,
and is far cheaper than hashing a ~400 KB executable on every process start.
Returns "" when the file cannot be read, which invalidates the cache by design.

### HardwareCapability.CurrentSessionKey (line 286)

Session identity for the RDP guard. `SESSIONNAME` starting with "RDP-" is Windows' own
marker for a remote session and is what the Section 10 RDP guardrail keys off too.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HardwareScanner.cs

### HardwareScanner.ScanFailed (line 9)

G03: returned when the scan could NOT complete (exception / timeout) as opposed to
completing and legitimately finding no hardware encoder. These two outcomes used to
collapse into the same "CPU" string, which made EncoderManager set ForcedCpu=true and
discard its own successful encoder detection — the exact reason a machine with a working
RTX exported every video on libx264. NEVER merge this back into "CPU".

### HardwareScanner.ScanSharedAsync (line 28)

Suite-wide entry point (issue 2). Returns the shared result published by whichever app
scanned first, and only pays for a real scan when there is nothing valid to reuse.

A full `ScanAsync` spawns ffmpeg.exe up to FOUR times (one `-hwaccels` listing
plus a real trial encode per vendor). Doing that in every process of a three-process suite
was pure waste AND allowed two apps to disagree about the same machine. Every caller should
use THIS method; call `ScanAsync` directly only when a forced re-probe is
genuinely wanted.

A successful result is published for the other apps. A failure is deliberately NOT
published — see `HardwareCapability`.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HudAutoDetector.cs

### HudAutoDetector — MAGICWAND_02, MAGICWAND_01 (line 13)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
MAGICWAND_02 — AUTOMATIC HUD DETECTION. THE C# PORT OF THE OLD PYTHON TOOL'S magic_wand.py.

WHAT THE BUTTON USED TO DO, AND WHY IT WAS HIDDEN.
`CropToolWindow.ShowMagicWandCandidates` drew six rectangles at hardcoded fractions of the
frame — 0.65, 0.02, 0.32, 0.28 and five more like it — and presented them as detections. On any
capture whose HUD did not happen to sit at those fractions it was confidently, silently wrong,
which is worse than absent, so MAGICWAND_01 hid the button rather than delete a handler someone
might one day back with a real analyser. This is that analyser.

WHAT IT ACTUALLY DOES, in the order it does it:
  1. Samples frames uniformly across the WHOLE clip (not the single frozen frame — see below).
  2. Takes the per-pixel TEMPORAL MEDIAN. Gameplay moves; the HUD does not. The median of sixty
     frames spread over a match is a picture of the parts of the screen that never change.
  3. Takes the per-pixel TEMPORAL STANDARD DEVIATION and inverts it into a stability mask, so
     "this pixel never changed" becomes a first-class, scoreable signal.
  4. Builds three more masks off the median: an adaptive threshold (dark HUD plates on bright
     gameplay), Canny edges (panel borders), and an HSV colour-anchor mask tuned to the four
     colour families a Fortnite HUD actually uses — health green, shield blue, loot amber,
     rarity purple/pink.
  5. For each of the five known HUD roles, looks ONLY inside that role's zone of the screen,
     finds connected blobs, filters them by size and aspect, and scores the survivors on eight
     weighted terms. Non-maximum suppression keeps the best one or two per role.
  6. Falls back to a generic detector if fewer than three roles were found, and to a Hough
     circle hunt if the minimap specifically was missed.

WHY IT ANALYSES THE CLIP AND NOT THE SNAPSHOT. The snapshot is one frame. One frame cannot tell
a HUD panel from a wall, because both are just pixels; it is the fact that the panel is STILL
THERE sixty frames later that identifies it. The old Python tool took the snapshot path as an
argument too and then ignored it for exactly this reason (`extract_all(snapshot_path, ...)`
reads `params["input_file"]`), and so does this. The snapshot's DIMENSIONS are what matter,
and those come in as `sourceWidth`/`sourceHeight`.

WHY THERE IS NO OpenCV. Invariant 1 in docs/README.md is the Single Binary Executable Mandate.
The primitives OpenCV supplied — adaptive threshold, morphology, contours, Canny, Hough — are
implemented in `HudImageOps`, which documents each one against the call it replaces
and every place the two differ.

COORDINATE SPACES. There are two, and mixing them is the classic way to break this file:
  • ANALYSIS space: every mask, blob and pixel index, 540 rows tall by construction.
  • SOURCE space: what the caller gets back, in the snapshot's own pixels.
`ScaledToSourceRect` is the ONLY crossing point. Nothing else may convert.

THREADING. Pure computation with no UI types; call it from a background task. It honours the
cancellation token between every stage and inside the frame reader.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### HudAutoDetector.DetectionRect (line 63)

One thing the detector found: a rectangle in the caller's SOURCE pixel space, plus the HUD
role it was found by.

`RoleKey` is one of `HudConfig.HudKeys`, or null when the rectangle
came from the generic or circle fallback and the detector genuinely does not know what it
is looking at. The Python original threw this away — `extract_all` returned bare
QRects and the UI made the user label every one. Keeping it costs nothing and lets the Crop
Tool pre-select the right role when the user clicks a box, so a confident detection becomes
one click instead of two.

### HudAutoDetector.DetectionProgress — WANDPROGRESS_01 (line 76)

WANDPROGRESS_01 — one progress beat, for a caller that wants to show its user what is
happening.

WHY THIS EXISTS. Detection takes anywhere from three seconds to the full
`MaxSeconds` ceiling, and for every one of those seconds the only thing the
screen said was that a button had gone grey. A user cannot tell a long job from a hung one
without being told, so they sit and wonder whether the feature is working or broken — and
the honest answer is that both look identical from outside.

`Stage` is written for the person watching, not for the log: it says what
the machine is looking at in words that mean something to someone who has never heard of a
temporal median. `Percent` is 0-100 and monotonic — it never goes
backwards, because a bar that retreats reads as a fault.

### HudAutoDetector.AnalysisHeight (line 93)

Analysis height, and therefore the whole pipeline's working resolution. 540 is the
Python original's figure and it is load-bearing: every pixel threshold, kernel size and
size range in `RoleSpecs` is calibrated against it. Changing it silently
re-tunes every one of them.

### HudAutoDetector.MaxSeconds (line 99)

Hard ceiling on the whole run. The old tool had the same idea
(`UI_BEHAVIOR.MAGIC_WAND_MAX_SECONDS`): a wand that never comes back is a hang, and the
user is staring at a disabled button while it thinks.

### HudAutoDetector.KeyframeOnlyThresholdSeconds (line 104)

Above this clip length the sampler switches to keyframes only. See
`SampleFramesAsync` for why.

### HudAutoDetector.MaxKeyframesRead (line 108)

Upper bound on frames pulled in keyframe mode before subsampling. A two-hour
capture has thousands; we only ever want sixty, and each one costs ~1.5 MB of RAM.

### HudAutoDetector.RoleSpec (line 113)

Everything the detector knows about where one HUD role lives and what it looks like. Ported
value-for-value from `magic_wand.py :: _get_role_specs` — these numbers are the result
of the original tool's tuning against real captures and are NOT to be "tidied".

**RoleKey:** The HudConfig key. The Python keyed these by DISPLAY name
("Mini Map + Stats"); this build keys by the stable config key and lets the Crop Tool supply
the display name, so a renamed label can never break detection.
**Zone:** Search window as fractions of the frame: x1, y1, x2, y2.
**MinW:** Minimum acceptable width in SOURCE pixels.
**MaxW:** Maximum acceptable width in SOURCE pixels.
**MinH:** Minimum acceptable height in SOURCE pixels.
**MaxH:** Maximum acceptable height in SOURCE pixels.
**MinAspect:** Minimum width/height.
**MaxAspect:** Maximum width/height.
**IdealAspect:** The shape this role usually is; deviation costs score.
**IdealCx:** Where this role usually sits, as a fraction of frame width.
**IdealCy:** Same, as a fraction of frame height.
**Pad:** Fixed padding added around a blob, in analysis pixels.
**MaxKeep:** How many candidates NMS may keep for this role.
**ExpandL:** Extra left padding as a fraction of the blob's own width.
**ExpandT:** Extra top padding as a fraction of the blob's own height.
**ExpandR:** Extra right padding as a fraction of the blob's own width.
**ExpandB:** Extra bottom padding as a fraction of the blob's own height.
**MinAnchorRatio:** Reject below this much HUD-coloured pixel coverage.
**MinStabilityRatio:** Reject below this much never-changed coverage.
**MinPosScore:** Reject below this much agreement with IdealCx/IdealCy.
**SecondaryMinRatio:** A second candidate is kept only if it scores at least this
fraction of the winner. Used for the roles that legitimately appear twice.

### HudAutoDetector.RoleSpecs (line 153)

The five roles, in the order the Python's `role_order` listed them. Order matters twice:
it is the order primary picks are appended in, and it is the order the caller sees, so the
first box the user is offered is the minimap rather than whatever happened to sort first.

### HudAutoDetector.DetectAsync — WANDPROGRESS_01 (line 213)

Runs the whole pipeline and returns what it found, best-first within each role and roles in
`RoleSpecs` order.

Returns an EMPTY list rather than throwing when the clip cannot be sampled or nothing scores
well enough — "found nothing" is a normal outcome the caller must handle anyway, and turning
it into an exception would make the ordinary case look like a failure in the logs.

**ffmpegPath:** Resolved ffmpeg executable.
**videoPath:** The clip to analyse.
**sourceWidth:** Snapshot width. Results come back in this space.
**sourceHeight:** Snapshot height. Results come back in this space.
**totalMs:** Clip length, if known; 0 to have it probed.
**progress:** WANDPROGRESS_01 — optional; receives a beat at every stage boundary
and once per sampled frame, so a caller can drive a real progress bar rather than a
spinner. Reports are posted on whatever thread the pipeline is running on, so a UI caller
must marshal (Progress<T> does this automatically when constructed on the UI thread).

### HudAutoDetector.MaxMedianFrames (line 297)

Maximum number of frames retained in the bounded ring buffer for temporal median calculation.

### HudAutoDetector.SampleResult (line 300)

Result of streaming frame analysis: bounded median frame, stability mask, and total frame count.

### HudAutoDetector.ReportProgress (line 307)

Monotonic progress reporter: ensures reported percentage never retreats.

### HudAutoDetector.SampleFramesAsync (line 321)

Pulls `targetFrames` frames spread evenly across the WHOLE clip, already
scaled to analysis size, as raw interleaved BGR.

TWO MODES, AND THE REASON THERE ARE TWO.
The Python seeked with `CAP_PROP_POS_FRAMES` sixty times, which OpenCV implements as
sixty seek-and-decode-forward operations. That is fine at OpenCV's level of abstraction and
intolerable here: sixty ffmpeg processes, or one full decode of a long capture, and the user
is watching a disabled button the whole time.

  • SHORT AND MEDIUM CLIPS (under `KeyframeOnlyThresholdSeconds`): one ffmpeg,
    full decode, `fps=` filter resampling the stream down to exactly the frames wanted.
    Uniform by construction, and a full decode of a two-minute clip is a few seconds.
  • LONG CLIPS: one ffmpeg with `-skip_frame nokey`, which decodes ONLY keyframes and
    is therefore roughly constant-cost per minute of footage no matter how long the file is.
    Keyframes land every 1-4 seconds in any normal game capture, so they already span the
    whole file; we read up to `MaxKeyframesRead` of them and then subsample
    uniformly in managed code. Deliberately NOT combined with `-frames:v`, which would
    take the first N keyframes and give us a detailed picture of the first two minutes and
    nothing at all about the rest.

Both modes end up at "frames spread evenly over the clip", which is the only property the
median and the stability map actually depend on.

A short read at the end of the stream is normal (ffmpeg closes the pipe mid-frame when the
stream ends); the partial frame is discarded rather than analysed as garbage.

### HudAutoDetector.FriendlyRoleName — WANDPROGRESS_01 (line 741)

WANDPROGRESS_01 — a role key in words the user recognises from the screen.

Deliberately NOT the display names in CropToolWindow.Roles: this assembly has no reference
to the App project, and duplicating that table here would create two sources of truth for
something a user reads. These are short, plain descriptions of the THING, which is what a
progress line wants anyway ("Checking for the health bar" beats "Checking for
Own Health Bar (HP)"). An unknown key falls through to itself rather than to "unknown", so a
new role added to RoleSpecs still produces a sensible line before anyone updates this.

### HudAutoDetector.AnalysisContext (line 761)

Everything the scorers need, bundled so the signatures stay readable.

### HudAutoDetector.TemporalMedian (line 768)

Per-pixel, per-channel median across every sampled frame — `np.median(frames, axis=0)`.

The median and not the mean, and this is not a detail: a mean smears a bright muzzle flash
across every pixel it touched, while a median ignores it entirely unless it was present in
more than half the frames. HUD elements are present in all of them.

Quickselect rather than a sort. With sixty samples per channel and roughly 1.5 million
channels at analysis resolution, an insertion sort is ~1.4 billion comparisons and a
noticeable stall; quickselect is linear in the sample count and finishes in well under a
second. The median-of-an-even-count convention is the LOWER of the two middle values, which
is what OpenCV/NumPy's integer path effectively yields after the cast back to uint8.
Per-pixel, per-channel median across the bounded ring buffer of frames — `np.median(frames, axis=0)`.
Quickselect over at most 15 samples per channel completes in milliseconds with zero LOH churn.

### HudAutoDetector.QuickSelect (line 799)

Hoare selection over a scratch buffer. Destroys `data`, which is
fine — the caller refills it for every pixel.

### HudAutoDetector.TemporalStabilityMask (line 825)

The stability mask — `_compute_temporal_stability_mask`. Bright means "this pixel did
not change over the clip", which is the closest thing to a definition of "HUD" that exists.

Per-pixel standard deviation over time (population, ddof=0, matching NumPy's default),
averaged across the three channels, min-max normalised, inverted, and thresholded hard at
165. The open-then-close pass afterwards removes single-pixel speckle and then seals the
gaps inside what survived, so a panel reads as one region rather than a constellation.

Under three frames there is nothing meaningful to measure and the Python returned an
all-pass mask rather than a misleading one; so does this.
The stability mask — converted directly from online variance accumulators (Welford's algorithm).
Bright means "this pixel did not change over the clip", which identifies static HUD panels.
Inverted min-max normalization via `HudImageOps.NormalizeMinMaxToByte`, hard thresholded
at 165, and morphological open/close to seal panel regions.

### HudAutoDetector.BuildColourAnchorMask (line 865)

`get_hud_color_anchors` — the four colour families a Fortnite HUD is actually built
from, in OpenCV's 8-bit HSV (hue 0..179).

These four ranges are the single strongest signal the detector has, which is why the role
scorer weights their coverage at 44 out of roughly 130. Gameplay is muddy; a health bar is
a saturated green rectangle and nothing else on screen looks like one.

### HudAutoDetector.ExtractRoleCandidates (line 893)

`_extract_role_candidates` — find and rank the candidates for one role.

The role's zone is cut out of the three masks first, so a blob is only ever found where that
role could legitimately be. Inside the zone the search mask is
`(base OR close(anchor)) AND dilate(stability)`, then closed: read that as "dark
structure or HUD-coloured pixels, but only where nothing moved".

Eight weighted terms decide the ranking; the weights are the Python's and are not arbitrary:
colour anchoring (44) dominates, then position agreement and aspect plausibility (16 each),
then edges (20), stability (16), fill (9) and size (6). Minimap candidates get a bonus for
having a busy top band, which is what the stats row above the map looks like.

### HudAutoDetector.ExtractGenericCandidates (line 1012)

`_extract_generic_candidates` — the "I do not recognise this HUD" path.

Searches the union of every role's zone and applies shape, size and position heuristics
instead of role knowledge: corners and edges of the screen are preferred, the centre is
penalised (that is where the game is), and the middle-right band has to clear an extra bar
because that is where gameplay most often produces a stable-looking rectangle that is not a
HUD element at all.

Returns candidates with no role key: the detector genuinely does not know what these are,
and guessing a label the user then has to correct is worse than asking.

### HudAutoDetector.BuildHudZoneMask (line 1085)

`_build_hud_zone_mask` — the union of all five role zones, i.e. everywhere a HUD
element is allowed to be at all.

### HudAutoDetector.HuntForCircles (line 1111)

`_hunt_for_circles` — the minimap-specific last resort, run only when the role detector
found no minimap at all.

A Hough gradient circle detector, matching `cv2.HoughCircles(HOUGH_GRADIENT, dp=1,
minDist=120, param1=50, param2=30, ...)` in structure: Canny at (param1/2, param1), then
every edge pixel votes for centres along its own gradient line at each radius in range, then
centres are the local maxima above param2, separated by minDist, and each centre's radius is
the most-supported distance to the edge pixels around it.

APPROXIMATION, DECLARED: OpenCV refines centres to sub-pixel accuracy and groups radii with
a sorted-distance walk; this votes on an integer accumulator and takes the modal radius
bin. Results can therefore differ by a pixel or two from the Python's. That is acceptable
here and nowhere else in this file: this path produces a SUGGESTION the user then drags into
place, it runs only when everything better has already failed, and the ±10px margin the
original added around every circle it found is an order of magnitude larger than the
disagreement.

Searched region is the top-right of the frame only, which is where a minimap is.

### HudAutoDetector.ScaledToSourceRect (line 1250)

THE ONLY PLACE ANALYSIS PIXELS BECOME SOURCE PIXELS. `_scaled_to_original_rect`.
Everything upstream is in the 540-row analysis space; everything the caller sees is in the
snapshot's own space. If a coordinate ever looks wrong by a constant factor, it went around
this method.

### HudAutoDetector.CropMask (line 1278)

Copies a rectangular window out of a full-frame mask. Callers have already clamped.

### HudAutoDetector.Ratio (line 1289)

Fraction of a window that is non-zero in the given mask. The denominator is the
CLAMPED window, so a window hanging off the frame still yields a meaningful ratio.

### HudAutoDetector.Iou (line 1297)

`_rect_iou` — intersection over union.

### HudAutoDetector.NonMaxSuppress (line 1314)

`_nms_candidates` — greedy non-maximum suppression, highest score first.

### HudAutoDetector.MergeOverlapping (line 1332)

`_merge_overlapping_rects` — unions any two rectangles that come within 25 source pixels
of each other, repeatedly, until nothing more merges.

This is what turns "the health bar" and "the shield bar just above it" into one box the user
can place as a unit, which is how the HUD is actually laid out and how the exporter expects
to receive it.

Role keys are preserved on merge: if either input knew what it was, the union inherits it.
When two DIFFERENT roles merge, the FIRST one wins, and since the caller appends in role
order that means the earlier (more important) role keeps the label.

### HudAutoDetector.DedupeByIou (line 1390)

`_dedupe_rects_by_iou` — keeps the first of any group of near-identical boxes.
Input order is significant and is role order, so the labelled box survives the unlabelled
one.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HudCandidateFusion.cs

### HudCandidateFusion — AIHUD_02 (line 8)

AIHUD_02 — deterministic LOCAL + AI candidate fusion.

**Inputs.** Local candidates in the detector's own rank order (the first is the
minimap, by design), and AI candidates ALREADY validated by `AiHudResponseParser`
and converted once by `AiHudGeometry` into the same source-pixel space.

**Algorithm.**

- AI candidates are sorted by confidence desc, then Y, X, W, H, label (ordinal) — a total
order, so identical inputs always give identical output whatever order the model listed them
in.
- Each AI box, in that order, claims the not-yet-claimed local box with the highest IoU,
provided the roles are compatible (equal, or either side unknown) AND the boxes overlap enough:
IoU ≥ `MatchIoU`, or the smaller box lies ≥ `MatchContainment` inside
the larger. Ties go to the lower local index.
- A matched pair becomes ONE `HudCandidateSource.Fused` result. It keeps the
LOCAL box unless the AI is confident (≥ `RefineConfidence`) AND agrees closely
(IoU ≥ `RefineIoU`) — then the AI box refines it. The local role wins; the AI
fills a missing one. A refinement never relocates a box, only tightens or loosens it.
- Unmatched local boxes survive unchanged — the AI missing something is not evidence.
- Unmatched AI boxes survive only at confidence ≥ `MinAiOnlyConfidence`, and
only if they do not duplicate an already-kept box (IoU ≥ `DuplicateIoU`).
- Output: local-rank order for local/fused results, then AI-only results in AI order; cut
at `MaxCandidates`. Local/fused results are placed first so a cap can never drop
a local result in favour of an AI-only one.

### HudCandidateFusion.Containment (line 135)

Intersection over the SMALLER box's area: 1.0 means one box lies wholly inside the other.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HudConfig.cs

### HudConfig.SourceCropsSection (line 19)

IDEA_1 — optional source-pixel crop rectangles. NOT in `RequiredSections`: the
exporter never reads it, only the Crop Tool editor does. `Sanitize` deep-clones
the document, so the section survives untouched; the loop at the end only prunes entries
whose matching content crop has been cleared, so a deleted layer cannot leave an orphan.

### HudConfig.CropDriftType (line 42)

Returns the drift correction type: "left" for stats/map/hp/team/spec, "right" for loot.
Enforces +1px Left bias for Map, Stats, and Health, and +1px Right bias for Loot.

ISSUE_6 — scope note. "map"/"minimap" are matched here but are NOT in
`HudKeys` and have no entry in CropConfigDefaults, so no shipped profile
exercises that branch. It is still live and correct for user-created layers: the Crop Tool
accepts a free-text element name, `Sanitize` builds its key set from
`HudKeys` UNION every key present in the config, and the substring fallback
below catches names such as "minimap_left". Keep both the exact and the substring match —
removing them would silently drop the +1px LEFT bias from any custom map layer.

### HudConfig.ToScale — ZEROSCALE_01, NOMASK_01 (line 82)

ZEROSCALE_01 — a scale that is not strictly positive is corruption, never intent.

The fallback was declared but only ever returned for a THROWN parse. A value that parsed
cleanly to zero or to a negative — "0", "0/1", "-1/2", a JSON 0, or anything Frac.FromString
resolves to zero rather than rejecting — was returned as-is. Sanitize then fed it to
QuantizeBackendSize, whose Math.Max(factor, ...) floor turned the element into a 32x32
backend sliver (27x27 in content space), and wrote that back to the file. The element did
not disappear, which would at least have been legible: it became a permanent postage stamp
that no amount of resizing in the editor could explain.

"Switched off" is expressed by a zero CROP RECT, never by a zero scale — see NOMASK_01 in
Validate below — so nothing is lost by refusing one here.

### HudConfig.ReadSchemaVersion — CROPCHK_01 (line 137)

CROPCHK_01 — READ THE VERSION WITHOUT CARING WHICH NUMERIC TYPE IT WAS STORED AS.

This used to be `(int)(config["schema_version"]?.GetValue<long>() ?? 0)`, and it
threw on EVERY export: "A value of type 'System.Int32' cannot be converted to a
'System.Int64'". `System.Text.Json` will not widen an Int32 node to Int64 —
`GetValue<T>` demands the exact stored type — and the config is written with an
Int32 schema_version (the log line "CROP CONFIG READY - schema=4" is that value).

⚠️ THE DAMAGE WAS SILENT AND TOTAL. `IsCurrentSpace` is reached from
`HudConfig.Validate`, whose only caller wraps it in a try/catch that logs at INFO and
carries on (VideoConfig.GetMobileCoordinatesAsync). So the crop-configuration check — added
specifically because a nonsense crop config used to reach export unnoticed — has never once
run. Every export logged "Crop configuration check could not run" and nobody read it.

Parsed off the raw text instead, so Int32, Int64 and a quoted "4" all work and a malformed
value degrades to 0 (treated as out of date) rather than throwing.

⚠️ DO NOT reintroduce a typed GetValue<T> on a JSON number that other code writes.
The identical trap was already fixed once in HardwareCapability (GetValue<bool>).

### HudConfig.IsContentSpace (line 168)

Whether the crop Y values are already content-relative.

CRITICAL — this deliberately ignores schema_version and looks ONLY at coordinate_space.
The one-time `y -= 150` migration converts canvas-space Y (0 at the top of the 1920 canvas)
into content-space Y (0 at the top of the 1620 content area). That is a property of the
COORDINATE SPACE, not of the schema number.

Sanitize used to gate that subtraction on `IsCurrentSpace`, which also requires
`version >= HudSchemaVersion`. Bumping the schema to 4 for IDEA_1's crops_source section
would therefore have made every existing v3 file look un-migrated and subtracted 150px from
every crop a SECOND time — silently shifting every saved mask up by 150 pixels on first
load. Any future schema bump would have done the same. Keying on the space string alone
makes the migration idempotent no matter how many times the version changes.

### HudConfig.Sanitize (line 186)

Exact port of sanitize_hud_config. Deep-clones, validates, and fixes all sections.
Migrates legacy coordinates if not in current space.

### HudConfig.Validate (line 326)

Validates config and returns list of issues. Exact port of validate_hud_config().

### HudConfig.ParseScaleStrict — CONFIGVAL_01 (line 502)

CONFIGVAL_01 — parses a scale WITHOUT ToScale's fallback, so Validate can tell "this value is
bad" from "this value is fine". ToScale deliberately swallows both cases into 1/1 because its
caller (Sanitize) has to produce something renderable; Validate has to produce the truth.

### HudConfig.HasKeyIgnoreCase — CONFIGVAL_01 (line 518)

CONFIGVAL_01 — JsonObject.ContainsKey is ordinal; element keys are not.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HudDetectionCoordinator.cs

### HudDetectionRequest (line 10)

One Magic Wand run's inputs.
**LocalDetect:** The offline detector (or its cached answer for this clip). Always runs.
**AiFrame:** The ONE frozen frame the AI may see, or null when the AI is not to be
asked this run (no key, no consent, frame unavailable). Null means zero network traffic.
**SourceWidth:** Source-frame width every candidate is expressed in.
**SourceHeight:** Source-frame height every candidate is expressed in.

### HudLocalPreview — WANDPROGRESSIVE_01 (line 22)

WANDPROGRESSIVE_01 — the offline candidates of run `Generation`, published while the AI
is still thinking so the user can start reviewing them. Suggestions only; nothing is committed.

### HudDetectionResult (line 28)

One Magic Wand run's published answer. Pure data: SUGGESTIONS for the user to pick from.
Nothing in it — and nothing the coordinator does — commits a crop.

### HudDetectionCoordinator — AIHUD_03 (line 41)

AIHUD_03 — runs the offline detector and the optional AI detector side by side, fuses their
answers deterministically, and guarantees that a cancelled or superseded run publishes NOTHING.

**Staleness.** Every `RunAsync` takes a new generation and cancels the
previous run's token; `Cancel` does the same without starting anything. A run only
returns a result if, after BOTH detectors have finished, its token is still live and its
generation is still the current one. Otherwise it returns null — so a slow AI response can
never overwrite a newer scan, and a cancelled run cannot surface late.

**Fallback.** The AI side never takes the local side down with it. Missing key,
timeout, rate limit, network or model error, malformed answer, or an outright exception from
the provider: the local candidates are returned exactly as the detector produced them. Each
AI failure is reported once through `IFaultSink` at `FaultTier.Degraded`
— the AI suggestions the user asked for did not happen — with wording that says the local
results are still shown. A missing key is not a fault: it is the default, offline state.

**Threading.** No UI types. Callable from the UI thread; all internal awaits use
`ConfigureAwait(false)`, and the caller re-checks `IsCurrent` on its own
thread before drawing.

### HudDetectionCoordinator.AiService (line 79)

The provider, if any. The UI reads `IAiHudDetectionService.ProviderName` for the privacy notice.

### HudDetectionCoordinator.Cancel (line 87)

Cancels the run in flight (if any) and invalidates everything it might still publish.

### HudDetectionCoordinator.RunAsync — WANDPROGRESSIVE_01, AIHUD_03 (line 100)

**localReady:** WANDPROGRESSIVE_01 — told once, with the local candidates, when the
offline scan has finished but the AI has not. Not called when there is no AI request, when the AI
already answered, or when the run is stale or cancelled. The final result still arrives through
the return value and still obeys AIHUD_03.

### HudDetectionCoordinator.MinSourcePixels (line 217)

The Crop Tool's own floor for a usable box (matches its local-candidate filter).

### HudDetectionCoordinator.RunAiGuardedAsync (line 236)

Turns a contract-breaking provider exception into a ModelError result. Caller cancellation still propagates.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HudImageOps.cs

### HudImageOps — MAGICWAND_02 (line 7)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
MAGICWAND_02 — THE IMAGE PRIMITIVES THE HUD DETECTOR NEEDS, AND NOTHING ELSE.

WHY THIS FILE EXISTS AT ALL.
`HudAutoDetector` is a port of the old Python tool's `magic_wand.py`, which was
written against OpenCV. This solution has no OpenCV and is not going to get one: invariant 1 in
docs/README.md is the Single Binary Executable Mandate, and OpenCvSharp ships a ~40 MB native
blob per RID. SkiaSharp is already referenced but is a RASTERISER — it draws, it does not
analyse; it has no adaptive threshold, no morphology, no contours and no Canny.

So the eight operations the detector actually uses are implemented here, deliberately narrowly:
8-bit single-channel, one anchor, one border rule. They are NOT a general image library and must
not grow into one. Each one documents which OpenCV call it stands in for and, where the two
differ, exactly how.

EVERYTHING IS ROW-MAJOR, `byte[]`, `index = y * width + x`, and every method returns a
NEW array rather than writing in place — the pipeline reuses its inputs (the same edge mask is
scored against four different roles), so in-place would be a very quiet bug.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### HudImageOps.BgrToGray (line 31)

`cv2.cvtColor(src, COLOR_BGR2GRAY)`. Same ITU-R BT.601 luma weights OpenCV uses
(0.114 B, 0.587 G, 0.299 R), same rounding.

**bgr:** Interleaved BGR, 3 bytes per pixel.

### HudImageOps.BgrToHsv (line 46)

`cv2.cvtColor(src, COLOR_BGR2HSV)` in OpenCV's 8-bit convention: H is 0..179 (degrees
halved so it fits a byte), S and V are 0..255. The detector's colour anchors
(`HudAutoDetector.BuildColourAnchorMask`) are written in those units, straight
out of the Python, so getting this convention right is not optional.

**Returns:** Interleaved HSV, 3 bytes per pixel, same layout as the input.

### HudImageOps.GaussianKernel (line 85)

OpenCV's `getGaussianKernel(n, sigma)`. When sigma is non-positive OpenCV derives it
from the kernel size with this exact formula, and the detector relies on that default
(`adaptiveThreshold(..., blockSize: 21, ...)` never passes a sigma), so it is
reproduced rather than approximated.

### HudImageOps.GaussianBlurToDouble (line 110)

Separable Gaussian blur with BORDER_REPLICATE, returning DOUBLES.

Doubles, not bytes, because the only consumer is `AdaptiveThresholdGaussianInv`,
which compares `src` against `localMean - C`. Rounding the mean to a byte first
would quantise the very quantity the comparison turns on and flip pixels near the boundary.
BORDER_REPLICATE is what `cv2.adaptiveThreshold` uses internally.

### HudImageOps.GaussianBlur (line 162)

Separable Gaussian blur returning bytes. Used by the circle hunter, which feeds a
blurred 8-bit image to Sobel exactly as `cv2.GaussianBlur(img, (9,9), 2)` did.

### HudImageOps.AdaptiveThresholdGaussianInv (line 176)

`cv2.adaptiveThreshold(src, 255, ADAPTIVE_THRESH_GAUSSIAN_C, THRESH_BINARY_INV, blockSize, C)`.

OpenCV computes `T(x,y) = gaussianMean(x,y) - C` and then, for BINARY_INV, emits
`src > T ? 0 : 255`. Note the STRICT greater-than: a pixel exactly equal to the
threshold is foreground. That asymmetry is preserved here because the HUD glyphs this is
hunting are flat-shaded and produce long runs of exactly-equal pixels.

The detector uses it to find DARK structure on a bright frame — HUD panels are typically
dark plates — which is why the INV variant is the one that got ported.

### HudImageOps.ThresholdBinary (line 200)

`cv2.threshold(src, thresh, 255, THRESH_BINARY)`: strictly greater wins.

### HudImageOps.NormalizeMinMaxToByte (line 208)

`cv2.normalize(src, None, 0, 255, NORM_MINMAX)` for a float map, rounded to bytes.
A flat map (max == min) normalises to all-zero, matching OpenCV's behaviour when the range
collapses.
collapses. When `invert` is true, inverts the output so min maps to 255 and max to 0.

### HudImageOps.Dilate (line 252)

Rectangular dilate — `cv2.dilate(src, getStructuringElement(MORPH_RECT, (w,h)))`,
centre anchor.

Separable (a rectangle is the product of a horizontal and a vertical line), so this is two
linear passes rather than one quadratic one — at 960x540 with a 5x5 kernel that is the
difference between 25 and 10 reads per pixel.

BORDER: out-of-range samples are simply skipped. That is not laziness — it is precisely
OpenCV's default `morphologyDefaultBorderValue()`, which is -infinity for dilate and
+infinity for erode, i.e. a border that can never win the max (or the min).

### HudImageOps.Erode (line 267)

Rectangular erode — `cv2.erode` with a MORPH_RECT element, centre anchor.
See `Dilate` for the border rule.

### HudImageOps.MorphClose (line 272)

`cv2.morphologyEx(src, MORPH_CLOSE, rect(kw,kh))` — dilate, then erode.
Closes the gaps between the strokes of a glyph so a word becomes one blob.

### HudImageOps.MorphOpen (line 277)

`cv2.morphologyEx(src, MORPH_OPEN, rect(kw,kh))` — erode, then dilate.
Removes speckle smaller than the kernel without shrinking what survives.

### HudImageOps.Or (line 328)

`cv2.bitwise_or` over two masks of equal length.

### HudImageOps.And (line 336)

`cv2.bitwise_and` over two masks of equal length.

### HudImageOps.Canny (line 345)

`cv2.Canny(src, low, high)` with the default aperture 3 and `L2gradient=False`.

Four stages, all of them OpenCV's:
  1. Sobel 3x3 for gx and gy. NO blur first — `cv2.Canny` does not blur, and the caller
     is responsible for that if it wants it. (The detector does not: it runs Canny on the
     temporal MEDIAN, which is already about as denoised as a frame gets.)
  2. Magnitude as |gx| + |gy| (the L1 norm), because L2gradient defaults to false.
  3. Non-maximum suppression against the nearer of the four 45-degree sectors.
  4. Hysteresis: seeds are pixels above `high`; the flood keeps any
     8-neighbour above `low`. Iterative with an explicit stack, not
     recursive — a long HUD edge on a 4K-sourced frame is thousands of pixels and would
     overflow the call stack.

### HudImageOps.Sobel (line 445)

Sobel gx/gy on an 8-bit image, borders left at zero. Used by the circle hunter,
which needs the gradient DIRECTION to vote along.

### HudImageOps.Blob (line 470)

One foreground blob: its bounding box and how many pixels it actually contains.

### HudImageOps.FindBlobs (line 473)

Stands in for `cv2.findContours(mask, RETR_EXTERNAL, CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE)` followed by
`boundingRect` and `contourArea` on each result.

This is 8-connected connected-component labelling rather than border following, because the
detector never looks at a contour's POINTS — it only ever asks for the bounding rectangle
and the area, and for an outer contour those are exactly a component's bounding box and
pixel count.

ONE DELIBERATE DEVIATION, AND IT IS THE ONLY ONE: `cv2.contourArea` returns the area of
the outer POLYGON, so it counts holes as filled; `Blob.Area` counts foreground
pixels, so it does not. The single consumer is `fill_ratio = area / (w*h)`, one of eight
weighted score terms, and it is weighted 9 out of ~130 in the role scorer. The effect is that
a ring-shaped blob (the minimap's outer bezel, most obviously) scores slightly LOWER on
fill than OpenCV would have given it. Two other terms — the colour-anchor ratio and the
temporal-stability ratio, worth 44 and 16 — are computed over the bounding box and are
unaffected, so ranking is dominated by them either way. Recorded here so the next reader
does not go hunting for a bug that is a documented approximation.

Iterative flood fill with an explicit stack; see `Canny` for why not recursion.

### HudImageOps.CountNonZero (line 546)

Non-zero count inside an axis-aligned window, the equivalent of
`cv2.countNonZero(mask[y:y+h, x:x+w])`. Clamps to the image the way NumPy slicing does,
and returns the CLAMPED window's size as the denominator so the caller's ratio stays a
ratio even for a window that hangs off the edge.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/HudOverlayEventDetector.cs

### OverlayEvent (line 9)

A moment where something appeared in a normally still part of the screen (a kill feed line, a banner).

### HudOverlayEventDetector — KILLGATE_01, AIELIM_06 (line 12)

KILLGATE_01 (docs/01 TL-AITRACKING, AIELIM_06) — game-agnostic "something popped up on the HUD" signal. The video
is read once as small grey frames. Pixels that barely change over the whole video are the STILL part of the screen
(HUD, kill-feed area, letterbox); the live picture is not. An event is a step where at least
`MinEventFraction` of the still pixels change at once, but not more than `MaxEventFraction`
(that is a menu, a cut or a death screen, not an overlay). Events closer than `MergeSec` keep the strongest.

### HudOverlayEventDetector.ScanAsync (line 37)

Reads the video at `SampleFps` as grey frames and returns its overlay events.

### HudOverlayEventDetector.Detect (line 66)

The pure part: grey frames (all the same size) sampled at `fps` → events.

### KillMomentPlanner — KILLGATE_01 (line 112)

KILLGATE_01 — which moments the Kill Tracker asks the AI about, from the loud audio peaks (loudest first) and the
on-screen overlay events. Without overlay events (still screen not found, scan failed) the plan is the old one:
the loudest peaks up to the budget. With them, a loud moment is kept only when an overlay appeared within
`ConfirmBeforeSec`..`ConfirmAfterSec` of it; overlay events with no loud moment near them
are added after (a silent kill), placed `SilentLeadSec` before the overlay appeared.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/IntroTag.cs

### IntroTag — SCRAPER_01 (line 7)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
SCRAPER_01 — THE HIDDEN "THIS FILE STARTS WITH A THUMBNAIL INTRO" TAG.

Every export that carries the 0.1 s still thumbnail intro (the first frame shown by SMS and
WhatsApp, so a shared clip never shows a black thumbnail) records the intro's exact length in
the container:

    fvs_intro_sec = "0.100"

It is written as an MP4 mdta key (`-movflags use_metadata_tags`). Players, Explorer's
Details tab and share targets ignore it; ffprobe reports it under `format.tags`.

The Video Merger's Thumbnail Scraper trusts ONLY this tag. It never guesses from pixels: an
untagged file (anything exported before this build, or by another program) is merged as-is.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### IntroTag.StandardIntroSec (line 28)

The still-intro length every current export uses.

### IntroTag.MaxPlausibleIntroSec (line 31)

Largest value accepted as a genuine intro. Anything bigger is treated as corrupt.

### IntroTag.MovFlags (line 34)

`-movflags` value for every tagged MP4 output (faststart kept).

### IntroTag.OutputArgs (line 37)

The muxer arguments for an output file: the movflags pair, plus the tag pair when the file
really starts with an intro. Always replaces a plain `-movflags +faststart` pair.

### IntroTag.OutputArgs — TIMINGTAG_02 (line 46)

TIMINGTAG_02 — muxer arguments carrying BOTH the v1 seconds key (older Merger builds read it)
and the frame-exact v2 `ExportTimingTag.Key`. Always uses mdta tags.

### IntroTag.Read (line 60)

Reads the tag from ffprobe's `format.tags` object. 0 when absent, unparsable, not
positive, larger than `MaxPlausibleIntroSec`, or larger than half the file.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/LibMpvInterop.cs

### LibMpvInterop.MPV_RENDER_PARAM_SKIP_RENDERING — ISSUE_05 (line 16)

ISSUE_05 — `int*` flag. When set to 1, mpv performs the whole frame-advance
bookkeeping but skips the actual draw, and — critically — does NOT touch the graphics
API, so this is safe to call with no GL context current.

WHY THIS EXISTS HERE: with `vo=libmpv` mpv only produces the NEXT video frame once
the client has consumed the current one by calling `mpv_render_context_render`.
Any path that receives a frame-ready callback and then declines to render (surface not
ready, control not laid out yet, keyed-mutex acquire timed out) permanently stalls the
pipeline: no render -> no new frame -> no new callback. Pumping a skipped render keeps
the clock moving so the preview recovers on the next frame instead of freezing forever.

⚠️ EXACT mpv render.h enum value — sits between BLOCK_FOR_TARGET_TIME(12) and
DRM_DISPLAY(14), matching the SW_* offsets documented below. DO NOT change.

### LibMpvInterop.MPV_RENDER_UPDATE_FRAME (line 38)

Bitmask flag returned by `mpv_render_context_update`.
When set, a new video frame is available and `mpv_render_context_render`
should be called to draw it to the FBO.

### LibMpvInterop.mpv_render_context_update (line 87)

Signals a "wakeup" to the render context. This must be called from the
update callback (or after receiving it) to acknowledge that the host
is ready to consume the next frame. Returns a bitmask of flags; if
`MPV_RENDER_UPDATE_FRAME` is set, call
`mpv_render_context_render` to draw the frame.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/MPVSafetyManager.cs

### MPVSafetyManager — MPVSAFETY_01, IPCTEARDOWN_01, ISSUE_13 (line 9)

Absorbs a rapid-scrub seek storm into ONE issued mpv seek, and watches for a seek that never
lands.

**MPVSAFETY_01 — TEARDOWN ORDER IS SIGNAL, WAIT, THEN FREE. IN THAT ORDER, ALWAYS.**

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
WHAT WAS WRONG: `Dispose()` was two lines — `_cts.Cancel()` immediately followed by
`_cts.Dispose()`. At that instant TWO threads were still bound to `_cts.Token`: the
watchdog polling `IsCancellationRequested` on a 500 ms `Thread.Sleep` cycle, and the
seek processor suspended inside `ReadAllAsync(_cts.Token)` or `Task.Delay(..., token)`.
Freeing the source underneath live registrations makes those consumers observe
`ObjectDisposedException`, NOT `OperationCanceledException`.

The watchdog already guarded that case. The seek processor did not — and it was declared
`async void` and started from a raw `Thread`, so it had no
SynchronizationContext to rethrow onto. The exception surfaced on the thread pool as an
unobserved top-level throw, which under the .NET default policy FAST-FAILS THE PROCESS. A user
closing a window mid-scrub could lose the whole app with no managed stack anywhere.

Neither thread was joined either, so `_mpvHandle` could be commanded after its owner had
already destroyed it — a native access violation (0xC0000005) that no managed catch can contain.

THE FIX is the ordering `Ipc/NamedPipeStateServer.Dispose` already proves under
IPCTEARDOWN_01, plus the handle-ownership discipline `MpvIpcClient.Dispose` proves under
ISSUE_13: stop producing, signal, WAIT (bounded) for every consumer to actually exit, and only
then free what they were using. There is no `async void` left in this file.
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### MPVSafetyManager.SeekDebounceMs — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 41)

MPVSAFETY_01 — minimum spacing between two ISSUED seeks. Tuned; do not raise.

### MPVSafetyManager.StuckSeekSeconds — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 44)

MPVSAFETY_01 — a seek still outstanding after this long is considered stuck.

### MPVSafetyManager.WatchdogPollMs — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 47)

MPVSAFETY_01 — watchdog cadence. The stop signal short-circuits the wait, so this
is a poll interval, NOT a floor on teardown latency.

### MPVSafetyManager.TeardownJoinCeiling — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 51)

MPVSAFETY_01 — a consumer that will not stop must not hang the caller's shutdown.

### MPVSafetyManager._handleValid — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 60)

MPVSAFETY_01 — set once the owner hands the handle back or teardown begins. Checked
immediately before every native command so a destroyed handle is never dereferenced.

### MPVSafetyManager._watchdogExited — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 64)

MPVSAFETY_01 — the ONLY reliable signal that each loop has genuinely stopped
touching `_cts` and `_mpvHandle`. Same contract as MpvIpcClient._eventLoopExited.

### MPVSafetyManager._stopSignal — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 69)

MPVSAFETY_01 — unblocks the watchdog's wait the instant teardown starts, so a join
never costs a full poll interval.

### MPVSafetyManager.RequestSeek (line 109)

Fire-and-forget, non-blocking, allocation-free. Safe to call from an input handler on the UI
thread and safe to call after `Dispose` (it becomes a no-op).

### MPVSafetyManager.SeekProcessorThreadBody — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 120)

MPVSAFETY_01 — the thread body. It exists so the loop can be `async Task` instead of
`async void` while the thread KEEPS ITS NAME (crash digests are read by that name), and
so every possible throw is terminated here rather than on the thread pool. Blocking on the
task is correct on a thread we own and started for exactly this purpose: there is no
SynchronizationContext to deadlock against and no pool worker is being starved.

### MPVSafetyManager.IssueSeek — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 197)

MPVSAFETY_01 — the ONLY place the native handle is dereferenced, and it is gated on
`_handleValid`. The `"F1"` / InvariantCulture formatting is load-bearing:
mpv's command-string parser reads an invariant decimal point, and a comma from a localised
format produces a command it silently fails to parse.

### MPVSafetyManager.WatchdogLoop — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 215)

MPVSAFETY_01 — trips when a seek has been outstanding past `StuckSeekSeconds`.

It used to report through `Console.Error.WriteLine`, which in a windowed Avalonia
process goes nowhere at all — a tripped watchdog left no artifact on any machine that was
not running under a console. It now reports through `CoreLogger` like every
other diagnostic in this assembly, and names the target it gave up on.

### MPVSafetyManager.Dispose — MPVSAFETY_01 (line 265)

MPVSAFETY_01 — idempotent, never throws, and NEVER frees the token source while a consumer
can still touch it. See the class remarks for the defect this ordering closes.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Media/MediaMetadataProbe.cs

### MediaProbeBackend (line 10)

Which metadata backend `MediaMetadataProbe` may use.

### MediaProbeBackend.Auto (line 13)

Native libav first; ffprobe subprocess once if native cannot answer. The default.

### MediaProbeBackend.Native (line 15)

Native libav only (tests, benchmarks). No subprocess.

### MediaProbeBackend.Ffprobe (line 17)

ffprobe subprocess only — the pre-LIBAVPROBE behaviour, kept as the explicit fallback.

### MediaProbeResult (line 21)

One metadata answer. `Data` is ffprobe `-show_format -show_streams` JSON (the
native backend emits the same keys) or null when no backend could read the file.
`ExitCode` is the ffprobe exit code when the subprocess ran, else 0 / -1.

### MediaMetadataProbe — LIBAVPROBE_03 (line 31)

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
LIBAVPROBE_03 — THE ONE ROUTER FOR MEDIA METADATA, AND ITS FALLBACK RULE.

MediaProber and OutputSizeEstimator ask here instead of starting ffprobe themselves. The answer
is the same JSON shape either way, so every accessor kept its existing selection rule
unchanged (first video stream, any audio stream, format duration then video duration, …).

FALLBACK — explicit, bounded, logged, testable, one direction only:
  • native Unavailable (DLLs missing, version/layout guard) → ffprobe. Latched by the backend;
    logged once by LibAvLibrary.
  • native MediaError (libav rejected the file) → ffprobe ONCE, with the remaining time budget,
    reported as a Recoverable fault (log-only: the user's outcome is whatever ffprobe says, i.e.
    exactly the pre-native outcome). ffprobe uses the same libraries, so it normally fails too —
    that is fine and costs what every probe cost before.
  • cancellation → re-thrown, never a fault, never a fallback.
  • native timeout → OperationCanceledException (as AsyncProcessRunner's timeout always threw);
    no fallback, because the budget is spent.
  • ffprobe never falls back to native. There is no loop.
`MediaProbeBackend.Ffprobe` (or env `FVS_MEDIA_PROBE=ffprobe`) forces the old path.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### MediaMetadataProbe.BackendEnvironmentVariable (line 59)

Environment override: `ffprobe` or `native`; anything else = Auto.

### MediaMetadataProbe.DefaultBackend (line 62)

The backend Auto resolves to for this process (environment override honoured).

### MediaMetadataProbe.NativeOverride (line 76)

Test seam: replaces the native backend for the CURRENT async flow only (AsyncLocal), so a
fault-injection test cannot leak into a parallel test. Null = `LibAvMediaBackend.Instance`.

### MediaMetadataProbe.FallbackCount (line 87)

Probes answered by ffprobe after native could not (process lifetime). Diagnostics/tests.

### MediaMetadataProbe.RunFfprobeAsync (line 117)

The pre-LIBAVPROBE subprocess path, unchanged: ffprobe -v quiet -print_format json -show_format -show_streams.
