Writes the four keys onto a meme object.

### MemePresentationJson.Apply (line 44)

Applies the four keys (when present) to a placement read without them.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Project/ProjectDocument.cs

### ProjectDocument — PROJ_01, PROJ_07 (line 10)

PROJ_01 — THE SAVEABLE DOCUMENT. Everything a user built, in one immutable value.

WHY THIS TYPE EXISTS: until now the application had no way to save work. The only thing that
ever reached disk was `recovery_v2.json`, which is a CRASH artefact — it answers "the app
died, what was on screen?", not "open the montage I made on Tuesday". Closing the app on
purpose destroyed the session. An hour of markers, cuts, speed ramps, zooms and meme placements
existed only in the live window.

The set of fields here is not a guess. It is exactly the input surface of
`OutputTimeline.Create` plus the settings that decide how those chunks are rendered.
If a field is not needed to reconstruct the finished video, it does not belong in the document;
if it is, its absence is a silent data-loss bug. `BuildTimeline` is the proof — it
reconstructs the authoritative timeline from nothing but this record, which is the test that the
document is complete.

⚠️ NORTH STAR #2 (ABSOLUTE AUTHORITY FOR TIME) APPLIES HERE. This document stores the INPUTS to
`OutputTimeline` and never a derived duration. Caching `TotalOutputSeconds` in
the file would create a second answer to "how long is the video", which would go stale the
moment the maths is corrected in a later build and would then be believed over the real model.
`SourceClip.DurationMs` is the one measured value stored, and it is a property of
the FILE, not of the edit.

⚠️ IMMUTABLE ON PURPOSE. Undo (PROJ_07 / `UndoStack`) keeps a stack of these. If the record
were mutable, every entry on that stack would be the same object and undo would restore the
present. Mutate by `with`-expression only, and keep every collection an
`IReadOnlyList{T}` of value types or immutable records — never a UI control, bitmap,
stream or IPC handle (the rule 04_UI_UX_AVALONIA_SPEC.md §6 UI-GRANULAR already states for the
granular editor's stack, generalised).

### ProjectDocument.FormatId (line 50)

Magic string written into every file so a wrong-type file is rejected by name, not by crash.

### ProjectDocument.SchemaVersion — PROJ_02, PROJ_11, PROJ_12, MEMEMODE_01, VOTIMING_01, CAPTIONS_01, MEMEAUDIT_01, VOLEVEL_01 (line 53)

PROJ_02 — the schema number this build WRITES. Bump it only when the meaning of an existing
field changes; adding a new optional field does not need a bump, because
`ProjectSerializer` tolerates missing keys on read.

**2 — PROJ_11.** Added `Mask` and `Merge`. Both are optional on
read, so a v1 file loads unchanged; the bump records that a v2 writer stores information a
v1 reader will park in `UnknownFields` rather than lose.
**3 — PROJ_12.** Added `ProjectMerge.Edl` (the Video Merger's full edit list:
effects, thumbnail, music). It lives INSIDE `merge`, where a v2 reader keeps no unknown
keys, so a v2 build would silently drop the effects; refusing the file is the safer answer.
**4 — MEMEMODE_01.** Each meme carries `mode` / `corner` / `size` / `sound`.
They live INSIDE a meme object, where a v3 reader keeps no unknown keys: it would read a corner
overlay as a full-screen cutaway (a different, longer video) and drop the keys on save. Refused
instead. Files at 1..3 read unchanged: no keys = full-screen with sound.
**5 — VOTIMING_01.** v4 readers would discard nested voice takes, freeze offsets and review state.
**6 — CAPTIONS_01.** Added `Captions` (AI Live Commentary Captions track).
**7 — MEMEAUDIT_01.** Each meme may carry `clipIn` / `posX` / `posY` /
`animation` / `transition`. Inside a meme object, where a v6 reader keeps no unknown
keys: it would play a trimmed meme in full (a longer video) and drop the keys on save. Refused
instead. Files at 1..6 read unchanged: no keys = the whole meme, in its corner, no effects.
**8 — VOLEVEL_01.** Per-take linear gain; older writers would silently reset it on save.

### ProjectDocument.MinimumReadableSchemaVersion — PROJ_02 (line 79)

PROJ_02 — the oldest schema this build can still READ. A file below this is refused with an
explanation instead of being silently mangled into something the user did not author.

### ProjectDocument.Source (line 87)

The gameplay file being edited, plus enough fingerprint to notice it changed.

### ProjectDocument.BaseSpeed (line 90)

Speed applied to every stretch not covered by a `Segments` entry.

### ProjectDocument.SourceCutStartMs (line 93)

Trim-in point in ABSOLUTE source milliseconds. This is the same number
`OutputTimeline.Create` takes as `sourceCutStartMs`, and it is the origin
that makes every other position in this document clip-relative.

### ProjectDocument.TrimmedDurationMs (line 100)

Length of the trimmed clip in milliseconds — `totalDurationMs` for
`OutputTimeline.Create`. Zero means "not trimmed", and
`EffectiveDurationMs` falls back to the measured file duration.

### ProjectDocument.Segments (line 107)

Speed ramps and freezes, in ABSOLUTE source milliseconds (as `SpeedSegment` is defined).

### ProjectDocument.Cuts (line 110)

Deleted stretches, CLIP-RELATIVE seconds (as `OutputTimeline.Cut` is defined).

### ProjectDocument.Memes (line 113)

Meme cutaways, CLIP-RELATIVE seconds (as `MemePlacement` is defined).

### ProjectDocument.Mask — PROJ_11 (line 120)

PROJ_11 — WHICH HUD MASK THIS MONTAGE WAS BUILT AGAINST. Null means "no mask was applied".

⚠️ THE DEFECT THIS CLOSES: the mask was a machine-wide setting, not a property of the work.
The active profile name lived in `SettingsManager.ActiveMaskOverlay` and the rectangles
lived in one shared `crop_coordinates.json`. Neither was in the project file. So a
project saved in March and reopened in May was exported through whatever mask happened to be
active THEN — different rectangles, different overlays, a different finished video — and
nothing anywhere said so. The user's own record of their edit did not include the single
setting that decides what the frame looks like.

The snapshot is stored, not just the name, because a profile is editable. Naming "Fortnite"
and trusting the machine to still mean the same thing by it is the same bug one level down.

### ProjectDocument.Merge — PROJ_11 (line 140)

PROJ_11 — the Video Merger's queue. Null means this project is a single-clip edit.

⚠️ THE DEFECT THIS CLOSES: merge state lived nowhere. A user could queue eight clips, trim
each of them, close the Merger — and the queue existed only in that window's
`ObservableCollection<string>`. Saving the project recorded none of it, so
reopening produced a single-clip document with no indication that seven clips had been
dropped on the floor.

⚠️ THIS IS ALSO THE DOCUMENT'S ROUTE OUT OF BEING SINGLE-SOURCE. `Source` is one
clip because the main editor edits one clip; a montage of several is what the Merger is for,
and until now those were two unrelated features sharing an application. Storing the ordered
list here makes the merge a property of the project, which is the precondition for ever
treating a multi-clip edit as one document rather than two workflows.

### ProjectDocument.Captions — CAPTIONS_01 (line 161)

CAPTIONS_01 — AI Live Commentary Captions track. Null means no commentary captions were generated.

### ProjectDocument.Title (line 166)

Free-text name shown in the title bar and the recent list. Never used as a file path.

### ProjectDocument.UnknownFields — PROJ_03 (line 173)

PROJ_03 — FORWARD COMPATIBILITY BAG. Top-level keys this build did not recognise, kept
verbatim so that opening a v2 file in a v1 build and saving it back does not AMPUTATE the
v2 fields. Without this, one accidental open-and-save on an older machine silently destroys
work the newer build could have read. It is never interpreted, only carried.

### ProjectDocument.EffectiveDurationMs (line 181)

The duration the timeline should actually be built against: the explicit trim when one was
set, otherwise the measured length of the file.

### ProjectDocument.BuildTimeline — PROJ_04 (line 188)

PROJ_04 — reconstructs the authoritative timeline from the document alone.

This is deliberately the ONLY way the rest of the application is allowed to turn a loaded
project into a timeline. Any screen that re-derives chunks from its own copies of these
lists is re-introducing exactly the duplicated-maths defect that
`OutputTimeline`'s own header documents.

### ProjectDocument.CheckSource — PROJ_05 (line 210)

PROJ_05 — is the gameplay file still the one this project was built against?

A project stores a PATH, and paths outlive the files they point at. Answering this before
restoring an edit is what lets the app say "that video has been replaced, your cuts may not
line up" instead of cheerfully rendering markers against different footage. Length and
modification time are a fingerprint, not a hash: hashing a 4 GB capture on every open would
cost more than the entire load.

### ProjectDocument.Equals — UNDOEQ_01 (line 250)

Creates the empty document a brand-new edit starts from.

UNDOEQ_01 — EDIT-STATE EQUALITY. `docs/07_UNDO_AND_HISTORY.md` §3: "U4 depends on
`Equals` being a real state comparison — a reference-equality default silently disables
it."

The compiler-generated record equality WAS that default. `Segments`,
`Cuts` and `Memes` are arrays built fresh by every
`ProjectSession.Capture()`, so they were compared BY REFERENCE. `ModifiedUtc`
and `CreatedUtc` default to `UtcNow`, and `Mask` carries a fresh
`JsonObject`. Two captures of an untouched editor were therefore never equal: U4
(never push a no-op) could not fire, and every no-op click became an undo step that visibly
did nothing.

Compared: everything the user can change and everything the export reads. The lists are
compared element-wise (the element types are value records). The mask is compared by its
content fingerprint, the same identity `ProjectMask.MatchesLive` uses.
NOT compared: `CreatedUtc`, `ModifiedUtc` (bookkeeping, not state),
`Title` (derived from the source path, which is compared) and
`UnknownFields` (round-trip carriage for newer writers, never edited here).

### ProjectDocument.GetHashCode — UNDOEQ_01 (line 292)

UNDOEQ_01 — consistent with `Equals(ProjectDocument?)`: timestamps excluded.

### SourceIntegrity — PROJ_05 (line 324)

PROJ_05 — the answer to "is the footage still what this project was cut against?".

File is present and matches the stored fingerprint.

### SourceIntegrity.Changed (line 330)

File is present but its size or timestamp differs — the edit may no longer line up.

PROJ_05 — the gameplay file plus the measurements the editor needs before it can lay anything

### SourceClip.DurationMs (line 349)

Full length of the FILE in milliseconds, before any trim.

Size in bytes at save time. Zero means "not measured" — see `ProjectDocument.CheckSource`.

### SourceClip.ModifiedUtcSeconds (line 361)

Unix seconds of last write at save time. Zero means "not measured".

### SourceClip.Probe (line 364)

Reads the fingerprint fields off disk. Never throws; an unreadable file yields zeroes.

### ProjectMask — PROJ_11 (line 396)

PROJ_11 — THE HUD MASK, AS A PROPERTY OF THE PROJECT RATHER THAN OF THE MACHINE.

**ProfileName:**
The profile the user chose, as shown in the Crop Tool's picker. Kept so the UI can say
"this project was built with *Apex Legends*" rather than showing an anonymous blob.

**Fingerprint:**
A stable hash of `Config`. Comparing hashes answers "is the profile on this
machine still the one this project was built with" in one line, and answers it correctly even
when the profile was edited without being renamed — which renaming-based checks cannot do.

**Config:**
The resolved crop configuration itself: rectangles, scales, overlay positions and z-orders.
Stored verbatim so the project reproduces its own export on a machine that has never seen the
named profile. A few kilobytes of JSON against the alternative of an unreproducible render.

The hash stored in `Fingerprint`. Content-addressed, whitespace-insensitive:
two configs that would produce the same filtergraph must produce the same fingerprint, or
the mismatch warning fires on formatting and is promptly ignored by everyone.

True when `liveConfig` is the same mask this project was built with.
A false answer is a Degraded fault, not a silent re-render: the user is about to export a
different video than the one they saved.

PROJ_11 — one clip in the merge queue, with the trim the user set on it.

**FilePath:** Absolute path to the source file, as the user picked it.

**EndSec:** Trim-out, seconds from the start of THAT file. Zero means "to the end".

The clips, in the order they are concatenated. Order is meaning here, not presentation.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Project/ProjectSerializer.cs

### ProjectSerializer — PROJ_02 (line 9)

PROJ_02 — TURNS A `ProjectDocument` INTO JSON AND BACK, WITHOUT REFLECTION.

⚠️ WHY THIS IS HAND-WRITTEN AND NOT `JsonSerializer.Serialize(document)`: the product ships
as a NativeAOT single binary with `TrimMode=full`. Reflection-based System.Text.Json is not
AOT-safe — it compiles, it passes in Debug, and it throws `NotSupportedException` only in
the published .exe on the user's machine. The codebase already avoids this correctly
(`IpcJsonContext` uses source generation; `AtomicJsonFile` works in
`JsonObject`). A user's saved work is the last place in the application that may
depend on a mechanism that fails silently after publish, so this reader and writer touch nothing
but `JsonNode`.

⚠️ THE READER IS DELIBERATELY FORGIVING AND THE WRITER IS DELIBERATELY EXPLICIT. Every
`Read*` helper below takes a default and never throws on a missing, null or wrong-typed
key. This is what makes "add a new optional field" a non-breaking change: an older file simply
does not have the key and gets the default. The one thing that IS refused is a schema number
from the future beyond what `ProjectDocument.SchemaVersion` understands — see
`Read`.

### ProjectSerializer.KnownKeys — PROJ_03 (line 54)

PROJ_03 — the top-level keys this build understands. Anything else found on read is carried
in `ProjectDocument.UnknownFields` and written back out untouched, so an older
build cannot amputate a newer build's data by opening and saving a file.

### ProjectSerializer.Read — PROJ_02 (line 209)

PROJ_02 — parses a document. Returns `null` and sets `error`
when the payload is not a project this build can honour.

The two refusals are deliberate and are NOT the same as a parse failure: a file that is not
a project at all, and a project written by a future build whose schema this one may
misinterpret. Both are reported in words the UI can show verbatim. Everything else — missing
keys, nulls, wrong types inside a known key — is absorbed with defaults, because a project
that opens with one setting reset is worth infinitely more to a user than a project that
refuses to open.

### ProjectSerializer.ReadDouble — PROJ_10, PROJ_05 (line 422)

PROJ_10 — EVERY NUMERIC READ TRIES EVERY NUMERIC BACKING, BECAUSE A `JsonValue` KNOWS
WHAT CLR TYPE IT WAS BUILT FROM AND WILL NOT CONVERT.

⚠️ THE BUG THIS CLOSES WAS SILENT DATA LOSS IN THE SOURCE FINGERPRINT.
`Write` stores `doc.Source.SizeBytes` — a `long` — so the node is a
`JsonValue` backed by `long`. `Read` asked it for a
`double`. `JsonValue.TryGetValue<double>` on a long-backed value
returns `false`: it is an exact-type accessor, not a numeric converter.
So the fallback won, and `SizeBytes` and `ModifiedUtcSeconds` came back as
**0 on every load** — while the writer kept faithfully saving the real values.

The damage was not the two fields. Those two fields ARE the source-integrity fingerprint
(`PROJ_05` / §6 PROJ-INTEGRITY). `CheckSource` compared a real file's size against
a stored 0 and answered `Changed` for every project ever reopened — so the warning that
exists to tell a user their source clip was re-encoded or replaced fired constantly and
meant nothing. A warning that is always on is a warning that is off.

The reader stays forgiving (§2: absorb a missing key, a null, a wrong type) — it just no
longer treats "stored as a different numeric type than I asked for" as absent.

### ProjectSerializer.ReadLong — PROJ_10 (line 461)

PROJ_10 — the 64-bit read. `(long)ReadDouble(...)` is not a substitute: a double carries
53 bits of mantissa, so a file size or a Unix timestamp past 2^53 would round on the way
through. These two fields are compared for EQUALITY by the integrity check, and a value that
rounds is a value that never matches.

### ProjectSerializer.ReadNullableDouble — PROJ_10 (line 479)

PROJ_10 — see `ReadDouble`. Same exact-type trap, same widening.

### ProjectSerializer.ReadInt — PROJ_10 (line 490)

PROJ_10 — see `ReadDouble`. Same exact-type trap, same widening.

### ProjectSerializer.ReadNullableInt — PROJ_10 (line 502)

PROJ_10 — see `ReadDouble`. Same exact-type trap, same widening.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Project/ProjectStore.cs

### ProjectIoResult — PROJ_08 (line 9)

PROJ_08 — the outcome of a save or a load, in a form the UI can show without guessing.

### ProjectStore — PROJ_08 (line 16)

PROJ_08 — READS AND WRITES `.fvsproj` FILES ON DISK.

⚠️ EVERY WRITE GOES THROUGH `AtomicJsonFile`. This is not a style preference; it is
`docs/05_SYSTEM_LIFECYCLE_STORAGE.md#SYS-ATOMICWRITE`, which states that atomic persistence
is "not optional, and it is not per-caller". A project file is the single artefact in this
product whose corruption costs the user work that cannot be regenerated, so the GUID-temp,
flush-to-platter, atomic-rename protocol matters here more than anywhere else it is already
used. Never replace this with `File.WriteAllText`.

⚠️ ONE GENERATION OF BACKUP IS KEPT. Before a successful overwrite the previous file becomes
`<name>.fvsproj.bak`. The crop configuration keeps a five-tier cascade
(SYS-RECOVERY) because it is machine state the user never sees and cannot re-author. A project
is different: the user knows what they saved, and a folder littered with five numbered backups
of every montage is its own kind of damage. One generation buys back the single mistake that
actually happens — saving over the wrong project — without turning their output folder into a
graveyard.

⚠️ THREADING. These calls perform synchronous disk I/O and MUST NOT be invoked on the Avalonia
dispatcher (North Star #6). Callers await them on a worker via `Task.Run` and marshal only
the resulting `ProjectIoResult` back to the UI thread.

### ProjectStore.Save — PROJ_08 (line 48)

PROJ_08 — writes the document to `path`.

Failure is returned, never thrown. A save that throws out of a window's close handler is how
an application loses the very work the user was trying to protect; the caller needs a value
it can render into "could not save, here is why, your edits are still open".

### ProjectStore.Load — PROJ_08 (line 95)

PROJ_08 — loads a document, falling back to the backup when the live file is unreadable.

The fallback is silent in the sense that it does not fail, but it is NOT silent to the user:
`loadedFromBackup` is set so the caller can say so. A user who is shown
yesterday's version without being told will keep editing and re-save over the good copy.

### ProjectStore.NormalizeExtension (line 173)

Guarantees the path ends in `ProjectDocument.FileExtension`. A file picker that
returns a bare name would otherwise produce a project the shell cannot associate and the
open dialog's filter will not show, which reads to the user as "my save vanished".

One entry in the recent list. `Exists` is resolved at read time, not stored.

### RecentProjects — PROJ_09 (line 13)

PROJ_09 — THE RECENT PROJECTS LIST.

Saving work is only half of the feature. A user who can save but must then hunt through Explorer
for the file has been given a filing chore, not a document model. The recent list is what makes
"carry on where I left off" a single click, and it is the surface the main window's empty state
should be built around.

⚠️ MISSING FILES ARE SHOWN, NOT SILENTLY DROPPED. A project on an external drive that is not
plugged in is not a project the user deleted. Entries carry `RecentProject.Exists`
so the UI can grey them out and explain, and `Prune` removes an entry only when the
user asks. Quietly deleting the row is how a user concludes the app "lost" their montage.

⚠️ Synchronous disk I/O — never call from the Avalonia dispatcher (North Star #6).

### RecentProjects.MaxEntries (line 34)

Ten is the number of projects a person can actually recognise in a menu. A longer list stops
being a shortcut and becomes a second file browser with worse sorting.

### RecentProjects.RecentProjects (line 48)

Test seam: point the list at an arbitrary file.

### RecentProjects.Touch (line 97)

Moves `path` to the top of the list, de-duplicating case-insensitively
(Windows paths differing only in case are the same file, and showing both is a bug the user

Removes one entry. Called when the USER dismisses a row, never automatically.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Project/RecoveryEnvelope.cs

### RecoveryEnvelope — RECOVERYDOC_01, PROJ_02 (line 6)

RECOVERYDOC_01 — THE CRASH-RECOVERY ENVELOPE: ONE `ProjectDocument` PLUS
CRASH-ONLY METADATA, AND NOTHING ELSE.

WHY THIS EXISTS — THERE MUST BE NO SECOND PROJECT SCHEMA. Until this type the recovery file was
written by a hand-rolled 60-field payload (`ProjectRecoveryService.SerializeState`) that
duplicated the project surface. Every new project feature therefore needed TWO serialisations:
the canonical `ProjectDocument`/`ProjectSerializer`, and a parallel
recovery copy that was forgotten the moment the next feature landed. A crash then "recovered"
the user's work while silently dropping everything the recovery schema never grew.

The fix is structural, not cosmetic: the recovery payload contains the CANONICAL document,
serialised by the CANONICAL `ProjectSerializer`. A field that can be saved can be
recovered, because they are the same field — by construction, not by discipline.

THIS TYPE MUST NEVER LIST PROJECT FIELDS. It carries exactly one embedded
`ProjectDocument` plus a `transient` object for crash-only state that
deliberately does NOT belong in a saved project. If you are tempted to add "baseSpeed" or
"cuts" or "mask" here, stop: they already live in the document inside `project`.

NO REFLECTION (PROJ_02). NativeAOT + TrimMode=full: this file touches nothing but
`JsonNode`/JsonObject, exactly like `ProjectSerializer`.

### RecoveryEnvelope.FormatId (line 35)

Magic string so a wrong-type recovery file is rejected by name, not by crash.

### RecoveryEnvelope.CurrentFormatVersion — RECOVERYDOC_02 (line 38)

RECOVERYDOC_02 — the envelope version this build WRITES. Bump only when the ENVELOPE shape
changes; the embedded document carries its own `ProjectDocument.SchemaVersion`
and is validated by `ProjectSerializer.Read` independently.

### RecoveryEnvelope.Write — PROJ_06 (line 51)

Builds the canonical recovery payload.

WHAT MAY GO INTO `transientMetadata`: ONLY state that must survive a
crash but must NOT be saved with a normal project, because it describes THIS machine or
THIS session rather than the user's work — the live preview volume, the thumbnail frame,
the freeze preview position, HUD tool selections mid-flight, voice-over takes metadata, the
in-flight granular editor session. Each of these is a pointer or a monitoring level, and
docs/06_PROJECT_DOCUMENT_MODEL.md (PROJ_06 / North Star #3) already rules them out of the
document. Anything the user BUILT goes in the document, not here.

### RecoveryEnvelope.IsEnvelope (line 82)

True when the root object is a canonical envelope written by this or a newer build.

### RecoveryEnvelope.TryRead (line 88)

Parses an envelope. Returns false and sets `error` when the payload is not
a recovery envelope this build can honour — including one written by a FUTURE build, whose
envelope shape this build may misinterpret (the same refusal rule
`ProjectSerializer.Read` applies to documents).
The document passes through every document-level protection unchanged:
schema minimum/maximum validation, unknown-field preservation, forgiving field reads.

### RecoveryEnvelope.HasContent — RECOVERYDOC_03 (line 138)

RECOVERYDOC_03 — "is there a project here a user would be upset to lose?", answered from the
CANONICAL document. An uploaded clip with no edits (a default document with no source) must
not produce a recovery file, a recovery prompt, or a restore — the same rule
HasUnsavedWork applies to the live session. Callers keep their richer live-state checks;
this is the document-level backstop so an empty capture can never be persisted.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Undo/GranularEditHistory.cs

### GranularEditorSnapshot — UNDO_01, UNDO_26, UNDO_02 (line 9)

UNDO_01 / UNDO_26 — one restorable state of the Granular Speed Editor.

Plain data only (rule U1 of UNDO_01): immutable arrays of value records and doubles. It never
references a control, a canvas, a bitmap, the IPC client or a pointer event, and it lives in
Core precisely so it CANNOT — Core does not reference Avalonia.

⚠️ `SelectedIndex` IS CARRIED BUT NOT COMPARED. It restores the side panel the user
was looking at (UNDO_02 rule 4) and is not an edit: selecting a segment must never be a
history step. Equality is the editor's long-standing `SameStateAs` with its tolerances.

### GranularEditorSnapshot.GetHashCode (line 54)

Consistent with the tolerant `Equals(GranularEditorSnapshot?)`: counts only.

### ParkedGranularHistory — UNDO_25, UNDO_26 (line 58)

UNDO_25 / UNDO_26 — a Granular history parked when its window closed, with the identity it
may ONLY be restored into.

**ClipKey:** Normalised full path of the clip the history was made on.
**Baseline:** The state that editor session OPENED on (the accepted project revision it was built from).
**Final:** The state the editor held when it closed.

### GranularEditHistory — UNDO_26 (line 72)

UNDO_26 — THE GRANULAR EDITOR ON THE SHARED `UndoStack{T}`.

NOT A SECOND HISTORY ENGINE. Every rule — U1 coalescing, U2 ceiling, U3 redo invalidation, U4
no-op rejection, the re-entrancy guard — is `UndoStack{T}`'s. This type only
translates the editor's calling convention onto it. The editor's ~40 call sites record the
state BEFORE a change (`PushUndo` at the top of the handler); `UndoStack{T}`
records the state AFTER it. Rewriting forty handlers to call back after every mutation is the
kind of edit that silently loses one, so the translation is done once, here:

  - `Checkpoint` takes the BEFORE state and remembers the action as
  PENDING. The pending action is committed — `UndoStack.Apply(after, label, key)` — the next
  time the live state is seen: the next checkpoint (whose before-state IS the previous action's
  after-state), a pointer release, an undo, a redo, or parking.
  - Because the commit compares before with after, U4 is now exact: an action
  that changed nothing records nothing, where the old stack recorded the before-state anyway and
  produced a Ctrl+Z that visibly did nothing.

⚠️ GESTURE FEEL IS PRESERVED, NOT RE-TUNED. The editor has always used a 700ms idle window
(`GestureIdleMs`); the shared default is 900ms. The stack is built with 700, and a
drag whose movement clamps (no state change) still refreshes the idle clock through
`UndoStack{T}.TouchGesture`, exactly as the old PushUndo refreshed it on a dropped
push. Neither changes any other editor.

⚠️ UI-thread only, like `UndoStack{T}`.

### GranularEditHistory.MaxDepth — UNDO_01 (line 105)

UNDO_01 (U2) — the editor's `MaxUndoDepth`. Equal to `UndoStack{T}.DefaultMaxDepth`.

### GranularEditHistory.GestureIdleMs — UNDO_02 (line 108)

UNDO_02 — the editor's long-standing `UndoGestureIdleMs`. Not the shared 900ms default.

### GranularEditHistory.CanUndo (line 114)

True when Ctrl+Z has something to take back (a committed entry or an action still pending).

### GranularEditHistory.CanRedo (line 117)

A pending action is an edit after the undo, so it hides the redo branch it is about to burn (U3).

### GranularEditHistory.Checkpoint — UNDO_02 (line 128)

Records that the action `label` is about to change the editor, which is
currently in `before`. `gestureKey` groups a continuous
gesture (UNDO_02); null for a discrete click.

### GranularEditHistory.EndGesture — UNDO_02 (line 163)

UNDO_02 — pointer released: commit what the gesture did and close it, so the next drag is its own step.

### GranularEditHistory.Undo (line 171)

Steps back one entry. Returns the state to restore and the action being undone.

### GranularEditHistory.Clear (line 190)

Drops every entry. The APPLY boundary and window teardown.

### GranularEditHistory.Park — UNDO_25 (line 197)

UNDO_25 — the history to keep when the window closes, or null when there is none.
`baseline` is the state this session opened on.

### GranularEditHistory.BelongsTo — UNDO_25, UNDO_26 (line 214)

UNDO_25 / UNDO_26 — whether a parked history may be replayed into an editor that opened on
`opening` for `clipKey`.

⚠️ PROVEN IDENTITY ONLY. The clip must match (another video's segment boundaries are
meaningless here), AND the editor must have opened on exactly the revision the history was
built from — its `ParkedGranularHistory.Baseline` (closed without APPLY, nothing
changed since) or its `ParkedGranularHistory.Final`. Anything else means the
accepted project state moved underneath the history — APPLY elsewhere, an edit in the main
window, a different project that uses the same file — and replaying it would walk the user
into a state that never existed on this timeline. Correctness beats deeper undo.

### GranularEditHistory.TryAdopt (line 235)

Replays `parked` if `BelongsTo` proves it may be. Returns whether it did.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Undo/UndoSidecarStore.cs

### UndoSidecarStore — UNDO_24 (line 13)

UNDO_24 — THE SIDECAR. EDIT HISTORY THAT SURVIVES CLOSING THE APPLICATION.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
**WHAT THIS CLOSES.** `07_UNDO_AND_HISTORY.md` §4 describes this store and §5 lists it
as item 1 of the work not yet done. Until now `UndoStack<T>` held everything in
memory, so quitting the application discarded every step of how a montage was built. A user who
closed the app, reopened their project and pressed Ctrl+Z got nothing — and got no explanation
either, because there was no history to say "there is none".

⚠️ **HISTORY IS NOT PART OF THE DOCUMENT, AND THIS IS NOT A STYLE CHOICE.** §4 is explicit:
history is per-machine, disposable, and would bloat a file meant to be portable. Sending a
colleague a montage must not send them forty snapshots of how it was made — and worse, a
`.fvsproj` carrying history would make the file grow without bound across sessions, on a
format whose whole appeal is that it is small enough to email. So it lives BESIDE the project,
keyed to it, and its absence is never an error.

**EVERY FAILURE HERE IS SILENT-BY-DESIGN AND THAT IS DELIBERATE, ONCE.** This is the one
place in the suite where a read failure genuinely leaves the user's outcome unchanged: they get
an empty history, which is exactly what they had before this class existed. Writing it through
`IFaultSink` as Degraded would produce a notice saying "your undo history could not be
loaded" for a feature the user has never been able to rely on, every time a sidecar is missing —
which is most of the time. The CALLER decides whether an empty result is worth mentioning;
this class reports the fact and does not editorialise.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

### UndoSidecarStore.SchemaVersion — UNDO_24 (line 47)

UNDO_24 — the format this build writes. Bumped when the meaning of a stored field changes.
A sidecar from a different schema is DISCARDED rather than migrated: it is disposable data
and a wrong migration would silently hand the user someone else's edits.

### UndoSidecarStore.MaxEntries — UNDO_24 (line 54)

UNDO_24 — the ceiling, mirroring U2. Enforced on WRITE as well as on restore, because a
file is the one place a stack can outlive the in-memory cap that was supposed to bound it.

### UndoSidecarStore.UndoSidecarStore (line 64)

**historyFolder:**
Where sidecars live. Under ProgramData, never beside the `.fvsproj`: the project may
sit on a network share, a USB stick or a read-only folder, and per-machine disposable data
has no business being written there.

### UndoSidecarStore.CreateDefault (line 74)

Builds a store rooted under the application's ProgramData directory.

UNDO_24 — the sidecar path for a project.

⚠️ Named by a HASH of the project's full path, not by its file name. Two projects called
`montage.fvsproj` in two folders are two projects, and sharing one history between them

UNDO_24 — writes the history beside the project.

`sourceFingerprint` is stored and checked on load. A project edited by

**Returns:** `true` when the file was written.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/Undo/UndoStack.cs

### UndoEntry (line 6)

One entry on the history: the state, and what the user did to leave it.
**T:** An IMMUTABLE state type. See the warning on `UndoStack{T}`.

### UndoStack — UNDO_10 (line 10)

UNDO_10 — ONE HISTORY FOR THE WHOLE APPLICATION, OVER ONE IMMUTABLE STATE.

WHY THIS TYPE EXISTS: undo used to live in exactly one window. The Granular Speed Editor had a
genuinely good implementation — coalescing, de-duplication, a ceiling enforced on push, redo
invalidation — and the Crop Tool, Music Wizard, meme placement, the merger and the main window
had NOTHING. Worse, the editor's forty states were discarded the moment its window was accepted
or closed (04_UI_UX_AVALONIA_SPEC.md §6 UI-GRANULAR), so noticing a mistake five seconds too
late meant it was permanent.

The four rules below are lifted DELIBERATELY from that editor's `PushUndo` rather than
reinvented. They were paid for in bug reports; this type generalises them to
`ProjectDocument` so every screen inherits the same behaviour instead of each growing its
own half-version.

  - **U1 COALESCE A GESTURE.** A drag raises hundreds of changes. Entries
  sharing a `gestureKey` within `GestureIdleMs` collapse into the ONE state
  from before the gesture began, so a single Ctrl+Z undoes the whole drag — not one pixel of
  it. The idle clock is refreshed even when the push is dropped, so the window tracks the LAST
  movement and a slow drag never splits in two.
  - **U2 CEILING ON PUSH.** The cap is applied as the entry goes on, so the
  list can never exceed `MaxDepth` even briefly. Trimming afterwards leaves a window
  where a snapshot of a large document is alive for no reason.
  - **U3 A NEW EDIT BURNS THE REDO BRANCH.** Editing after undoing makes
  every redo state unreachable history. Keeping them offers the user a "redo" that would
  silently discard the edit they just made.
  - **U4 NEVER PUSH A NO-OP.** If the new state equals the current one,
  nothing happened. Growing the stack anyway produces the worst undo bug there is: a Ctrl+Z that
  visibly does nothing, which users read as undo being broken and stop trusting.

⚠️ `T` MUST BE IMMUTABLE AND MUST HAVE VALUE EQUALITY. This holds a list of
states; if they are mutable, every entry is the same object and undo restores the present —
the defect the type exists to prevent. A C# `record` of value types and immutable
collections qualifies (`ProjectDocument` is exactly this). UI controls, bitmaps, streams
and IPC handles are forbidden, the rule UI-GRANULAR already states, generalised. U4 relies on
`object.Equals(object)` being a real state comparison — a reference-equality default
silently disables it.

⚠️ NOT THREAD-SAFE, BY DESIGN. History is UI-thread state. Locking it would invite exactly the
dispatcher-blocking this codebase forbids (North Star #6). Call it from the dispatcher only.

### UndoStack.DefaultMaxDepth — UNDO_11 (line 63)

UNDO_11 — matches the Granular editor's long-standing `MaxUndoDepth`. Forty is deep
enough that nobody reaches the end in a session, and shallow enough that forty document
snapshots cost nothing worth measuring.

### UndoStack.GestureIdleMs (line 70)

U1 — two pushes sharing a gesture key closer together than this are the same gesture. 900ms
is the editor's tested value: long enough to bridge a hesitation mid-drag, short enough that
a deliberate second action is never swallowed into the first.

### UndoStack.GestureIdle — UNDO_26 (line 93)

UNDO_26 — the U1 idle window THIS stack uses. Defaults to `GestureIdleMs`, so
every existing caller keeps 900ms. The Granular editor passes its own long-standing 700ms
(`UndoGestureIdleMs`) so moving it onto this type does not change how a drag feels.
Per instance, never global: one editor's tuning must not leak into another's.

### UndoStack.Current (line 101)

The live state. Only `Apply`, `Undo` and `Redo` move it.

### UndoStack.NextUndoLabel (line 108)

What Ctrl+Z would reverse, for the menu item and the tooltip — "Undo move zoom box", never a
bare "Undo". A user who cannot see what they are about to lose does not press the button.

### UndoStack.Changed (line 120)

Raised after any change to `Current`, `CanUndo` or `CanRedo`.

### UndoStack.Apply (line 123)

Records `label` as the way the user left the CURRENT state, then moves to
`next`.

⚠️ THE LABEL DESCRIBES THE ACTION BEING TAKEN, NOT THE STATE BEING STORED. "delete segment"
means pressing Ctrl+Z brings the segment back. This is the one thing that is easy to get
backwards and produces an undo menu that names the wrong operation.

`gestureKey` groups a continuous gesture (a drag, a slider sweep). Pass the
same key for every push in that gesture and `null` for a discrete action.

**Returns:** `true` if a new history entry was created.

### UndoStack.EndGesture (line 179)

Closes the current gesture so the next push starts a new history entry even if it carries the
same key. Call on pointer-release: without it, two drags of the same handle within the idle
window merge into one, and the user's second drag cannot be undone separately.

### UndoStack.TouchGesture — UNDO_26 (line 190)

UNDO_26 — U1's "the idle clock is refreshed even when the push is dropped", for a caller
whose push turned out to be a no-op (U4 returns before the gesture bookkeeping in
`Apply`, by design). Refreshes the clock ONLY if `gestureKey` is
the gesture already open: it can extend a live gesture, never open one, so it cannot make a
no-op swallow the user's next real edit.

### UndoStack.Undo (line 204)

Steps back one entry. Returns the restored state, or `null` if there is nothing to undo.

### UndoStack.Reset (line 254)

Replaces the state WITHOUT recording history, and clears both branches.

This is for loading a different project or starting a new one — moments where the old
history describes a document that is no longer open. It is NOT for ordinary edits: a
"cheap" reset to avoid a snapshot is how a user loses the ability to undo the step that
mattered.

### UndoStack.ReplaceCurrent — MERGEUNDO_01 (line 281)

MERGEUNDO_01 — swaps `Current` for an EQUIVALENT normalised form WITHOUT touching
either branch. For the moment right after a window applied an undo/redo target and re-read its
own state: sub-microsecond rounding must not become a phantom entry that burns the redo branch
(U3). Not for edits: a real change goes through `Apply`. Raises no event.

### UndoStack.UndoEntries (line 293)

Oldest-first history, for persistence and for a history panel. Excludes `Current`.

### UndoStack.Restore (line 298)

Rebuilds a stack from persisted entries (07 §4 UNDO-PERSIST). Entries beyond
`MaxDepth` are dropped from the OLDEST end, matching U2.

---

Added 2026-11-16 by `tools/FvsComments`.

## src/FreeVideoStudio.Core/FreeVideoStudio.Core.csproj

### near PropertyGroup — ISSUE_03 (line 27)

ISSUE_03: the Win32 version block a user sees in Properties > Details comes from the ENTRY
assembly (the App project), not from here. These are carried anyway so every managed
assembly in the product reports the same publisher and version - a mismatched Company on a
satellite assembly is exactly the kind of detail a security reviewer flags. Keep in step
with FreeVideoStudio.App.csproj on every release.
